The world was not made by a soul that fell, nor by reasoning, but by soul's contemplation of what is above it; it is the best possible image of the intelligible world, and the stars have souls better than ours. The Gnostics borrow from Plato and spoil what they borrow, multiply beings, despise the gods, claim to be the only sons of God, offer magic for cures, and teach no virtue. The good person lives here calmly, as in a fine house built by another.

@ch II.9.1 | Three principles, and no more
The Good is simple and first, the same as the One, with nothing above it. After it comes intellect, then soul: no fewer, since they differ, and no more. There is no intellect at rest beside one in motion, nor one that thinks beside one that thinks that it thinks, nor a rational principle between intellect and soul.

@p
Since we have seen that the nature of the Good is simple and first (for everything that is not first is not simple), and has nothing in itself, but is one thing, and that the nature of what is called the One is the same (for it is not something else and then one, nor this something else and then good), when we say "the One" and when we say "the Good" we must think it is the same nature, and call it one, not predicating anything of it, but making it plain to ourselves as far as we can. We call it first because it is the simplest; self-sufficient, because it is not made of many things, for then it would hang on the things it is made of; not in another, because everything in another also comes from another. If, then, it is not from another, nor in another, nor any kind of composite, there can be nothing above it. So we must not go to other principles, but put this first, then intellect after it, the first thinker, then soul after intellect. This is the order according to nature: to set neither more than these in the intelligible world, nor fewer. If fewer, they will say that soul and intellect are the same, or intellect and the first; but it has been shown in many places that they are different from one another.

@p
@greek λοιπὸν δὴ ἐπισκέψασθαι
It remains to look now at whether there are more than these three. What natures could there be besides them? No one could find a principle simpler than, or higher than, the principle of all things we have described. They will not say that there is one in potency and another in act: it is absurd, among things that are in act and without matter, to divide into potency and act and make more natures. Nor among the things after these: we must not imagine one intellect at a kind of rest and another as it were moving. What would the rest of intellect be, and what its motion and going forth? What would the idleness of one be, and what the work of the other? Intellect is as it is, always the same, set in a stable activity. Motion towards it and around it is already the work of soul; and a rational principle goes from it to soul and makes soul intelligent, without making some other nature between intellect and soul.
!flag For they [the Gnostics] will not say 1 that there is one principle in capacity, but another in energy | The Gnostics are not named in the Greek here; Taylor's square brackets say whom Plotinus means. "Capacity" and "energy" are Taylor's words for potency and act (dynamis, energeia).

@p
@greek οὐ μὴν οὐδὲ διὰ τοῦτο πλείους νοῦς
Nor should we make several intellects because one thinks and another thinks that it thinks. Even if, among us, thinking is one thing and thinking that one thinks is another, still it is a single grasp, not unaware of its own activities. It would be absurd to suppose otherwise of the true intellect: the one that thinks that it thinks will be the very same as the one that was thinking. Otherwise one will only think, and the other will think that it thinks, but that it is another who thinks, not itself. If they say the difference is only in our way of conceiving it, first they will give up their many realities. Then we must ask whether our conception even has room to take an intellect that only thinks and is not aware that it thinks. If that happened to us, who always watch over our impulses and our thoughts, we would be charged with folly, if we were even moderately serious people.
!flag if we are moderately worthy, it becomes the cause to us of folly | The Greek says that if this happened to us, who are at least moderately serious people, we would be charged with folly; Taylor's "it becomes the cause to us of folly" loses the charge.

@p
@greek ὅταν δὲ δὴ ὁ νοῦς ὁ ἀληθινὸς
But when the true intellect thinks itself in its thoughts, and the intelligible, what it thinks, is not outside it but it is itself what it thinks, then necessarily in thinking it has itself and sees itself; and seeing itself it sees itself not unthinking, but thinking. So in thinking first it would have thinking that it thinks too, as one thing; and there it is not double even in conception. And if it is always thinking, which it is, what room is there for a conception that separates thinking from thinking that one thinks? If someone brought in a third conception besides the second, the one that thinks that it thinks that it thinks, the absurdity is still plainer. And why not go on so to infinity? As for the one who makes a rational principle come from intellect, and then from it another in soul, so that this is between soul and intellect, he will deprive soul of thinking, if it gets its rational principle not from intellect but from some other between. It will have an image of a rational principle, not a rational principle; and it will not know intellect at all, nor think at all.

@ch II.9.2 | One intellect; and our soul in three parts
There is one intellect, always the same, imitating its father as far as it can. Part of our soul is always with the things there, part with the things here, and part between; the lower part can drag down the middle, but not the whole. The soul of the universe never fell: it orders the world without effort, by looking at what is above it.

@p
So we must set neither more than these, nor superfluous conceptions among them, which they do not admit, but one intellect, the same, always in the same state, unswerving everywhere, imitating its father as far as it can. And of our soul, one part is always with the things there, one part with the things here, and one in the middle of these. Being one nature in several powers, sometimes the whole of it is carried along with the best of itself and of being; sometimes its worse part is dragged down and drags the middle part with it; for it was not lawful for it to drag the whole down. This happens to it because it did not stay in the most beautiful, where the soul that stayed, which is not a part, and of which we are no longer a part, gave the whole body of the world to have from it as much as it can have. This soul stays untroubled, governing not by reasoning, nor setting anything right, but ordering the world with a wonderful power by its contemplation of what comes before it. The more it is turned to that, the more beautiful and powerful it is; and having this from there, it gives it to what comes after it, and as it lights, it is always lit.
!flag For the more it looks to itself | The Greek says "the more it is towards that", what comes before it (pros autēi, read by the editors as towards the intelligible); Taylor's "looks to itself" says the opposite.

@ch II.9.3 | Everything gives of itself: the world has no beginning
Lit always, soul always gives light to what follows, as a fire warms what is round it. Every principle must give of itself to another, or the Good would not be good, nor intellect intellect. So all things follow one another always: what is called "made" was always being made, and will not perish. Matter too is always lit.

@p
So it is always lit and has the light without break, and it gives it to what comes next; and these are always held together and watered by this light, and enjoy life as far as they can. It is as if a fire lay somewhere in the middle and warmed those who could be warmed. And yet fire is limited. But when powers that are not measured have not been removed from the things that are, how could they exist and nothing share in them? Each must give of its own to another as well, or the Good will not be good, or intellect not intellect, or soul not this, unless something lives secondarily after what lives first, as long as what is first exists. So all things must follow one another, and always; and the others are "made" in the sense that they come from others. So what are called made did not come to be at a time, but were always coming to be and will be; nor will they perish, except those things that have something to dissolve into. What has nothing to dissolve into will not perish either.
!flag and should be generated by other things, because they depend on others for their subsistence | The Greek says only that the others are "made" (genēta) in that they come from others; Taylor's "generated" suggests a beginning in time, which Plotinus here denies.

@p
@greek εἰ δέ τις εἰς ὕλην
If someone says things dissolve into matter, why not matter as well? If he says matter too dissolves, what necessity was there, we shall ask, for it to come to be? If they say it had to follow, it has to now as well. And if matter is to be left alone, the divine things will not be everywhere, but in some fenced-off place, walled off, as it were. If that is impossible, matter will be lit.

@ch II.9.4 | The world-soul did not fall
If they say the soul made the world when it lost its wings, the soul of the universe suffers no such thing. If it erred, when, and why then? It made the world not by sinking, but by not sinking; a forgetful soul could not make, and one that remembers would go back. Nor is the world badly made because it has much that is hard; it is the best image of the intelligible that there could be.

@p
If they say that the soul made the world when it had, as it were, shed its wings, the soul of the universe does not suffer this. If they say it erred, let them say what caused the error. And when did it err? If from eternity, by their own account it remains in error. If it began at a time, why not before? We say that the soul that makes did not sink, but rather did not sink. If it sank, it was plainly because it had forgotten the things there. And if it forgot them, how does it make the world? From what does it make, if not from what it saw there? But if it makes remembering them, it did not sink at all; for even if it has them dimly, it would not sink further, so as not to see them dimly. If it had any memory at all, why would it not want to go back up? What would it have reckoned it would gain by making a world? To be honoured, they say, which is absurd, a notion carried over from the makers of statues here.
!note The shedding of the soul's wings is from the Phaedrus (246c, 248c).
!flag For it does not possess nor see them obscurely, if it is without this tendency | The Greek says that even if the soul holds the things there dimly, it would not then sink further and see them more dimly still; Taylor's sentence says something else.

@p
@greek ἐπεὶ καὶ εἰ διανοίᾳ
Again, if it made by reasoning, and making was not in its nature, and its power was not a making power, how would it have made this world? And when will it destroy it? If it repented, what is it waiting for? If not yet, it would not repent now, being used to it and grown fonder of it with time. If it is waiting for the individual souls, they ought by now no longer to come into birth, having long ago had a taste of the evils here in an earlier birth; so they would have stopped coming by now. Nor must we grant that the world has been badly made because there are many unpleasant things in it. That would be to claim too great a dignity for it, if they think it should be the same as the intelligible world, and not an image of it. What other image of it, more beautiful, could there have been? What other fire would be a better image of the fire there than the fire here? What other earth than this, after the earth there? What sphere more exact or more orderly in its motion, after the encircling of the intelligible world in itself? What other sun after that one, before this sun we see?
!flag For this arises from forming too exalted an opinion of this sensible world | Taylor's words are close; the Greek says that those who find fault claim too high a worth for the world, asking it to be the intelligible world and not its image.

@ch II.9.5 | They rank themselves above the stars
It is unreasonable for people who have bodies and passions to claim to touch the intelligible, and deny that the sun's soul is better than theirs, or that the heavens share in immortality. Their soul made of the elements cannot live. They hate the world and dream of a "new earth" as its pattern; but where would this pattern come from?

@p
It is unreasonable that they, who have a body such as men have, and desires, pains and angers, should not despise the power that is in them, and yet say they may touch the intelligible; but that there is not, in the sun, a power more free from passion than this, more ordered and less subject to change, nor a wisdom better than ours, who were born only lately and are kept back by so many deceptions from coming to the truth. Nor is it reasonable to call their own soul immortal and divine, and that of the worst of men, but to say that the whole heaven and the stars there have no share in the immortal soul, though they are made of far finer and purer things. They see the order there, the good shape and the good arrangement, and they themselves above all find fault with the disorder here round the earth: as if the immortal soul had chosen the worse place on purpose, and left the better to the mortal soul.

@p
@greek ἄλογος δὲ καὶ ἡ παρεισαγωγὴ
Unreasonable too is their bringing in of this other soul, which they put together from the elements. How could a compound of the elements have any life? Their mixture makes hot or cold or a blend, or dry or wet or a mixture of these. And how is soul the bond holding the four together, if it came after them and from them? And when they add to it perception and deliberation and a thousand other things, what can one say? They do not honour this making of the world, nor this earth, but say that a new earth has been made for them, to which they will go from here; and that this is the rational principle of the world. And yet why should they need to come to be in the pattern of a world they hate? And where does this pattern come from? By their account it comes from the maker of the pattern when he had already sunk towards the things here. If, then, the maker himself took great care to make another world after the intelligible world that he has (and why should he?), he made it either before this world or after. If before, why? So that souls might be on their guard. Then how is it that they were not, so that it was made in vain? If after, taking it from the world, stripping the form from matter, then the experience of the souls who had tried the world was enough to put them on guard. And if they claim to take the form of the world into souls, what is new in the doctrine?
!note The "new earth" is the Gnostics' own, from Revelation 21:1; the "other soul" made of the elements is their lower soul.
!flag was it in order that souls might be saved | The Greek verb (phylaxōntai) means that souls might be on their guard, warned by the pattern; Taylor's "saved" gives it a Christian sense the Greek does not have.

@ch II.9.6 | What is new in them is not true
Their "sojourns", "antitypes" and "repentances" are empty words to found a sect. What is true in them they took from Plato; what is new is false. They misread the Timaeus and multiply intelligible beings. They should take from the ancients what is well said, argue fairly where they differ, and not abuse the Greeks to win a name.

@p
What should one say of the other realities they bring in: "sojourns", "antitypes" and "repentances"? If they say these are affections of the soul, when it repents, and "antitypes" when it contemplates images of the things that are, as it were, and not yet the things themselves, these are the words of people talking emptily to found a sect of their own. Because they do not take hold of the old Greek teaching, they contrive these things; while the Greeks knew clearly, and said without bombast, that there are ascents out of the cave, going on little by little to a truer and truer sight. In short, some of their ideas are taken from Plato; and the others, all the novelties they bring in to set up a philosophy of their own, have been found outside the truth. The judgments, the rivers in Hades and the passings into other bodies come from there.
!note The ascents out of the cave are those of the Republic (514a-517a); the judgments of the dead, the rivers of Hades and the passing of souls into other bodies are in the myths of the Gorgias, the Phaedo and the Republic.
!flag such as transmigrations, repercussions, and repentances | The Greek words are Gnostic terms: paroikēseis "sojourns" (in the world as in a foreign land), antitypoi "antitypes" (copies), metanoiai "repentances". "Transmigrations" and "repercussions" are Taylor's guesses.

@p
@greek καὶ ἐπὶ τῶν νοητῶν δὲ πλῆθος ποιῆσαι
And making a plurality in the intelligible world, Being and intellect and a different Maker and soul, is taken from what is said in the Timaeus. Plato said: "As intellect sees the Ideas present in the Living Being that truly is, how many and of what kind, so many the maker of the universe thought this world too should have." They did not understand, and took one intellect to be at rest, holding all beings in itself, another intellect besides it, contemplating them, and another that reasons; often, instead of the one that reasons, they have soul as the maker. And they think that this is the Maker according to Plato, falling far short of knowing who the Maker is. In general they falsify his account of how the world was made, and much else, and drag the man's views down to something worse, as if they had understood the intelligible nature, and he and the other blessed men had not. By naming a multitude of intelligibles they think they will seem to have found out the exact truth; but by the very multitude they bring the intelligible nature down into likeness with the sensible and lesser one. There one should aim at as small a number as possible, and give everything to what comes after the First, and be done with it: it is everything, the first intellect and substance, and all the other beautiful things after the first nature. The form of soul comes third.
!note The quotation is from the Timaeus (39e).
!flag understood by the discursive energy of reason | The Greek verb (dienoēthē) here means simply that the maker "thought" or "intended"; the Gnostics made it a third, reasoning intellect, and Taylor's "discursive energy" leans their way.

@p
@greek διαφορὰς δὲ ψυχῶν
One should trace the differences of souls in their affections or in their nature without disparaging the divine men in any way, but receiving what they say kindly, as from older teachers, and taking from them what they say well: the immortality of the soul, the intelligible world, the first god, that the soul must flee from its company with the body, the separation from it, the flight from becoming to being. These are laid down in Plato, and when they say them clearly in this way they do well. If they want to disagree on some things, no one grudges them that. But they should not set up their own views among their hearers by disparaging and abusing the Greeks. They should show that what seemed to them their own, against the views of the Greeks, is right on its own merits, setting out their views kindly and as philosophers. And where they disagree, they should do it fairly, looking to the truth, not hunting for a name by finding fault with men judged good long ago and not by poor judges, and saying that they are better than them.
!note The flight from the body and from becoming to being are from the Phaedo (64e-67d) and the Theaetetus (176a-b).

@p
@greek ἐπεὶ τά γε εἰρημένα
For what the ancients said about the intelligible world is far better, and said with learning; and those who are not taken in by the deceit now running over people will easily see it. And what these people took from them later has had some additions that do not fit at all. Where they want to disagree, they bring in comings to be and perishings that are total, they find fault with this universe, they blame the soul's fellowship with the body, they find fault with the one who governs this universe, they make the Maker the same as soul, and give him the same affections as the souls in parts.
!flag hence in their oppositions to them, they introduce manifold generations and corruptions | The Greek says the Gnostics bring in "total" (panteleis) comings to be and perishings, of the whole world; not "manifold".

@ch II.9.7 | The world-soul is not bound as we are
The world never began and will not end. Our soul's union with the body is not best for it; but to judge the soul of the universe by ours is like blaming a whole city for its potters. We are bound by the body; the world-soul binds and is not bound, and orders its body without effort. What perishes is like a tortoise caught in a great dance.

@p
That this world neither began nor will cease, but always is, as long as those things are, has been said. That our soul's fellowship with the body is not the better thing for the soul was said before them. But to judge the soul of the universe by ours is like taking the tribe of potters or smiths in a well-governed city and blaming the whole city. We must grasp the differences in the way the soul of the whole governs: it is not in the same manner, nor bound. Besides the other differences, a thousand of which have been told elsewhere, we should have kept this in mind: that we are bound by the body, once the bond has been made. For the nature of the body, already bound in the whole soul, binds together whatever it embraces. But the soul of the universe would not be bound by what it binds; for it rules. So it is free from affection by them, while we are not their masters. As much of it as is turned to the divine above remains unmixed and unhindered; as much of it as gives life to the body takes nothing from it in return.
!note "Said before them": the soul's fellowship with the body is not better for it, in the Phaedo (66b-67a) and elsewhere.
!flag That this world, therefore, never began, nor will ever cease to be | This is the start of the Greek's chapter 7; Taylor runs chapters 6, 7 and 8 together as one.

@p
@greek ὅλως γὰρ τὸ μὲν ἄλλου πάθημα
For in general what is in another necessarily receives that other's affection, but does not in turn give its own to the other, which has a life of its own. It is as if something were grafted on to another: when the stock suffers, the graft suffers with it; but if the graft withers, it leaves the stock to keep its own life. When the fire in you goes out, the whole fire does not go out; and even if the whole fire perished, the soul there would suffer nothing, only the structure of the body. And if there could be a world made of the other elements, the soul there would not care. For the world is not held together like each living thing. There, the soul runs over it, as it were, and bids it stay; here, the parts, as if trying to slip away into their own order, are bound by a second bond. There they have nowhere to flee to. So the soul need not hold them in from inside, nor press them in from outside; each stays where its nature wished from the beginning. If any of them moves according to its nature, the things for which this is not natural suffer, but they themselves move well, as parts of the whole. Some things perish, unable to bear the order of the whole: as if, when a great chorus moves in order, a tortoise caught in the middle of its path were trampled, unable to get out of the way of the dance. If it had placed itself in order with the dance, it would not have suffered from it either.
!flag but the latter being decayed, should permit the former to have its own life | The Greek says the graft, if it withers, leaves the stock its own life; Taylor reverses the two, making the stock decay.
!flag And if in each of the remaining elements there should be a certain world | The Greek says "if there could be a world made of the remaining elements"; not a world in each element.

@ch II.9.8 | The world is a clear image of the intelligible gods
To ask why soul made the world is to ask why soul exists: it assumes a beginning to what is eternal. The world shows the greatness of the intelligible, a clear and beautiful statue of the intelligible gods; it had to exist, since the intelligible acts both in itself and on another. The stars are gods with virtue; and if souls came here willingly, why blame the place?

@p
To ask why soul made a world is the same as to ask why soul exists and why the Maker made. First, this is the question of people who take the everlasting to have a beginning. Next, they think he became the cause of the making by turning from something to something and changing. So they must be taught, if they would bear it kindly, what the nature of these things is, so that they stop abusing what deserves honour, as they do so readily, where great reverence would be fitting. For one could not rightly find fault with the governing of the universe. First, it shows the greatness of the intelligible nature. If it has come into life in such a way that its life is not jumbled, like that of the smallest things in it, which its great life brings forth night and day, but is continuous, clear, great, and everywhere a life showing a wisdom beyond reckoning, how could one not call it a clear and beautiful statue of the intelligible gods? If, imitating, it is not that, this itself is natural: otherwise it would no longer be imitating. To say it imitates badly is false: nothing has been left out of what a beautiful image in nature could have.
!flag To enquire, however, why the world was made, is the same thing as to ask why soul is | This is the start of the Greek's chapter 8, in Taylor's long chapter 6.

@p
@greek ἀναγκαῖον μὲν γὰρ ἦν εἶναι
It was necessary for the imitation to exist, and not from reasoning and contrivance. The intelligible could not be the last thing: its activity had to be double, one in itself and one towards another. So there had to be something after it; only that which is the weakest of all has nothing further below it. A wonderful power runs there, and so it worked. If there were another world better than this, which is it? But if there must be one, and there is no other, this is the one that keeps the likeness of that world. The whole earth is full of living things of every kind, and immortal ones, and everything up to the heaven is full of them. And the stars, those in the lower spheres and those in the highest, why are they not gods, moving in order and going round in their good arrangement? Why should they not have virtue? What prevents them from gaining it? The things that make people here bad are not there, nor the badness of the body, troubled and troubling. Why should they not understand, always at leisure, and take into their intellect God and the other intelligible gods? Will our wisdom be better than that of those there? Who, if he had not gone out of his mind, would bear this?
!flag so that this power perpetually 1 fabricates | Taylor adds "perpetually" from Ficino's Latin, as his footnote says; Volkmann's Greek says only "and so it worked".
!flag If, however, there is another [sensible] world better than this, what is it | Volkmann's Greek has "not" here (οὐκ), which makes no sense in the question; Taylor, and the modern version, read it without the "not".

@p
@greek ἐπεὶ καὶ αἱ ψυχαὶ
And if souls came here forced by the soul of the universe, how are the forced better? Among souls, what prevails is stronger. If they came willingly, why do you blame the world you came into willingly, which lets you leave if you do not like it? And if this universe is such that one can have wisdom in it and, while here, live by the things there, how does this not bear witness that it hangs on the things there?
!note "Lets you leave": Plotinus takes it that the world allows those who are not content to depart; the Phaedo (61c-62c) forbids suicide, and so does Plotinus elsewhere (I.9).

@ch II.9.9 | Wealth, poverty and wrongdoing; the many gods
The good man does not seek equality in wealth or power. Wrongdoing comes from souls like children, not from intellect; and there are judgments and punishments here, and gods who watch. We should try to be as good as we can, but not think ourselves the only good ones: there are good daemons and gods, and the world-soul. God's greatness is shown in the many gods he makes. The Gnostics flatter fools: "you are a son of God".

@p
If someone finds fault with wealth and poverty and the lack of equality in all such things, first, he does not know that the good man does not look for equality in these, nor thinks that those who have much have more, nor rulers more than private people, but leaves this kind of eagerness to others. He has learned that life here is double, one life for the good, another for most people. For the good it leads to the summit, upward. For those who are more merely human it is double again: one kind remembers virtue and shares in some good; the other is the base crowd, like workmen making necessities for the better sort. If someone kills, or is beaten by pleasures through weakness, what is surprising in there being faults, not in intellect, but in souls like boys who have not grown up? And if it is a training ground of winners and losers, how is it not well so? If someone wrongs you, what harm is it to the immortal? And if he kills you, you have what you want. If you already find fault with being a citizen, you are not compelled to be one. It is agreed that there are judgments and punishments here. How, then, can one rightly find fault with a city that gives each his due, where virtue is honoured and vice has its fitting dishonour, and where not only statues of the gods are, but the gods themselves look on from above? They, as is said, will easily escape blame from men, leading all things in order from beginning to end, giving each his fitting portion, in the changes of lives, following what was done before. Whoever does not know this is one of the rasher men, boorish about divine things.
!flag And if you kill another [instead of being killed yourself,] you have what you wish | The Greek says "if he kills you, you have what you want": death frees the soul from the world it blames. Taylor makes the man who is wronged the killer.
!flag If, however, you still blame the administration of things, there is no necessity for you to continue any longer in life | The Greek says "if you find fault with being a citizen, you are not compelled to be one": not "to continue in life".

@p
@greek ἀλλὰ χρὴ ὡς ἄριστον
One should try to become as good as one can, but not think that one alone can become best (for then one is not yet best). One should think that there are other men who are best, and also good daemons, and still more gods: those who are in this world and look to that, and above all the leader of this universe, the most blessed soul. From here one should go on to sing of the intelligible gods, and above all of the great king of the things there, showing his greatness above all in the multitude of the gods. Not to shrink the divine into one, but to show it as many as he himself has shown it, this is to know the power of God: when, remaining who he is, he makes many, all hanging on him, being through him and from him. And this world is through him and looks there, the whole of it and each of the gods, and it tells men his will as a prophet and gives them oracles of what is dear to the gods. If they are not what he is, this itself is natural.
!note "The great king" recalls the Second Letter of Plato (312e), "around the king of all are all things".

@p
@greek εἰ δ’ ὑπερορᾶν θέλεις
If you want to look down on them, and think yourself grand, as not worse, first: the better a man is, the kinder he is to all, gods and men. Next, one should think oneself grand with measure, without boorishness, going up only as far as our nature can rise, and think that there is room beside God for others too, and not put oneself alone next to him, flying as in dreams, and so rob oneself of becoming a god, as far as a human soul can. It can as far as intellect leads; to go above intellect is already to fall outside it. Foolish men believe such talk when they suddenly hear: "You will be better than everyone, not only men but gods too." There is much self-will among men. A man once humble and modest and ordinary, if he hears "You are a son of God; the others you admired are not sons of God, nor are the things they honour, received from their fathers, divine; you are better even than the heaven, without any effort", and then others join in the chorus, it is as if, among many who could not count, a man who could not count heard that he was a thousand cubits tall. What would he do but think himself a thousand cubits tall, and, hearing that the others were five cubits, imagine only that a thousand was a big number?
!flag For there is much arrogance among men [of the present time.] | Taylor's words in square brackets are his own; the Greek speaks of men in general.

@p
@greek εἶτ̓ ἐπὶ τούτοις ὑμῶν προνοεῖ
Then again: God cares for you, they say; why does he neglect the whole world, in which they too are? If it is because he has no leisure to look at it, or it is not lawful for him, then when he looks down, at them, why does he not look outside and at the world in which they are? If he does not look outside, so as not to see the world, he does not see them either. "But they have no need of him." Yet the world needs him, and knows its order, and those in it know how they are in it and how there; and among men, those who are dear to God bear calmly what comes to them from the world, if anything must come from the movement of all things. One must look not to what pleases each, but to the whole. Such a man honours each as they deserve, and always hastens to where all things that can hasten. There are many that hasten there: those that reach it are blessed, and the others have, as far as they can, the portion that fits them. He does not give the power to himself alone. For a man does not have a thing because he claims to have it; many say they have it knowing that they do not, and many think they have it when they do not, and think that they alone have what they alone do not have.
!flag Divinity, however, knows the order of the wrorld | The Greek has an objection and an answer: "But they have no need of him." "Yet the world needs him, and knows its order." It is the world that knows its order, not God; Taylor makes it Divinity.

@ch II.9.10 | Why Plotinus writes; the fall of Wisdom
Much more could be examined, but Plotinus writes for his own pupils, out of regard for friends still held by these views, not to persuade the Gnostics. He ends with the most absurd thing: their story that soul and a certain "Wisdom" sank down and lit up the darkness, and that from images of images came the maker of this world.

@p
There is much else, or rather everything, that one could examine, and have plenty to say, showing how each point stands. But a certain reticence holds us back with regard to some of our friends, who came upon this teaching before they became our friends, and, I do not know how, still keep to it. Yet they themselves do not hesitate to say what they say, wanting it to seem convincingly true, or perhaps believing that it really is so. But we have said these things speaking to our own pupils, not to them (for there would be no gain in trying to persuade them), so that our pupils should not be troubled by people who bring no proofs (how could they?) but only self-will. There is another way in which one might write in defence against those who dare to disparage what the ancient and divine men said well and in keeping with the truth.
!note The "friends" are Gnostics who came to Plotinus' school in Rome; Porphyry names some of their books in his Life of Plotinus (ch. 16).
!flag We, indeed, are ashamed of certain of our friends,1 who before they were intimate with us were conversant with these opinions | The Greek word (aidōs) is a respectful reticence: Plotinus holds back out of regard for friends; he is not "ashamed" of them.
!flag and not to the many who are auditors of these men | The Greek says "speaking to our own pupils, not to them", the Gnostics; Taylor's "the many who are auditors of these men" is not there.

@p
@greek ἐκείνως μὲν οὖν ἐατέον ἐξετάζειν
So let us leave examining in that way; for those who have grasped exactly what has now been said will also know how all the rest stands. But let me say one thing before leaving the subject, which goes beyond everything in absurdity, if absurdity is the word. They say that soul sank down, and a certain Wisdom, whether soul began it or Wisdom was the cause of soul's being so, or they mean both are the same. They say the other souls came down together, the members of Wisdom, and put on bodies, human bodies for instance. But the one for whose sake they came down, they say, did not come down in turn, did not sink, as it were, but only lit up the darkness; and then an image came into being from there in matter. Then they fashion an image of the image, somewhere here, through matter or materiality, or whatever they like to call it, saying one thing and then another, and using many other names to make what they say obscure. So they beget what they call the Maker, make him revolt from his mother, and draw the world from him down to the last of images; so that the one who wrote this might abuse it thoroughly.
!note "Wisdom" (Sophia) and the Maker who revolts from his mother are figures of Gnostic myth, as in the Apocryphon of John.

@ch II.9.11 | The image that made the world
If the soul did not come down but only lit the darkness, how did it sink? Why did it alone shine, and not the higher powers? Why did it wait for images to be born? An image of soul would need no matter. Is the image a substance or a "thought"? Either way, their story of a maker made of images falls apart.

@p
First, then, if the soul did not come down, but lit up the darkness, how could it rightly be said to have sunk? If something flowed from it like light, it is not fitting to say at once that it has sunk; unless the darkness lay somewhere below, and the soul went to it in place, and when it came near, lit it up. But if it lit it while staying by itself, doing nothing towards it, why did it alone light it up, and not the beings more powerful than it? If it was able to light it because it formed a reasoned plan of a world, why did it not make the world at the same time as lighting it, instead of waiting for the images to be born? Then too the plan of the world, the "foreign earth" as they call it, made by the greater powers, as they say themselves, did not bring its makers down to sinking. Then, how does matter, when lit, make images of soul, and not the nature of bodies? An image of soul would need no darkness or matter: once it came to be, if it does, it would follow its maker and be joined to it.

@p
@greek ἔπειτα πότερον οὐσία τοῦτο
Next, is this image a substance, or, as they say, a "thought"? If a substance, what is its difference from what it comes from? If it is another kind of soul, if that one is rational, perhaps this one is the soul of growth and generation. But if so, how could it still make the world to be honoured, and how could it make through arrogance and daring? In short, making through imagination, and still more through reasoning, is done away with. And why did it need to make the maker out of matter and an image? If it is a "thought", first they must show where the name comes from. Then, how does it exist, unless they give the thought the power to make? And how is a fiction a making? They say this was made first and that after, but they speak as if they could say what they please. Why fire first?
!flag Again, whether is this illumination from a reasoning process, essence, or as they say, a conception | The Greek asks whether "this", the image, is a substance or, as they say, a "thought" (ennoēma); "this illumination from a reasoning process" is Taylor's.

@ch II.9.12 | How could an image remember, or plan?
Their image, just born, could not remember what it never saw; yet they, true souls, scarcely recollect. Nature does not make fire first and then the rest, but outlines the whole animal at once. Foreseeing the heavens and the zodiac is no work of an image. If the lighting of the darkness was natural, it was always; if not, evil was there before the world, and the cause goes back to the first.

@p
And how does it set to work as soon as it is born? By memory of what it saw? But it did not exist at all, so that it might have seen, neither it nor the mother they give it. Then is it not surprising that they, who came here not as images of souls but as true souls, scarcely and with difficulty, one or two of them, are moved out of the world, and coming to recollection scarcely call back what they once saw; while this image, even if dimly, as they say, but just after it was born, thought of those things, or its mother did, a material image; and not only thought of them and took the idea of a world from that world, but learned what it should be made of? Where did it get the idea of making fire first, thinking this must come first? Why not something else? If it could make fire by thinking of fire, why, thinking of a world (for it must have thought of the whole first), did it not make a world all at once? For those things too were contained in the thought.
!note "Scarcely recollect" recalls the Phaedrus (250a), where few souls keep enough memory of what they saw.

@p
@greek φυσικώτερον γὰρ πάντως
It would have made in a way more like nature, not as the arts do, for the arts come after nature and the world. Even now, in the things born singly from natures, it is not first fire, then each of the others, then a kneading of them together, but an outline and sketch of the whole animal, stamped on the menstrual matter. Why, then, was matter not outlined there too with a sketch of the world, a sketch in which were earth and fire and the rest? But perhaps they would have made a world like this, using a truer soul; he did not know how to make it so. And yet to foresee the size of the heaven, or rather that it should be so great, and the slant of the zodiac, and the course of the bodies below it, and the earth to be as it is, so as to be able to say the causes for which they are so, is not the work of an image: it came wholly from a power that came from the best things. They admit this themselves, against their will.
!flag and [the central position of] the earth | Taylor's words in square brackets are his own; the Greek says only "and the earth to be as it is".

@p
@greek ἡ γὰρ ἔλλαμψις ἡ εἰς
For the lighting of the darkness, if examined, will make them admit the true causes of the world. Why did it have to light it, unless it had to altogether? It was either by nature or against nature. If by nature, it was always so. If against nature, what is against nature will be in the things there, and evils will be before this world, and the world will not be the cause of evils, but the things there will be the cause for it; and evil will come to the soul not from here, but from soul to here. And the argument will carry the world back to the first things. And if so, matter too, from where it appeared. For the soul that sank, they say, saw and lit up the darkness that was already there. Where, then, did this come from? If they say the soul made it when it sank, there was plainly nowhere for it to sink to, nor was the darkness the cause of the sinking, but the nature of soul itself. And this is the same as necessities that went before; so the cause goes back to the first things.
!flag But if matter also is the cause of evil, whence does it appear that it is so | The Greek says that the argument will carry matter too back to the first things, "from where it appeared"; it does not ask whether matter is the cause of evil.

@ch II.9.13 | Do not fear the spheres
Whoever blames the world does not know the order of first, second and third. The spheres are not terrors, as the Gnostics' tragedies make them, but bring good things; their bodies are fine and their souls finer. What they do are signs of things to come. Not all can be good; what is less good is not therefore evil, or there would be evil in the intelligible world too.

@p
So whoever blames the nature of the world does not know what he is doing, nor where his boldness is taking him. This is because they do not know the order of things in succession, of first, second and third, and so on down to the last; and that one must not abuse what is worse than the first, but give way gently to the nature of all things, and oneself run towards the first, putting an end to the tragic tale of the terrors they think are in the spheres of the world, which, in fact, "make all things gentle" for them. What do these have that is fearful, to frighten people without experience of argument, who have never heard a cultivated and harmonious knowledge? Even if their bodies are of fire, one must not fear them, since they are in proportion to the universe and to the earth; one should look to their souls, by which these people surely think themselves worthy of honour too. And yet their bodies too, which excel in size and beauty, act with and work together with the things that come about by nature, which could never fail to come about as long as the first things exist; and they fill out the universe and are great parts of it. If men are something honourable beside the other animals, much more are these, which are in the universe not to be tyrants but to give it order and arrangement.
!flag by whom they imagine themselves to be considered as beings of a very honourable nature | The Greek says one should look at the souls of the stars, the souls by which the Gnostics themselves claim to be honourable; it is not that the stars think well of them.
!flag For otherwise subordinate beings would not be generated, as long as the first of things subsist | The Greek says the natural events the stars help to bring about "could never fail to happen as long as the first things exist"; Taylor's sentence says something else.

@p
@greek ἃ δὲ λέγεται γίνεσθαι
What are said to come from them should be thought of as signs of what will be. What happens happens differently by fortunes, for the same things could not happen to everyone, and by the times of birth, by places very far apart, and by the dispositions of souls. Again, one must not demand that all be good, nor, because that is not possible, be quick to find fault, claiming again that these things should not differ from those there. Nor should one think evil anything other than what is lacking in wisdom, a lesser good, always tending to the smaller. It is as if one called nature evil because it is not perception, and the perceiving part evil because it is not reason. Otherwise they will have to say there are evils there too; for there soul is worse than intellect, and intellect less than another.
!note That the stars signify rather than cause is argued in II.3, Whether the Stars Are Causes.

@ch II.9.14 | Magic, and diseases as daemons
They make the higher beings open to spells, as if words and hissings could charm what is incorporeal. They say diseases are daemons they can drive out; but diseases have bodily causes, and are cured by purging, bleeding and fasting. Our philosophy, by contrast, shows simplicity of character and pure thinking, with dignity, not arrogance.

@p
Above all, they themselves make the things there not pure in another way too. When they write incantations, as if addressing them, not only to the soul but also to what is above it, what are they doing but saying that these yield to spells and charms and persuasions, and obey a word and are led by it, if one of us is more skilled in saying such things, in such a way: chants and cries and aspirations and hissings of the voice, and the other things written in their books to work magic there? If they do not mean this, how are incorporeal things moved by sounds? So by the very means by which they make their words seem grander, they have unknowingly taken away the grandeur of those beings.
!note Porphyry, Life of Plotinus (ch. 10), tells how an Egyptian rival tried to work magic against Plotinus, and how it turned back on him.
!flag and utter them as to the stars | The Greek says the incantations are addressed to "them", the things there, the higher beings, not to the stars.
!flag but that sounds possess certain incorporeal powers | The Greek asks "how are incorporeal things moved by sounds?"; Taylor turns the question into a claim about sounds.

@p
@greek καθαίρεσθαι δὲ νόσων
When they say they purify themselves of diseases, if they said they did so by self-control and an orderly way of life, they would be right, as the philosophers say. But as it is, they suppose the diseases are daemons, and claim they can drive them out by a word, and advertise this. They may seem grander to the many, who admire the powers of magicians; but they would not persuade sensible people that diseases do not have their causes in strain, or excess, or lack, or decay, and in general in changes that begin from outside or inside. Their cures show it too. The bowels move, or a drug is given, and the illness passes downward and out; blood is let; and lack too has cured. Or was the daemon starved, and the drug made it waste away? Sometimes it left all at once, or stayed inside. If it still stays, how is one not still ill, with it inside? If it has left, why? What has happened to it? Was it fed by the disease? Then the disease was something other than the daemon. Again, if it comes in when there is no cause, why is one not always ill? If when a cause has arisen, why is the daemon needed for the illness? The cause is enough to make the fever. It is absurd that as soon as the cause arises, the daemon, ready and waiting, should at once take up its place beside it.

@p
@greek ἀλλὰ γάρ, ὅπως
But how and why they have said these things is clear; for this reason not least we have mentioned these daemons. The rest I leave to you to look into as you read, and to see this everywhere: that the kind of philosophy we follow, besides all its other goods, shows simplicity of character, together with a pure wisdom, and seeks what is dignified, not what is self-willed, with a boldness that goes with reason and much security, caution and great circumspection. Compare the rest with philosophy of this kind. What the others have is made up in every way opposite to it; it would not become us to say more about them.
!flag There are four lines more in this section in the original ; hut the meaning of them is so very obscure, that I have not attempted to translate them | Taylor's own footnote: he leaves the last sentences of the chapter untranslated. The modern version gives them: compare the rest with this philosophy; the Gnostics' is the opposite in every way.

@ch II.9.15 | They teach no virtue
Of the two schools of the end of life, pleasure and the beautiful, the Gnostics are worse than Epicurus: he removed providence, but they blame its lord, mock the laws and self-control, and leave only pleasure. They say nothing about virtue, what it is or how the soul is purified. "Look to God" helps nothing without being taught how; without true virtue, God is only a name.

@p
But this above all we must not miss: what these doctrines do to the souls of those who hear them and are persuaded to despise the world and what is in it. There are two schools about attaining the end of life: one sets the pleasure of the body as the end, the other chooses the beautiful and virtue, and for these the longing hangs on God and leads to God (how, must be seen elsewhere). Epicurus, doing away with providence, urges us to pursue pleasure and its enjoyment, which is what was left. But this doctrine, still more headstrong, finds fault with the lord of providence and with providence itself, scorns all the laws here and the virtue found out over all time, and laughs at self-control, so that nothing beautiful may be seen to exist here. So it has done away with self-control and with the justice that is inborn in characters and perfected by reason and training, and in general with everything by which a man might become good. So they are left with pleasure, with what concerns themselves, with what is not shared with other people, and with mere use; unless someone is better by his own nature than these doctrines. For none of these things is beautiful to them, but something else, which they will pursue some day.
!flag But the doctrine of the Gnostics, as still more juvenile than this | The Greek word (neanikōteron) here means "more headstrong", more reckless, not "more juvenile".

@p
@greek καίτοι ἐχρῆν τοὺς ἤδη ἐγνωκότας
And yet those who have already come to know should pursue it from here; and pursuing, they should first set these things right, as people who have come from a divine nature. For it belongs to that nature, which scorns the pleasure of the body, to understand the beautiful. Those who have no share in virtue would not be moved towards those things at all. This too bears witness against them: they have made no account of virtue, they have left out the subject altogether, and they say neither what it is, nor how many kinds there are, nor the many fine things seen about it in the writings of the ancients, nor from what it will come and be gained, nor how the soul is cared for and purified. For saying "Look to God" achieves nothing, unless one teaches how one is to look. What prevents a man, someone might ask, from looking, and abstaining from no pleasure, or being unable to control his anger, remembering the name "God" while held by all the passions and trying to rid himself of none of them? Virtue, going on to its end and coming to be in the soul with wisdom, shows God. Without true virtue, God spoken of is a name.
!flag though they ought to endeavour to correct those with whom they are well acquainted, applying themselves from a divine nature to human concerns | The Greek says that those "who have already come to know" should first set right their life here, as people who have come from a divine nature; Taylor's "those with whom they are well acquainted" and "to human concerns" are not there.

@ch II.9.16 | To despise the world is not to be good
Despising the world and its gods does not make anyone good; one who loves the father loves his children too. They say God cares for them alone, but if he is absent from the world he is absent from them. The universe is ordered and wise; no musician or geometer is unmoved by the image of the intelligible he sees here, and whoever is unmoved by the beauty of the stars has not seen the beauty there.

@p
Nor, again, does one become good by despising the world, and the gods in it, and the other beautiful things. Every bad man might despise the gods even before; and one who was not bad before would, by despising them, have become bad by this very thing, even if he were not bad in everything else. And the honour they say they pay to the intelligible gods would be an honour without fellow-feeling. For whoever has love for anything also welcomes all that is akin to what he loves, and the children of the father he loves. Every soul is a child of that father. And there are souls in these, in the stars, intelligent and good, joined to the things there far more than ours. How could this world be cut off from that one? How could the gods in it? But this was said before. Now we say that, despising what is akin to those things, they do not know those things either, except in words.
!flag and no one is completely bad till he does despise them | The Greek says that one who was not bad before would become bad by this very act of despising the gods, even if he were not bad in anything else; Taylor's words say something different.
!flag is utterly incongruous | The Greek word (asympathēs) means "without fellow-feeling": an honour to the intelligible gods that does not feel for what is akin to them.

@p
@greek ἐπεὶ καὶ τὸ πρόνοιαν μὴ διικνεῖσθαι
And how is it pious to say that providence does not reach to things here, or to anything at all? And how is it consistent with what they say themselves? For they say it cares for them alone. Do they mean when they were there, or also while they are here? If there, how did they come? If here, how are they still here? And how is he himself not here? From where will he know that they are here? And how, that while here they have not forgotten him and become bad? If he knows those who have not become bad, he knows those who have too, so as to tell the one from the other. So he will be present to all and be in this world, in whatever way; so the world will share in him. If he is absent from the world, he will be absent from you too, and you would have nothing to say about him or about what comes after him. But whether some providence comes to you from there, or whatever you like, the world has it from there, and has not been left, and will not be. Providence is far more for the wholes than for the parts, and the sharing of that soul far more. The world's being shows it, and its being wise. For which of those who look down on it so foolishly is so ordered, or so wise, as the universe? To compare them is absurd, and full of strangeness; and anyone who compares them except for the sake of the argument would not be free of impiety.
!flag For xvho among those that are stupidly proud , is so orderly and wise as the universe | The Greek plays on words: "which of those who are so foolishly over-wise (hyperphronountes aphronōs) is as ordered and wise (emphrōn) as the universe?"

@p
@greek καὶ οὐδὲ τὸ ζητεῖν περὶ τούτων
Even to ask about these things is not the mark of a wise man, but of someone blind, with neither perception nor intellect, and far from seeing the intelligible world, since he does not see this one. For what musician would there be who, having seen the harmony in the intelligible, would not be moved when he hears the harmony in sounds we perceive? Who, skilled in geometry and numbers, would not be pleased when he sees with his eyes what is proportionate, in ratio and ordered? Even in paintings, those who see the works of art with their eyes do not see the same things in a like way; when they recognise in what they perceive an imitation of what lies in thought, they are as it were stirred, and come to a recollection of the truth. It is from this experience that loves are moved. Someone who sees beauty well imitated in a face is carried to that other. Will anyone be so sluggish in mind, and moved by nothing else, that seeing all the beauties in the world we perceive, all its proportion, and this great good order, and the form that shows itself in the stars, far off as they are, he will not think of it, and be seized with awe, at what they are and from what? Then he has neither understood these things nor seen those.
!note The beauty of a face that recalls true beauty is from the Phaedrus (251a).
!flag But the geometrician and arithmetician, knowing in the sensible object the imitation of that w hich subsists in intellection | The Greek speaks here of those who look at paintings; recognising in the picture an imitation of what is in thought, they are stirred to recollection. Taylor gives this to the geometer and the arithmetician.

@ch II.9.17 | The beauty of the world, inside and out
If they hate the body because Plato blamed it, they should take the body away in thought and see the intelligible sphere and the souls that give it size. The world-soul gives body all the beauty it can take, without jealousy. Despise the beauty of boys and women if you must, but not the beauty of the whole: the universe was never a child, and nothing bad can be added to it.

@p
And yet, even if it occurred to them to hate the nature of the body, because they have heard Plato find much fault with the body for the hindrances it gives the soul, and call all bodily nature worse, they should have taken this away in thought and seen what remains: an intelligible sphere that holds the form that is on the world; souls in order, giving size without bodies, and leading the intelligible out into extension, so that what came to be might, by its size, as far as it could, be made equal to the partless pattern. For what is great there in power is great here in bulk. And whether they wanted to think of this sphere as moving, carried round by the power of a god who holds the beginning, middle and end of all of it, or as standing still, not yet governing anything else, it would be well, for a conception of the soul that governs this universe.
!note "Beginning, middle and end" is from the Laws (715e-716a), said of God.
!flag souls in an orderly series without bodies, imparting magnitude according to the intelligible | The Greek says the souls give size "without bodies", and lead the intelligible into extension; "according to the intelligible" is not there.

@p
@greek ἐνθέντας δὲ ἤδη καὶ τὸ σῶμα
Then, putting the body into it too, as it would suffer nothing, but give to another to have whatever each can receive, since jealousy is not lawful among the gods, they should think in this way about the world, granting to the soul of the world so much power that it has made the nature of body, which is not beautiful, share in beauty as far as it could be made beautiful. And this beauty itself moves souls that are divine. Unless they say that they themselves are not moved by it, and see no difference between ugly and beautiful bodies. But then they see none between ugly and beautiful ways of life, nor fine studies, nor, then, contemplations, nor, then, God. For these are because of the first things. If, then, not these, not those either; so these are beautiful after those.
!note "Jealousy is not lawful among the gods" is from the Phaedrus (247a); compare the Timaeus (29e).

@p
@greek ἀλλ’ ὅταν λέγωσι
But when they say they despise beauty here, they would do well to despise the beauty in boys and women, so as not to be overcome by licentiousness. Yet they should know that they would not be proud if they despised what is ugly; they are proud because they despise what they first called beautiful, being in a bad state. Next, that beauty is not the same in a part and in the whole, in each and in all. Then, that there are such beauties even in things we perceive and in partial beings, those of daemons for instance, that one admires the maker and believes they come from there; and from this one can say that the beauty there is beyond reckoning, not clinging to these, but going from these to those, and not abusing these. If their inner selves are beautiful too, one should say the inner and outer agree; if the inner are bad, that they have fallen short of the better.
!flag but if they despise what they before had acknowledged to be beautiful, and by which they were in a certain respect affected | The Greek says they are proud of despising what they first called beautiful, "being in a bad state" (kakōs diatethentes); Taylor's "by which they were in a certain respect affected" softens it.

@p
@greek μήποτε δὲ οὐδὲ ἔστιν
Perhaps there is not even anything truly beautiful outside that is ugly inside; for everything beautiful outside is so because the inner has prevailed. Those who are called beautiful but are ugly inside have a false outer beauty too. If someone says he has seen people truly beautiful but ugly inside, I think he has not seen them, but thinks others are the beautiful ones; or if he has, their ugliness was acquired, since they were beautiful by nature, for here there are many hindrances to coming to fulfilment. But what hindrance was there for the universe, being beautiful, to be beautiful inside too? Again, those to whom nature did not give the complete from the beginning might perhaps not come to fulfilment, so that they can even become bad. But the universe was never a child, as being incomplete, nor did anything come to it as it went on, nor was anything added to its body. From where would it come? It had everything. Nor could one invent an addition to its soul. Even if one granted them this, it would not be anything bad.

@ch II.9.18 | Two men in one fine house
They say their teaching makes people flee the body, and ours keeps the soul in it. It is like two men in a fine house: one finds fault with it and stays; the other praises the builder and waits calmly for the time to leave. We should live in our bodies as the world-soul lives in the whole, untroubled; and not grudge the stars their contemplation, as though they did not see because we do not see their souls.

@p
But perhaps they will say that their doctrines make people flee from the body, hating it from afar, and that ours hold the soul down to it. This would be like two people living in the same fine house. One finds fault with how it is built, and with its builder, and stays in it none the less. The other does not find fault, but says the builder made it with the greatest skill, and waits for the time to come when he will leave it, when he will no longer need a house. The first would think himself wiser and readier to leave, because he knows how to say that the walls are made of lifeless stones and timber and fall far short of a true dwelling. He does not know that he differs only in not bearing what is necessary, if indeed the other does not resent these things but quietly loves the beauty of the stones. We should stay in the houses, while we have bodies, that were built by a good sister soul, which has great power to make without toil.
!flag The former of these, however, is thought to be the wiser of the two | The Greek says that the one who finds fault "would think himself" wiser and readier to leave; Taylor's "is thought to be" makes it others' opinion.

@p
@greek ἢ ἀδελφοὺς μὲν καὶ τοὺς φαυλοτάτους
Or do they think it right to call even the worst of men brothers, but scorn to call the sun and the beings in the heaven brothers, or the soul of the world, with their raving mouths? It is not lawful to join ourselves to them in kinship while we are bad; but when we have become good, and are not bodies but souls in bodies, able to live in them in a way as close as possible to the way the soul of the universe lives in the whole body. This is not to be struck, nor to give way to the pleasures and sights that fall on us from outside, nor to be troubled if something is hard. That soul is not struck, for there is nothing to strike it. We, being here, would by virtue push the blows away, making some smaller by greatness of mind, others not even blows, by our strength. Coming near to what cannot be struck, we would imitate the soul of the universe and of the stars; and coming close to likeness, we would hasten towards the same goal, and the same things would be ours to contemplate, since we too would be well prepared by nature and by care; but they have it from the beginning.
!flag we shall hasten to be one and the same with them | The Greek says we would hasten "towards the same" goal as the world-soul and the stars, and the same things would be ours to see; not that we would become one with them.

@p
@greek οὐδέ, εἰ μόνοι λέγοιεν
Nor, if they say they alone can contemplate, would they contemplate more; nor because they say that they can go out when they die, and the others cannot, who always adorn the heaven. They would say this from ignorance of what "being outside" is, and of the way the whole soul cares for all that is soulless. So it is possible not to love the body, to become pure, to despise death, to know the better things and pursue them, and not to grudge others who can pursue them and always do, as if they did not; and not to suffer the same as those who think the stars do not move, because their perception tells them that they stand still. For this is why they do not think the nature of the stars sees what is outside: because they do not see that their soul is outside.
!note "The whole soul cares for all that is soulless" is from the Phaedrus (246b).

@treatise III.6 | On the impassibility of things without body
How can the soul, which has no body, be free from affection, when it seems to feel pleasure and pain, fear and desire, and to change with virtue and vice? Plotinus answers that perception, memory and virtue are activities, not imprints; the feelings belong to the body or to the living compound, with soul as their cause; and purification is the soul's waking from images. Then matter: it too is untouched. It is not being but a phantom, a mirror in which images come and go and leave no trace, "the receptacle and nurse" of becoming, which takes on nothing and keeps nothing.

@ch III.6.1 | Can the soul be affected?
Perceptions are not affections but activities and judgments about them. But how is the soul unchanged, when vice, false opinions, pleasures, pains and desires seem to arise in it? If soul has no size and is to be imperishable, we must not give it such affections; we must think of them as "affections without affection", taken over from bodies by analogy.

@p
We say that perceptions are not affections, but activities and judgments about affections. The affections happen in something else, in the body, say, of a certain kind; the judgment happens in the soul, and it is not an affection, for then another judgment would be needed, and we would go up and up without end. Even so we would still have a difficulty here: whether judgment, as judgment, has nothing of what it judges. If it had an imprint, it has been affected. We might also speak of the so-called imprints, and show that the way they come about is quite different from what is supposed, the same as with thoughts, which are activities too, able to know without being affected. In short, our argument and our aim is not to subject the soul to changes and alterations of the kind that bodies undergo when they are heated or chilled. We ought also to look at what is called its affective part, and ask whether we shall grant that this too is unchanging, or allow it alone to be affected. But that comes later; first we must look into the difficulties about the earlier ones.

@p
@greek πῶς γὰρ ἄτρεπτον
How is what comes before the affective part, and before perception, and in general any part of soul, unchanging, when vice comes to be in it, and false opinions, and ignorance? When it feels at home with things and estranged from them, takes pleasure and pain, is angry, envies, is jealous, desires, and in short is never at rest, but is moved and changed by each thing that comes upon it? If the soul is a body and has size, it is not easy, or rather it is wholly impossible, to show it free from affection and unchanging in any of the things said to happen to it. But if it is a substance without size, and imperishability must belong to it, we must take care not to give it such affections, lest we find we have made it perishable without noticing. And if its substance is a number, or a rational principle, as we say, how could an affection come to be in a number or a rational principle? We must rather think that what come upon it are "unreasoning reasons" and "affections without affection"; these words, carried over from bodies, must each be taken in the opposite sense, and by analogy: it has them without having them, and is affected without being affected. And we must look at how such things happen.
!flag so that the soul [after a manner] possessing these, does not [really] possess them | Taylor's words in square brackets are his own; the Greek says only that the soul has them without having them.

@ch III.6.2 | Virtue and vice are not alterations
Virtue is a harmony of the soul's parts, vice a discord; but each part has its own virtue first. Ignorance is an absence; false opinion is like a false sight. Reason "hears" intellect as sight sees: the activity is no alteration. Memory too is a power woken, not a store of imprints. Cowardice, courage and licence are ways each part acts or fails to act, with no change in its substance.

@p
First we must speak of vice and virtue, and say what happens when vice is said to be present. For we say we must take away something, as if there were some evil in the soul, and put in virtue, and give it order, and make beauty in it in place of its former ugliness. If we say that virtue is a harmony and vice a lack of harmony, we would be saying what the ancients thought, and the argument would make no small gain towards what we are looking for. If virtue is the parts of the soul attuned to one another according to nature, and vice their not being attuned, nothing would come in from outside, but each part would come into the harmony as it is, and would not come into it in the discord while being of that kind. So it is with dancers who dance and sing together, even if they are not the same; and when one sings alone and the others do not, and when each sings by himself. For they must not only sing together, but each must sing his own part well, with a musical skill of his own. So in the soul too there is a harmony when each part does what belongs to it. But before this harmony there must be another virtue of each part, and a vice of each before their discord with one another.
!note Virtue as a harmony of the parts of the soul is the view of the Republic (443c-e); the harmony theory of the soul itself is rejected in the Phaedo (85e-86d, 92a-95a).
!flag like dancers who in dancing do not accord with each other | The Greek compares the soul to dancers who dance and sing together, even though they are different people; the "not" belongs to the discord, not to the dancers.

@p
@greek τίνος οὖν παρόντος
By the presence of what, then, is each part bad? Of vice. And good? Of virtue. In the reasoning part one might perhaps say that vice is ignorance, and ignorance as an absence, not the presence of something. But when false opinions are in it, which most of all make vice, how will he say that something does not come to be in it, and that this part does not become different? And is the spirited part not in one state when cowardly, and another when brave? And the desiring part, one when licentious, another when self-controlled? We shall say that when each is in virtue, it acts according to the substance it is, listening to reason: the reasoning part listening to intellect, the others to the reasoning part. To listen to reason is like seeing: the part is not shaped, but sees, and is in act when it sees. For sight is the same in substance whether it is in potency or in act; its act is not an alteration, but at once it comes to what it has its substance for, and it knows, and has known, without being affected. So the reasoning part stands towards intellect, and sees; and this is the power of thinking, not a seal stamped inside it. It has what it has seen, and again does not have it: it has it by knowing, it does not have it in that nothing from the sight is laid up in it, like a shape in wax.
!flag And is not the epithymetic 1 part likewise | Taylor keeps the Greek word (epithymētikon): the part of the soul that desires.

@p
@greek μεμνῆσθαι δὲ δεῖ
We must remember that memories too were said not to be things laid up in the soul, but the soul's waking its power so as to have even what it does not have. What, then? Was it not different before it remembered, and later, when it remembers? Do you want to call it different? Not altered, at least; unless one calls going from potency to act alteration. Nothing has been added: it does what it was by nature fitted to do. In general, the activities of things without matter come about without their being altered, or they would perish; rather, they remain. Being affected while acting belongs to things with matter. If what is without matter is affected, it has nothing by which to remain. So in seeing, when the sight is active, what is affected is the eye; and opinions are like sights.
!note Memory as a power woken, not an imprint stored, is argued in IV.6, On Perception and Memory.

@p
@greek τὸ δὲ θυμοειδὲς πῶς δειλόν
How is the spirited part cowardly, and how brave? Cowardly, by not looking to reason, or by looking to reason that is bad, or by a lack of instruments, such as a want or weakness of its bodily weapons, or by being hindered in acting, or by not being moved, but only provoked, as it were. Brave, if the opposite. In none of these is there alteration or affection. The desiring part, when it acts alone, brings about what is called licentiousness. It does everything alone, and the others are not present, whose part it would be to rule when present and to show it the way; the part that sees would be doing something else, not wholly, but partly at leisure from seeing the others as far as it can. Perhaps for the most part what is called the vice of this part is a bad condition of the body, and its virtue the opposite; so that in neither case is anything added to the soul.

@ch III.6.3 | Feelings belong to the body; their source is in soul
We cannot deny that pleasure, pain, fear and desire are real changes; but we must ask what changes. To say the soul blushes or turns pale would be absurd: the shame or fear begins in soul, the blood changes in the body. Soul's movements come from it, as life does, without alteration; memories and images are not stamped in it like wax.

@p
How about feeling at home and estranged? And how are pains, angers and pleasures, desires and fears not changes and affections present and moving in the soul? About these we must distinguish as follows. To say that alterations and strong perceptions of them do not happen would be to contradict what is plain. But granting them, we must ask what it is that changes. For we are in danger of supposing, when we say these things of the soul, something like saying the soul blushes, or turns pale, not reckoning that these affections come through soul but happen in the rest of the composite. Shame is in the soul when an opinion of something shameful arises in it; the body, which the soul as it were holds (not to be misled by words), being under the soul and not like something soulless, was changed in the blood, which is easily moved. As for what is called fear, its source is in the soul; the pallor is the blood withdrawing inward. Of pleasure, the spreading out comes to perception in the body; what is in the soul is no longer an affection. So with pain.
!flag the body, (that we may not err in our conceptions) being as it were contained in the soul | Taylor's words are near the Greek; the warning "not to be misled by words" belongs to the word "holds", that the soul "holds" the body.

@p
@greek ἐπεὶ καὶ τὸ τῆς ἐπιθυμίας
Again, desire: its source is in the soul and is hidden; what goes out from there, perception knows. For when we say the soul is moved in desires, reasonings and opinions, we do not mean that it does these things while being shaken, but that the movements come from it.
