Nor the interval of motion: motions of the same kind differ in speed, and are measured by something else; the interval in a motion is only its quantity, and that is in time; and rest has a duration too.

@p
Time cannot be motion, whether one takes all motions together and makes them one, as it were, or takes the ordered motion; for each motion so called is in time. If someone says there is a motion not in time, it would be much further from being time, since what motion is in is one thing and motion itself another. Of all that could be and has been said, this one thing would be enough: motion can stop and break off, but time cannot. If someone says the motion of the universe does not break off, still this too, if he means its circuit, would go round to the same point in some time, not the time in which half is done; and the one time would be half, the other double, though both are motions of the universe, the one from the same point to the same, the other reaching only half. And saying that the motion of the outermost sphere is the quickest and fastest bears witness to the argument, that its motion and time are different. For it is plainly the fastest of all by covering the greater, the greatest, distance in less time; the others are slower because they cover it, or a part of it, in more. If, then, the motion of the sphere is not time, still less would the sphere itself, which was supposed to be time because it moves.

@p
@greek ἆῤ οὖν κινήσεώς τι
Is it, then, something of motion? If interval, first, it is not the same for every motion, not even for motion of the same kind; for motion, even in place, is faster and slower. Both intervals would be measured by one other thing, which one would more rightly call time. And time would be the interval of which of them, or rather of which of all of them, since they are without number? If of ordered motion, not of all of it, nor of all of that kind, for there are many; so there will be many times at once. If it is the interval of the motion of the universe: if it is the interval in the motion itself, what would it be but the motion, of so much? This so much will be measured either by place, because the motion has gone through so much, and this will be the interval; but this is not time, but place. Or the motion itself, by its continuity and by not stopping at once but always going on, will have the interval. But this would be the muchness of motion. If someone looking at it declares it much, as one might say the heat is much, here too time will not appear or come into view, but motion again and again, like water flowing again and again, and the interval seen on it. The "again and again" will be number, like two or three; the interval will belong to bulk.

@p
@greek οὕτως οὖν καὶ πλῆθος κινήσεως
So too the muchness of motion, like ten, or like the interval that appears on the bulk of motion, as it were, has no concept of time in it; this so much will have come to be in time, or time will not be everywhere but in motion as in a substrate, and we come round again to saying that time is motion. For the interval is not outside it; it is motion not all at once. And what is not all at once, if what is all at once is in time, how will it differ from what is all at once, except by being in time in another way? So spread-out motion and its interval are not time itself, but in time. If someone says time is the interval of motion, not that of the motion itself, but that along which the motion has its extension, as if running along beside it, he has not said what this is. For it is plain that this is time, in which the motion has taken place. And this was what the argument was looking for from the beginning: what time is. It is the same as if someone, asked what time is, said: the interval of motion in time. What, then, is this interval, which he calls time, setting it outside the motion's own interval? And again, whoever sets the interval in the motion itself will be at a loss where to set the duration of rest. For as long as one thing is moving, another may be standing still; and you would say the time of each is the same, as being, plainly, other than both. What, then, is this interval, and what nature has it? It cannot be spatial, since that too is outside.
!flag And you may say that the time of each is the same, though its relation to the one, is different from its relation to the other | The Greek says the time of the moving and the resting thing is the same, "as being other than both"; Taylor's "its relation to the one is different" is not there.

@ch III.7.9 | Time is not the number or measure of motion
If time is the number or measure of motion, of which motion? The same number for orderly and disorderly, fast and slow, would be like ten measuring horses and oxen: it says what time measures, not what it is. A measure running along with motion, or a number "according to before and after", already presupposes time; and time, being without limit, would exist before it was measured, even before a soul measured it.

@p
How time is the number or measure of motion (measure is better, since motion is continuous) must be considered. First, here too there is the difficulty, as with the interval of motion, if someone says it is of all motion. How could one number disorderly and irregular motion, or what number or measure would there be of it, or by what would the measure go? If by the same thing for both, and in general for all, fast and slow, the number and measure will be such as if ten were measuring both horses and oxen, or the same measure were for wet things and for dry. If it is a measure of this sort, it has been said of what things time is, of motions, but not yet what it is itself. If, as ten can be thought as number even taken without horses, and a measure is a measure having a nature of its own even if it is not yet measuring, so time too, being a measure, must be: if it is in itself something like number, how would it differ from this number of ten, or from any other number of units? If it is a continuous measure, being a measure of some quantity, it will be like the cubit. It will be a magnitude, then, like a line running along with the motion. But how will this, running along, measure what it runs along with? Why should either measure the other rather than be measured? It is better and more convincing to suppose it is not of all motion but of the motion it runs with. This must be continuous, as long as the one running with it holds on.
!flag it may be doubted* whether it is similarly the numher or measure of every motion | The scan's "numher" is "number". Plotinus is reviewing Aristotle's definition, "the number of motion according to before and after" (Physics IV.11, 219b).

@p
@greek ἀλλ’ οὐκ ἔξωθεν δεῖ τὸ μετροῦν
But one should not take the measure from outside, or apart, but motion measured together. And what will be the measure? The motion will be measured, and the measuring thing will be a magnitude. And which of them will be time, the measured motion or the measuring magnitude? Time will be either the motion measured by the magnitude, or the magnitude that measured, or what uses the magnitude, as one uses a cubit, to measure how much the motion is. In all these cases it is more convincing, as we said, to suppose the motion regular; for without regularity, and further without one motion, that of the whole, the account is harder for whoever puts forward any kind of measure. If time is measured motion, measured by quantity, then, just as the motion, if it had to be measured, had to be measured not by itself but by something else, so, if the motion is to have a measure other than itself, and for this we needed the continuous measure, the magnitude itself will in the same way need a measure, so that the motion may be measured as so much as the thing it is measured by. So time will be the number of the magnitude that goes along with the motion, not the magnitude that runs along with it.

@p
@greek οὗτος δὲ τίς ἂν εἴη
What would this number be but one made of units? And how it will measure is a hard question. For even if someone finds out how, he will find not time measuring but so much time; and this is not the same as time. It is one thing to say time and another to say so much time; before "so much" one must say what it is that is so much. Perhaps time is the number that measured the motion from outside the motion, like ten for the horses, not taken with the horses. What this number is, then, which is what it is before it measures, as ten is, has not been said. Perhaps it is the number that measured by running along according to the before and after of the motion. But what this number according to before and after is, is not yet plain. At any rate, measuring according to before and after, whether by a point or by anything else, it will measure entirely according to time. This number that measures motion by before and after will then be holding on to time and touching it, in order to measure. For either the before and after of place is taken, like the start of a race-course, or one must take the before and after of time. For, in general, before and after are, the one, time ending at the now, the other, time beginning from the now. So time is something other than the number that measures motion by before and after, not only any motion, but orderly motion too.

@p
@greek ἔπειτα διὰ τί ἀριθμοῦ
Again, why is it that when number is added, whether on the side of the measured or of the measuring (the same number can be both), there will be time, but when there is motion, with before and after wholly belonging to it, there will not be time? It is as if someone said a magnitude is not as much as it is unless someone grasps how much it is. Since time is, and is said to be, without limit, how could there be number about it, unless someone took a part of it and measured it, in which case it was there before it was measured? And why will it not be there before the soul that measures? Unless someone says it gets its coming into being from the soul. For it certainly is not because of the measuring: it is not needed; it is as much as it is even if no one measures. If someone says it is the soul that uses the magnitude for measuring, what would this have to do with the concept of time?
!flag 2 therefore, which measures motion by prior and posterior, will be successive to, and in contact with time | Taylor's footnote numbers are printed into the scan here.

@ch III.7.10 | What follows motion
To call time something that follows motion does not say what it is before saying what follows; and whatever this is, it is said to be in time. Enough of reviewing others; the views already answered serve against the rest. We must now say what time is.

@p
To say it is what follows on motion, what that is, is not the mark of one teaching or able to say, before saying what follows; for perhaps that would be time. One must consider whether this follower is later, or at once, or earlier, if there is such a follower at all; for however it is spoken of, it is spoken of in time. If so, time will be what follows motion in time. But since we are looking not for what it is not, but for what it is; and much has been said by many before us on each position, which, if someone went through it, would make history rather than inquiry; and something has been said about them in passing; and against the one who says it is the measure of the motion of the universe one can argue from what has already been said, both the rest and what has now been said about the measure of motion (all of it, apart from the point about irregularity, will fit them too): it follows that we say what time must be thought to be.
!note "What follows motion" was the view of Epicurus, time as "an accident of accidents".
!flag he who discusses these would rather compose a history [than discover the nature of time ] | Taylor's words in square brackets are his own.

@ch III.7.11 | Time born when soul set out from eternity
Go back to eternity, the still, whole, unlimited life. Time was not yet. A restless power of soul, wanting to rule itself and seek more than the present, moved, and time moved with it. As a seed unfolds into a weaker length, soul, making the world in imitation, made itself temporal in place of eternity and put the world under time. Time is the life of soul in a motion passing from one way of life to another: the image of eternity, one by continuity, unlimited by always going on, a whole by parts, in soul as eternity is in being.

@p
We must, then, take ourselves back again to that state which we said belonged to eternity, that untroubled life, all together and already unlimited, unbending in every way, standing in one and towards one. Time was not yet, or at least was not for those things; it was to come into being, by the rational order and nature of what comes after. With these at rest in themselves, how time first fell out, one would perhaps not call on the Muses to tell, since they were not yet; and perhaps not even if the Muses were there then. But one might ask time itself, now that it has come into being, how it came to light and came to be. It would say something like this about itself: that before, before it had produced this "before" and needed the "after", it rested with it in being, not being time, and was itself at rest in that. But there was a meddlesome nature, which wanted to rule itself and be its own, and chose to seek more than what it had present; and it moved, and time moved with it; and moving always on to the "next" and the "after", and not the same but other and again other, making a certain length of our journey, we made time, an image of eternity.
!note "An image of eternity" is from the Timaeus (37d). The "meddlesome nature" is the soul.
!flag But we from this motion producing a certain length of progression, conceive time to be the image of eternity | The Greek says "we made time" (εἰργάσμεθα), not that we "conceive" it to be the image; it is the soul's motion, with ours, that brings time about.

@p
@greek ἐπεὶ γὰρ ψυχῆς ἦν τις δύναμις
For since there was a power of soul that was not at rest, which wanted always to carry over what it saw there into something else, it did not want the whole to be present to it all together. As the rational forming power, unfolding itself from a seed at rest, makes its way out into much, as it thinks, but does away with the much by dividing, and, in place of the one in itself, spends the one not in itself and goes out into a weaker length: just so the soul, making the perceptible world in imitation of that one, moving with a motion not the one there, but like the one there and wanting to be an image of it, first made itself temporal, making time in place of eternity; then it gave what had come to be into the service of time, making the whole of it to be in time, and wrapping all its passages in time. For the world moves in soul (there is no other place of this universe than soul), and so it moved in soul's time too. For, putting out its activity one after another, then another again in order, it produced, with the activity, the succession; and with another thinking after that one, there went out what was not there before, since neither had the thinking been put into act, nor was the present life like the one before it. So at once there was another life, and in being another it had another time. So the spreading out of life had time, and the ever-forward of life always has time, and the life that has passed has past time.
!flag in the first place indeed, renders itself temporal, producing this instead of eternity | Taylor is right; the Greek has the rare verb ἐχρόνωσεν, "made itself temporal", "timed itself".

@p
@greek εἰ οὖν χρόνον τις λέγοι
If, then, someone were to say that time is the life of soul in a motion passing from one way of life to another, would he seem to be saying something? For if eternity is life at rest, in the same and in the same way, and already unlimited, and time must be an image of eternity, as this universe is to that one, then in place of the life there we must say there is another life, that of this power of soul, as if bearing the same name; and in place of intellectual motion, the motion of some part of soul; in place of sameness and "in the same way" and remaining, what does not remain in the same but is active now in one thing, now in another; in place of the unextended and one, an image of the one, the one in continuity; in place of what is already unlimited and whole, what goes on to the unlimited by always going on to the next; in place of the whole all together, what will be a whole part by part, and always will be. For so it will imitate what is already whole, all together and already unlimited, if it means always to be gaining more in its being; for so its being will imitate the being of that one. And time must not be taken as outside soul, as eternity is not outside being there; nor as following or later, any more than there; but as seen within it, and being in it and with it, as eternity is there.
!flag If, therefore, some one should say that time is the energy 1 of soul | The Greek says time is the life (ζωή) of soul in a motion of passing, not its "energy" (activity); Taylor's footnote 1 may say so, but his text has "energy".

@ch III.7.12 | The heavens show time; they do not make it
Time is the length of soul's life going on in smooth, silent changes. If soul turned back to eternity and was still, time would be gone, and the sphere with it: so soul's motion and life produce time, which is why Plato says time came to be with the universe. The motions of the stars were made to show and mark off time, the day and night giving the idea of number; time is not itself a measure, and is measured by the circuit only incidentally, as a magnitude is "what a cubit measures".

@p
One must understand from this too that this nature, this length of such a life, going on in changes that are smooth and alike and go on without a sound, has its activity continuous. If, then, in thought we were to make this power turn back and stop this life, which it now has without stopping and never ceasing, because it is the activity of a soul that always is, not towards itself nor in itself, but in making and coming into being; if, then, we supposed it no longer active, but with this activity stopped and this part of the soul turned to what is there and to eternity and remaining at rest, what would there still be after eternity? What would be other and other, with all things remaining in one? What would be before, or after, or more? Where would soul turn its attention to something other than what it is in? Or rather, not even to that; for it would have to have stood apart from it first, to turn its attention to it. For not even the sphere itself would be there, since it does not exist before time; for it too both is and moves in time; and if it stood still while that soul was active, we would measure how long its rest was, as long as that soul is outside eternity. If, then, when that soul withdraws and becomes one, time is done away with, it is plain that the beginning of its motion towards these things, and this life, produces time.
!flag And though time should stop, this sphere still continuing to energize | The Greek has it the other way: if the sphere stood still while the soul went on being active, we would still measure its rest. "As long as the permanency of eternity is external to it" is not there either: it is "as long as that soul is outside" eternity.
!flag it is evident that the commencement of its motion, round the earth | The Greek has "its motion towards these things", the things here, not "round the earth".

@p
@greek διὸ καὶ εἴρηται ἅμα
That is why it is said that time came to be together with this universe, because soul produced it along with this universe. For in such activity this universe too came to be; and the activity is time, and the universe is in time. If someone says that the courses of the stars are called times by him, let him remember that he says they came to be for showing and marking off time, and that there might be a clear measure. For since it was not possible for the soul to mark off time itself, nor for people to measure each part of it among themselves, invisible as it is and not to be grasped, and especially for people who did not know how to count, he made day and night, through which it was possible, by their difference, to grasp two, from which, he says, came the idea of number. Then, grasping how long it is from sunrise to sunrise again, they could have so long an interval of time, the kind of motion being regular, which we lean on; and we use such a thing as a measure, a measure of time; for time itself is not a measure.
!note From the Timaeus: time came to be with the heaven (38b); the sun, moon and planets were made to mark off time (38c); day and night gave the idea of number (39b, 47a).

@p
@greek πῶς γὰρ ἂν καὶ μετροῖ
For how would it measure, and what would it say in measuring, "this is as much as I am, so much"? Who, then, is this "I"? Surely that by which the measuring is done. So it is, in order to measure, and is not the measure. So the motion of the universe, being measured, will be measured by time, and time will not be the measure of motion by what it is, but, being something else first, will incidentally show how much the motion is. And the one motion taken in so much time, counted many times, will lead to the idea of how much time has passed. So if someone says that the motion and the circuit in a way measure time, as far as possible, as showing, in their own so much, the so much of time, which cannot otherwise be grasped or understood, he would not be out of place in his way of showing. So what is measured by the circuit, that is, what is shown by it, will be time, not produced by the circuit but shown by it. And so the measure of motion is what is measured by a definite motion, and is measured by it, being other than it; since as measuring it was one thing and as measured another, but measured incidentally. It would be just as if someone said that magnitude is what a cubit measures, not saying what it is, defining magnitude; or as if someone, unable to show motion itself because it is without limit, said it was what is measured by place; for, taking the place that the motion went over, he would say it was as much as the place.
!flag For if that which measures was one thing, and that which is measured another, but is measured accidentally | Taylor's "if" makes a condition of what the Greek gives as a reason: "since, as measuring, it was one thing, and as measured another".

@ch III.7.13 | Time is in soul, as eternity is in being
The circuit shows the time it is in; time itself is first, the "in which" of all else. The philosophers called it the measure of motion, putting the incidental for the essential; Plato says only that it came to be with the heaven as a moving image of eternity. Soul's own motion is first in time and produces it, and is everywhere in the world. Bodily motion leads back to soul's motion, and that to the unextended. Time is in every soul of this kind alike, and all are one, so time is not torn apart.

@p
So the circuit shows time, in which it itself is. But time itself must no longer have an "in which"; it must be first what it is, that in which the other things move and rest smoothly and in order, and it must show itself and appear from something orderly to our understanding, but not come into being from it, whether that thing is at rest or moving, but rather from what is moving. For motion moves us more towards knowing and passing over to time than rest does, and how much something has moved is more knowable than how long it has rested. That is why they were carried into saying "measure of motion" instead of saying "measured by motion", and then adding what it is that is measured by motion, and not saying something that comes about about it incidentally, and these things the wrong way round. But perhaps they did not have them the wrong way round, and it is we who do not understand them; when they said plainly "measure" in the sense of "measured", we missed their meaning. The cause of our not understanding is that they do not make clear in their writings what time is, measuring or measured, writing for those who knew and had heard them.

@p
@greek ὁ μέντοι Πλάτων οὔτε μετροῦν
Plato, however, said neither that its being is measuring nor that it is measured by something, but that, to show it, the circuit took a very small part for a very small part of it, so that from it one could know of what kind and how much time is. But wanting to show its being, he says that it came to be together with the heaven, after the pattern of eternity, and as a moving image, because time does not remain, since the life it runs and races along with does not remain; and together with the heaven, because such a life makes the heaven too, and one life produces heaven and time. If this life were turned back into one, if it could be, at once time would have stopped, being in this life, and the heaven too, not having this life. If someone takes the before and after of this life and motion, and calls it time, saying that it is something, but says that the before and after of the truer motion is not anything, he would be utterly absurd, granting to a lifeless motion that it has a before and after, and time with it, but not granting this to the motion by imitation of which this one exists, from which the before and after first came about, since it is a motion that works of itself, and as it produces each of its activities, so it produces their succession, and with the producing, their passing from one to the next.
!flag that the circulation [of the universe] is allotted something which is the smallest [i. e. the centre,] | Taylor's gloss "the centre" in square brackets is his own and misleads: the Greek says the circuit takes a very small part (as the day) for the smallest part of time, so we can know time from it.
!note "A moving image of eternity" is from the Timaeus (37d), and "together with the heaven" from 38b.

@p
@greek διὰ τί οὖν ταύτην μὲν
Why, then, do we bring this motion of the universe under the embrace of that one, and say it is in time, but not the motion of soul too, which is in itself and in an everlasting passage? Because what is before it is eternity, which does not run along or stretch along with it. This motion, then, is first in time, and it produced time and has it with its own activity. How, then, is time everywhere? Because that soul is apart from no part of the world, as the soul in us is apart from no part of us. If someone says that time is in no real existence or is not there at all, he will plainly be calling God himself a liar when he says "was" and "will be"; for it will be and was in the way that that in which he says it will be is. But for such people there is another kind of argument.
!flag for we are deceived about its essence, in the same manner as when we say of God that he was or will be | Taylor's words are not in the Greek. The Greek says that whoever denies that time is real makes God himself false when he says "was" and "will be", taking "God" as Plato's maker in the Timaeus, or the god who speaks there.

@p
@greek ἐκεῖνο δὲ ἐνθυμεῖσθαι
Beyond all that has been said, one must keep this in mind: when someone takes a man in motion, how far he has gone, he takes how much the motion is too; and when he takes the motion, say, the walking, he sees that the movement in the man before this motion was so much, if it kept the body's motion going for so much. He will lead the body moved for so much time back to the motion of so much, since this is the cause, and to its time; and this motion back to the motion of the soul, which was spread out in equal parts. And the motion of the soul back to what? Whatever he chooses will now be unextended. This, then, is what is first, and that in which the rest are; but it is itself no longer in anything, since it will have nothing to be in. And the same holds of the soul of the universe. Is time, then, in us too? It is in every soul of this kind, alike in all, and all are one. So time will not be torn apart; any more than eternity, which in another way is in all the things of the same form.
!flag he directs his attention to the boundary s of motion existing in the man prior to this motion, in order that he may judge whether he has walked to the full extent of this boundary | The Greek speaks of the movement in the man, in his soul, before the bodily motion, which kept the body's motion going so far; not of a "boundary" of motion.

@treatise IV.2 | On the essence of the soul
A short treatise, the second in Porphyry's order on the soul's essence. Having shown elsewhere that the soul is not a body, nor a harmony, nor an entelechy, Plotinus places it among the intelligibles and goes further. There is what is wholly divided, bodies; there is what is wholly undivided, intellect; and between them, forms and qualities that are divided in bodies, and soul, which is divided about bodies because it is present in every part, yet undivided because it is whole in each. This is what Plato meant when he said the maker mixed a third kind of being from the undivided and the divided. Sensation proves it: if soul were divided like a body, one part would not feel what another suffers; if it were simply one, it would give life to no body.

@ch IV.2.1 | Divided and undivided being
There is being wholly divided, as bodies are, and being wholly undivided, needing no place, like the centre from which the lines of a circle go out. Next to bodies are forms and qualities, divided in them but whole in each piece. Next to the undivided is soul: a being that gives itself to bodies and is divided about them, yet remains one, whole in all their parts and whole in any part. The division belongs to the bodies, not to the soul.

@p
Looking for what the essence of the soul is, we showed it is not a body, and not a harmony among things without body either; we left aside the entelechy, as not true as it is said and not showing what it is; and we said it belongs to the intelligible nature and to the divine portion. Perhaps we would have said something clear about its essence. Still, it is better to go further. Then we divided by distinguishing the perceptible and the intelligible nature, and setting the soul in the intelligible. Now let it lie in the intelligible; but by another road let us track down what is closest to its nature. We say, then, that some things are divided in the first place and scattered by their nature: these are things none of whose parts is the same as another part or as the whole, and their part must be less than the whole. These are the perceptible magnitudes and masses, each of which has its own place, and the same cannot be in many places at once.
!note The earlier work is Ennead IV.7, On the immortality of the soul, which Porphyry places later but Plotinus wrote earlier: it argues that soul is not a body, not a harmony and not an entelechy. "The divine portion" is Plato's phrase (Phaedrus 230a, Republic 493a).
!flag the entelecheia ,* or perfection of the body | Taylor is right that the word is Aristotle's (On the Soul II.1, 412a): the soul as "the first actuality of a natural body". "Of the body" is his own addition.

@p
@greek ἡ δέ ἐστιν ἀντιτεταγμένη
But the being set over against this admits no division at all; it is without parts and undivided, and admits no extension, not even in thought; it needs no place and comes to be in none of the beings, neither in parts nor in wholes, riding, as it were, on all beings together, not to be settled in them, but because the others cannot be, and do not want to be, without it; being always the same, common to all that come after, like the centre in a circle, from which all the lines going out to the circumference, fastened to it, leave it none the less staying by itself; they have their origin and being from it, and share in the point, and the partless is their principle, yet they have gone out fastened to it. With this, which is undivided in the first place among the intelligibles and leader among beings, and that again among perceptibles which is divided every way, there is before the perceptible, and near it and in it, another nature, divided not in the first place, as bodies are, but divided when it comes to be in bodies: so that when the bodies are divided, the form in them is divided too, yet is whole in each of the divided pieces, the same becoming many, each of which stands quite apart from the other, since it has become wholly divided. Such are colours and all qualities, and each shape, which can be whole in many things at once that are apart, with no part of it affected as the other is. So this too must be set down as wholly divided.
!flag and is the foundation 1 of all following natures | The Greek has "common" (κοινόν) to all that come after, not "foundation".

@p
@greek πρὸς δ’ αὖ ἐκείνῃ τῇ ἀμερίστῳ
And again, next to that wholly undivided nature, there is another being, coming from it, which has its undividedness from it, but by its going out from it hastens towards the other nature, and so stands in the middle of both, the undivided and first, and the divided about bodies that is upon bodies. It is not as colour and every quality is the same in many places in many masses of bodies, while what is in each stands quite apart from the other, as far as one mass stands apart from another. Even if the magnitude is one, still what is the same in each part has no fellowship for being affected together, because this "same" is one thing here and another there; for the sameness is an affection, not itself a being. But the nature we say is next to this, drawing near to the undivided being, is a being and comes to be in bodies, and happens to be divided about them, not undergoing this before it gives itself to bodies. In whatever bodies it comes to be, even if it comes to be in the greatest, spread out over everything, giving itself to the whole it does not cease to be one: not one as a body is one, for a body is one by continuity, and each of its parts is different, and in a different place; nor as a quality is one.
!flag because this sameness is at the same time attended with [a predominant] difference | Taylor's words in square brackets are his own.

@p
@greek ἡ δ’ ὁμοῦ μεριστή τε καὶ ἀμέριστος
The nature that is at once divided and undivided, which we say is soul, is not one as the continuous is one, having one part and then another; it is divided, because it is in all the parts of that in which it is, and undivided, because it is whole in all of them and whole in any one of them. Whoever sees this will know the greatness of the soul and its power, how divine and wonderful a thing it is, among the natures beyond things. Having no magnitude, it is with every magnitude; and being here, it is there again, not by another part but by the same. So it is divided and again not divided; or rather, it is not divided and has not become divided; for it stays whole with itself, and is divided about bodies, since the bodies, by their own divisibility, cannot receive it undividedly. So the division is an affection of the bodies, not of it.
!flag and that it possesses a supernatural essence | The Greek says soul is "among the natures beyond things" (τῶν ὑπὲρ τὰ χρήματα φύσεων), playing on χρῆμα, "thing", just used of the soul; Taylor's "supernatural essence" is not quite it.

@ch IV.2.2 | Why the soul must be both
If the soul were divided like a body, the soul in the finger would feel its own pain and no other part would; there would be many souls in each of us. The Stoic account of sensations passed on to a ruling part fails: which part feels, and how could one know a thing from eyes and ears together? If soul were wholly one, it would animate nothing. So soul is one and many, as Plato's "third kind of being" says: soul one and many, forms in bodies many and one, bodies many only, the highest one only.

@p
That the nature of the soul had to be of this kind, and that besides this there cannot be a soul that is only undivided or only divided, but that it must be both in this way, is plain from what follows. If it were like bodies, having one part here and another there, when one part was affected the other part would not come to perceive what the affected part felt; but that soul, say the one about the finger, as being different and on its own, would perceive the affection; and there would be many souls in all governing each of us; and this universe too would have not one but unlimited souls apart from each other. For continuity, unless it comes together into one, is pointless. For what those who deceive themselves say, that perceptions reach the ruling part by passing on, is not to be accepted.
!note The "ruling part" (τὸ ἡγεμονοῦν) and the passing on of perceptions to it are Stoic teaching.

@p
@greek πρῶτον μὲν γὰρ ἡγεμονοῦν
First, a ruling part of the soul is spoken of without examination. How will they divide, and say one part is this and another that? By what size of quantity will they separate each, or by what difference of quality, when the mass is one and continuous? And will the ruling part alone perceive, or the other parts too? If the perception falls on the ruling part alone, in what place will it perceive what is perceived? If on another part of the soul not made by nature to perceive, this part will not pass on its affection to the ruling part, and there will be no perception at all. And if it falls on the ruling part itself, either it will fall on a part of it, and when this has perceived the rest will no longer perceive, for it would be pointless; or there will be many perceptions, unlimited, and not all alike, but one will say "I was affected first", another "I perceived the affection of another"; and where the affection came about, each will not know, except the first. Or each part of the soul will be deceived, thinking it came about where it is itself. If not only the ruling part but any part perceives, why will one be ruling and another not? Or why must the perception go up to it? And how will it know one thing from many perceptions, from ears and eyes?
!flag it will either fall on a part of it, and this being sentient, the remaining parts will no longer be sensitive ; for it would be in vain | Taylor is right. But his "Hence, one sensible perception will say" makes a conclusion of what the Greek gives as the second case: the many unlike perceptions, one saying "I was affected first", another "I perceived another's affection".

@p
@greek εἰ δ’ αὖ πάντη ἓν ἡ ψυχὴ
But if the soul were wholly one, wholly undivided and one in itself, and escaped the nature of multitude and division altogether, nothing whole that the soul took hold of would be ensouled; but setting itself, as it were, at the centre of each thing, it would leave the whole mass of the living being without soul. So the soul must be one and many in this way, divided and undivided, and we must not disbelieve, as if it were impossible for the same thing to be one and in many places. If we do not accept this, there will be no nature holding together and governing all things, which holds all things in its embrace together and leads them with wisdom; many, since the beings are many, but one, so that what holds them together may be one; giving life to all the parts by its many, and leading them wisely by its undivided one. In things where there is no wisdom, the one that leads imitates this.
!flag By its multitudinous one, therefore, it supplies all the parts of body with life | The Greek says "by its many one" (τῷ πολλῷ αὐτῆς ἑνί): the soul is a one that is many.

@p
@greek τοῦτο ἄρα ἐστὶ τὸ θείως
This, then, is what is said in a divine riddle: "from the being that is undivided and always the same, and from the being that becomes divided about bodies, he mixed a third form of being from both." So the soul is one and many in this way; the forms in bodies are many and one; bodies are many only; and the highest is one only.
!note The quotation is the Timaeus (35a), where the maker mixes the soul of the world.

@treatise IV.3 | On difficulties about the soul (I)
The first of three books of problems about the soul (IV.3 to IV.5), and the longest treatise Taylor translated. Plotinus first asks whether our souls are parts of the soul of the world, and answers no: all souls, the world's and ours, are one and many as the theorems are of one science, sisters coming from one soul as minds come from one intellect. Then he asks how soul is in body: not as in a place or a vessel, nor as a form in matter, but as the body is in soul, like air in light. Souls come down, each at its time, by a kind of natural law, to the body made ready for them, and each has the share it deserves. The last part asks what in us remembers, and answers that memory belongs to soul, not to the body, and to both the imagining powers, the higher and the lower; when the soul goes up it forgets more and more.

@ch IV.3.1 | Are our souls parts of the world's soul?
Nothing is more worth inquiring into than the soul, for it gives knowledge of what comes from it and what it comes from, and the god bids us know ourselves. Some say our souls come from the soul of the universe, as parts: they are like in kind, Plato said our body is part of the world's body and so our soul part of its soul, we follow its circuit and take our characters from it, and "all soul cares for all that is without soul".

@p
It would be right to make a study of the soul: of the difficulties about it that we must find our way through, or, if we stay stuck in them, at least gain this much, to know what is hard in them. For about what could one more reasonably spend time in long speaking and inquiry than about this? For many other reasons, and because it gives knowledge both ways, of the things it is the principle of and of those it comes from. And we would be obeying the god's command, who bids us know ourselves, in examining this. Wanting to inquire into the rest and find it, we would justly ask what this is that inquires, and seek to grasp what loves among the sights it loves. For in the intellect of the whole too there was the twofold; so that it is reasonable that in the particular ones it should be more so in one way and less in another. How souls receive the gods must be considered. But that will be when we inquire how soul comes to be in body; now let us go back to those who say our souls too come from the soul of the universe.
!note The command is the Delphic "know yourself", from Apollo's temple.
!flag Is it necessary to consider such doubts as pertain to the soul as sufficiently solved | Taylor makes a question of the opening, where the Greek says it would be right to make the study, to find our way through the difficulties or at least to know them.
!flag especially since we desire to apprehend that which is lovely in the objects of contemplation | The Greek has "to grasp what loves (τὸ ἐραστὸν ποθοῦν) among the sights": the soul as the lover, not "that which is lovely".

@p
@greek οὐδὲ γὰρ ἴσως ἱκανὸν
They will perhaps say it is not enough, to show that ours are not parts of it, that ours reach as far as the soul of the universe reaches, or that they are intellectual in a like way, even if they grant the likeness; for parts are of the same form as wholes. And they will bring Plato forward as holding this, when, confirming that the universe has a soul, he says that as our body is a part of the body of the universe, so our soul is a part of the soul of the universe. And our following the circuit of the universe is both said and clearly shown, and we take our characters and fortunes from there, and, coming to be inside it, we take our soul from what surrounds us. And as each part of us takes from our soul, so we too, by the same reasoning, being parts with respect to the whole, share as parts in the whole soul. And "all soul cares for all that is without soul" means just this, that nothing else outside soul is left after the soul of the whole; for this is what has everything without soul in its care.
!note Plato's argument is in the Philebus (30a); "all soul cares for all that is without soul" is the Phaedrus (246b).
!flag For perhaps they will say it is not sufficient [in order to establish this hypothesis,] | Taylor's bracket gets the sense wrong: the opponents say these things are not enough to show that our souls are not parts of the world's soul.

@ch IV.3.2 | In what sense a part?
Not a part as of a body, nor as two is part of ten, nor as an arc of a circle: soul is not a quantity, and all souls are said to be alike and whole. Perhaps as a theorem is part of a science, which stays whole while each theorem has it all potentially. But then the whole soul belongs to nothing, not even to the world; the world's soul too is one of the parts. Why, then, is one the world's?

@p
Against this, first, it must be said that, in making souls of the same form by granting that they reach the same things, and giving them a common genus, they put them outside being parts; they would more justly say that it is one and the same, and each is all. But making it one they hang it on something else, which, being no longer this one's or that one's but nobody's, either the world's or anything else's, does itself what the world's soul and any ensouled thing's does. For it is right that not all soul belongs to something, since it is a being; but that there is soul which belongs to nothing at all, and that those which belong to something come to be so at some time incidentally. Perhaps we should get more clearly how "part" is said in such things. Part as of bodies, whether the body is of the same form throughout or not, we must leave aside, noting only this: in things whose parts are alike, when "part" is said, the part is by mass, not by form, as with whiteness. The whiteness in a portion of milk is not a part of the whiteness of the whole milk; it is the whiteness of a portion, not a portion of whiteness; for whiteness has no magnitude at all and is not a quantity.
!flag exclude them from ranking as parts of one soul, and will rather make one and the same soul | Taylor is right in sense, but the "many other absurdities" further on are about making the soul a number of units.

@p
@greek ἀλλὰ τοῦτο μὲν οὕτως
So much for that. When we speak of a part in things that are not bodies, we would say it either as in numbers, as two of ten (let this be said of bare numbers only); or as a part of a circle or a line; or as a part of a science. With units and figures, as with bodies, the whole must be lessened by the division into parts, and each part must be less than the whole; being quantities, and having their being in quantity, not being quantity itself, they must become greater and less. In these senses a part cannot be spoken of in soul. It is not a quantity in this way, so that the whole is ten and one soul a unit; many other absurdities would follow, and ten would not be one thing, and either each unit would be a soul, or soul would be made of things all without soul; and the part of the whole soul has been granted to be of the same form. With the continuous, it is not necessary that the part be such as the whole, as of a circle or a square; or not all the pieces are alike, in the cases where one can take a part like the whole, as triangles in triangles, but they differ; but they set down soul as of one form. And with a line the part has being a line, but here too it differs in magnitude. With soul, if the difference of the particular soul from the whole were said to be in magnitude, it will be a quantity and a body, taking its difference as soul from quantity. But all souls were supposed alike and whole.

@p
@greek φαίνεται δὲ οὐδὲ μεριζομένη
It plainly is not divided as magnitudes are; nor would they themselves grant that the whole is cut up into parts; they would use up the whole, and it would be only a name, if there ever was a whole, as if, when wine was divided into many jars, what is in each jar were called part of the whole wine. Is it a part, then, as a theorem of a science is called part of the whole science, the science itself remaining none the less, and the division being as it were the bringing forward and the activity of each? In such a thing each has the whole science potentially, and the science is none the less whole. If it is so with the whole soul and the others, the whole, whose parts are of this kind, will not belong to anything, but be by itself; nor, then, will it be the world's, but this too will be one of those in part. So all are parts of one, being of the same form. But how is one the world's, and the others the souls of parts of the world?
!flag And unless the first soul was every soul, it would be a name alone | The Greek says the whole would be "only a name, if there ever was a whole": dividing it would use it up.

@ch IV.3.3 | Not as the soul in a finger
Is it a part as the soul in a finger is part of the whole animal's soul? That would leave no soul outside body, or the world's soul outside the world's body. If the world's soul gives itself to each living thing, it is undivided, the same in many, and there is no whole and part; as in us the same soul is in eye and ear, with different powers acting because the organs differ, and one judge of all.

@p
Are they, then, parts in the way one might say, in one living being, that the soul in the finger is part of the whole soul in the whole animal? But this account either makes no soul come to be outside body, or makes the whole, the one called the soul of the universe, not in body but outside the body of the world. This must be looked into; now we must examine how it might be said by way of the image. If the soul of the universe gives itself to all the particular living beings, and so each is a part, it would not, divided, give itself to each; it will be the same everywhere, the whole one and the same being in many at once. This would no longer allow one to be whole and another a part, especially in things where the same power is present. For even where the work is different, one here and another there, as with eyes and ears, we must not say that one portion of soul is present to sight and another to the ears (dividing in this way is for other things), but the same, even if a different power is active in each; for all are in both; and because the organs differ, the perceptions come out different, all of them, however, being of forms, of a soul able to take the shape of all.

@p
@greek δηλοῖ δὲ καὶ τὸ
This is shown too by the necessity that all come into one. The organs being different, and not able to receive everything, the affections come out different in the organs, but the judging is by the same one, like a judge who has given his mind to what was said and done. But it has been said that what is one is everywhere, even in the different works; and if they are like perceptions, it is not possible for each of them to think, but that one; "if thinking were its own, each would be on its own." And when the soul is rational, and rational in such a way as the whole is said to be, what is called a part will be the same, but not a part of the whole.
!flag Nevertheless, all the powers rank among forms, and are reduced to a form which is capable of being fashioned according to all things | Taylor's words are close, but the Greek says the perceptions are "of forms", of a soul able to take shape to all of them, without "reduced to".

@ch IV.3.4 | One soul in all, some in bodies, some not
If all souls are one, how is one in body and another not? With intellect there is no difficulty, but with soul there is, unless one soul stays above and the world's soul and ours come from it, joined at their upper edge and reaching down here and there, like light divided among houses. The world's soul never comes down; ours have a part marked off here and turn to what needs care: the world's lowest soul is like the soul in a great plant, ours like worms in a rotten part of it, or like a gardener caring for them.

@p
What, then, must we say, if soul is one in this way, when someone inquires what follows, first puzzled whether one thing can be in all things at once in this way, and then when one soul is in a body and another not? For perhaps it will follow that every soul is always in body, and especially that of the universe; for it is not said, as ours is, to leave its body; though some say it will leave this one, yet will not be wholly outside body. But if it will be wholly outside body, how will one leave the body and the other not, being the same? With intellect, which separates itself by otherness into parts that differ greatly from one another but are always together (for this being would be undivided), no such difficulty would hold us. But with the soul that is said to be divided about bodies, this being one thing for all souls would have many difficulties.
!flag In intellect, therefore, which is separated from itself by difference | Taylor's "separated from itself" is right; the Greek ἑτερότητι is "by otherness", the intelligible kind Plato names in the Sophist.

@p
@greek εἰ μή τις τὸ μὲν ἓν στήσειεν
Unless one sets the one on its own, not falling into body, and then all souls from it, that of the whole and the others, as being together up to a point, and one by belonging to nothing in particular, but, fastened by their limits and together with one another above, reaching out here and there; like light, divided by houses when it is already near the earth, and not divided, but one none the less. The soul of the universe always stays above, since coming down is not for it, nor the lower, nor turning to the things here; ours not always, because they have a part marked off for them here and turn to what needs care. The lowest of the soul of the universe is like the soul in a great plant, which governs the plant without toil and without a sound; the lower part of us is as if worms came to be in a rotten part of the plant, for that is how the ensouled body is in the universe; while the rest of the soul, which is of the same form as the upper parts of the whole, is as if a gardener became concerned for the worms in the plant and his cares were given to the plant. Or as if someone said that a man who is healthy and with others who are healthy is with them, at whatever he is doing or giving himself to contemplation; but when he falls ill and is busy with the care of his body, he is with the body and has become the body's.
!flag by the projecting energies of intellect | Taylor's words are not in the Greek, which says only that the souls reach out "here and there" (ὡδὶ καὶ ὡδί).
!flag resembles a great vegetable soul, which without labour and silently governs the plant of which it is the soul, [i. e. in the same manner as worms are generated in wounds] | Taylor's bracket is misplaced in the scan; it belongs to the worms in a rotten part of the plant, which is our body.

@ch IV.3.5 | Socrates will not perish above
How will this soul be yours and that one his? Socrates is Socrates while his soul is in body; will he perish when he comes to the best? No: nothing that is perishes. Up there the minds do not perish, since they are not divided like bodies into one, but each remains what it is in otherness. So too souls, hanging from each intellect, are rational forms of minds, unfolded further, and stay each one and all one.

@p
But how then will one soul be yours, another his, another someone else's? Is it his by the lower part, and not his but that one's by the upper? But in that way Socrates will be, while Socrates' soul is in a body, and will perish when he comes to be most of all in the best. No: none of the beings will perish. For there too the minds will not perish, since they are not divided bodily into one, but each remains holding in otherness the same, what it is to be it. So too the souls, hanging next in order from each intellect, being rational forms of minds and more unfolded than they, as if become much from little, joined to the little, which is less divided than each of them, already wishing to be divided and unable to go to the end of division, keeping the same and the other, each remain one and all are one together. The heading of the argument has been said: that all are from one, and those from one are many, divided and not divided in the same way as intellect; and the soul that remains is one rational form of intellect, and from it the partial and immaterial rational forms, as there.

@ch IV.3.6 | Why the world's soul made the world
Why did the world's soul make the world and ours did not, though it is of the same form? Those with the same science may rule more or fewer. The world's soul did not leave the whole and had the body about it while there; ours took their lots when their sister had already prepared their dwellings. It looks to the whole intellect, they to their particular ones. Power is greater the more it stays above; ours were drawn down. Plato's "second and third" mean nearer and further.

@p
Why has the soul of the universe, being of the same form, made the world, and that of each person not, though it too has all things in itself? That it can come to be and be in many at once has been said; now it must be said how. For perhaps how the same thing in one and another does or undergoes, or both, this or that, will be known; or this must be looked at by itself. How, then, and why has it made the world, and the others govern a part of the world? Perhaps it is no wonder that of those who have the same science some rule over more and others over fewer. But one could say why. There is, one might say, a difference among souls in this especially, that one did not stand off from the whole soul, but, being there, had the body around itself, while the others, when it was already there, with their sister soul, as it were, ruling, took their lots, as if it had made their dwellings ready for them. There is a difference too in that it looks to the whole intellect, they rather to their own particular ones. Perhaps they too could make, but when it had made, it was no longer possible for them as well, since it began first. The same difficulty would arise if any other had first taken hold.
!flag that the difference of souls is greater, so far as one of them does not depart from the soul of the universe | The Greek says it is "from the whole soul" (τῆς ὅλης) that the world's soul did not stand apart, the one soul above all, not "the soul of the universe".

@p
@greek βέλτιον δὲ λέγειν τῷ ἐξηρτῆσθαι
It is better to say that it is by hanging more upon what is above; for the power of those that have inclined towards that is greater. For keeping themselves in safety, they make from the greatest ease (for it belongs to a greater power not to be affected in what it makes); and the power comes from staying above. So, staying in itself, it makes, and the things come to it; while the others themselves went to the things. So they stood off into the depth. Or rather, much of them, dragged down, drew them too down with it in their purposes. For "second and third" must be taken as said of being nearer and further, as with us too not all souls are in a like state towards what is there, but some might be united, others reach close in their longing, and others have this less, as they are active with powers not the same, some with the first, some with the one after, some with the third, though all have all.
!note "Second and third" is from the Timaeus (41d): the maker mixed our souls in the same bowl, "no longer pure in the same way, but second and third".
!flag It is better, however, to say that the soul of the wrorld rather than other souls fabricated the universe, because it in a greater degree adheres to intelligibles | Taylor is right in sense; the scan's "wrorld" is "world".

@ch IV.3.7 | What Plato meant
The Philebus says only that the heaven has a soul, since we, parts of its body, have souls. The Timaeus makes our souls later, from the same bowl, second and third; the Phaedrus distinguishes the perfect soul that rides above from the one that sheds its wings. Following the circuit and taking our characters from it no more makes us parts than taking them from places, waters, air and cities; and we set another soul against these influences, as a child in the womb has a soul other than its mother's.

@p
So much for this. But what of the passage in the Philebus that suggests the other souls are portions of the soul of the universe? The argument does not mean what someone thinks, but what was useful to it then: that the heaven too has a soul. He confirms this by saying it is absurd to call the heaven soulless when we, who have a part of the body of the universe, have a soul; for how could the part have one, if the universe were soulless? He makes his view plainest in the Timaeus, where, after the soul of the universe has come to be, he makes the others later, mixing them from the same bowl from which the soul of the whole came, making the other soul of the same form, and giving the difference by "second and third". And as for the Phaedrus' "all soul cares for all that is without soul": what could govern the nature of body and shape or order or make it, but soul? And it is not that one soul is made by nature able to do this and another not. "The perfect one", he says, the soul of the universe, "travels on high", not sinking in but riding, as it were, and acts upon the world; and "whichever is perfect governs so". When he says "the one that has shed its wings", he makes this one other than that.
!note The passages are the Philebus (30a), the Timaeus (41d), and the Phaedrus (246b-c).
!flag Nor is what he asserts in the Phaedrus wonderful | The Greek asks "what else could it be" that governs body but soul: Taylor's "wonderful" is not there.

@p
@greek τὸ δὲ συνέπεσθαι τῇ τοῦ παντὸς
Following the circuit of the universe, and bringing our characters from there, and being affected by it, would be no sign that our souls are parts. For soul is able to take many impressions from the nature of places, waters and air; and the dwellings in different cities and the blends of bodies leave their stamp. We said that being in the universe we have something of the soul of the whole, and we granted that we are affected by the circuit; but we set another soul against these, shown to be other above all by its resistance. As to our being born inside it, we say of mothers too that the soul that comes into the child is another, not the mother's.

@ch IV.3.8 | How souls differ, and the number of them
Souls are in sympathy, since all come from the one the world's comes from. They differ by bodies, by characters and by earlier lives; and by nature, as each is active with one power or another. They are an ordered whole of fixed number, since true beings stand fast and are each one in number; their unlimitedness is in power. Soul reaches through the whole body without being torn apart, and when an animal rots and many come from it, the one soul is not multiplied.

@p
This may be the way to solve these difficulties, and sympathy does not stand in the way of the argument; for being all from the same soul, from which the soul of the whole comes too, they are in sympathy. It has been said that soul is one and many. How the part differs from the whole has been said. It has been said in general about the difference of soul too, and now let it be said briefly that souls may come to differ because of bodies too, and above all in characters and in the works of thinking, from the lives lived before; for he says the choices come to souls according to their earlier lives. If one takes the nature of soul as a whole, the differences have been told in the passage where "second and third" were spoken of, and that all souls are all things, but each is according to what is active in it: that is, one is united in act, another in knowing, another in desiring, and different souls look at different things and are and become what they look at. And the fullness and completeness of souls is not the same in all. But the whole ordering of them is varied; for every rational form is one, much and varied, like a living being of soul with many shapes.
!note The choice of lives according to earlier lives is from the myth of Er in the Republic (620a).
!flag For in the Republic of Plato it is said | Taylor rightly names the passage; the Greek says only "he says".

@p
@greek εἰ δὴ τοῦτο, καὶ σύνταξίς ἐστι
If so, there is an ordering, and the beings are not torn apart from one another altogether, and there is nothing random in beings, when there is not even in bodies; and it follows that they are of some number. For again, the beings must stand fast, and the intelligibles be the same, and each be one in number; for so it is "this". With bodies, since the nature of the particular flows, the form being brought in from outside, being by form always belongs to them by imitation of the beings; but to the beings, since they are not from composition, being is in what is one in number, which belongs to them from the beginning, and neither does what was not come to be, nor will what is cease to be. For even if something were to make them, it would not be from matter; and if from matter, it must add something of being from itself; so that there will be change about that maker itself, if it makes now more and now less. And why now, and not always so? And what came to be would not be everlasting, if it were more and less; and soul is set down as everlasting. How, then, is it unlimited, if it will stand fast? It is unlimited in power, because the power is unlimited, not as if it would be divided without limit. For God, too, is not bounded.
!flag soul is supposed to be a thing of this kind | Taylor gets the Greek backwards: the soul is supposed to be everlasting, the kind of thing that does not admit more and less.

@p
@greek καὶ αὗται τοίνυν οὐ πέρατι
So these souls are not each what they are by a limit from outside, as being of so much; each is as much as it wants, and will never come to go out of itself, but reaches everywhere that it is in its nature to reach into bodies; it is not torn from itself when it is in the finger and in the foot. So too in the universe, wherever it reaches, in one part of a plant and in another, and even in a part cut off; so that it is both in the plant as it was from the beginning and in what was cut from it; for the body of the universe is one, and soul is everywhere in it as in one thing. And when an animal rots, if many come from it, that soul, the soul of the whole animal, is no longer in the body, for it does not have what can receive it, or it would not have died; but the things that come out of the rotting, fitted for the birth of animals, some of one kind and some of another, have soul, since soul is not lacking from anything, though one thing is able to receive it and another not. And the things that become ensouled in this way have not made more souls; for they hang from the one, which remains one. As in us, too, when some things are cut off and others grow in their place, the soul has left the first and come to the others, as long as the one remains. In the universe the one remains always; the things within it take soul and put it down, while the same powers of soul remain.
!flag nor yet does it perish | The Greek says "or it would not have died": the animal dies because its body can no longer take its soul.

@ch IV.3.9 | How soul comes into body
Soul enters body in two ways: from one body into another, or from being without body into body. Begin with the world's soul, though there was never a time when the world had no soul: the separation is for teaching. Soul, shining out like a great light, made darkness at its edge, saw it, and gave it form. The world is like a fine house that its maker never leaves; it lies in soul like a net in the sea, which spreads as far as it can, while soul is as great as it is because it has no size.

@p
But we must inquire how soul comes to be in body. What is the manner, and how? For this too is no less worth wondering at and inquiring into. Since the way of soul's entry into body is twofold (one comes about for a soul that is in a body, changing body, or going from a body of air or fire into one of earth, which they do not call a change of body, because where the entry came from is not plain; the other from being without body into any body at all, which would be soul's first fellowship with body), it would be right to look into this one, and ask what the experience is that comes about when soul, wholly pure of body, takes the nature of body around itself.
!flag and from an aerial [or fiery], becomes situated in a terrestrial body ; which some do not call a transmigration | Taylor's bracket is in the Greek ("of air or of fire"); his "transmigration" is μετενσωμάτωσις, "change of body".

@p
@greek περὶ μὲν δὴ τῆς τοῦ παντός
About the soul of the universe (for perhaps it is worth beginning there, or rather it is necessary): we must think that the entry and ensouling come about in the account for the sake of teaching and clearness. For there was no time when this universe was without soul, nor when body was there with soul absent, nor matter when it was unordered; but one can think of these by separating them from one another in the account; for it is possible to break up every composite by the account and by thought. The truth is this: if there were no body, soul would not go out, since there is no other place where it is its nature to be. If it is to go out, it will produce a place for itself, and so a body. While its rest is, as it were, made strong in rest itself, darkness came about, as from a great light shining out, at the outermost edge of the fire; and the soul, seeing it, since it had come to be, gave it form. For it was not lawful for what bordered on it to be without a share in rational form; it received what is called "dim in the dim thing that came to be".
!flag Hence, by this obscure nature which w^as generated by soul, that which is called obscure was received | The Greek says the darkness received as much form as is called "dim in the dim": a faint form in the faint thing that came to be.
!flag since it was likewise the cause of its subsistence | The Greek has "since it had come to be" (ἐπείπερ ὑπέστη), not that soul was its cause.

@p
@greek γενόμενος δὴ οἷον οἶκός τις
Having come to be like a fine and varied house, it was not cut off from its maker, nor again did he give himself to it in partnership; but, thought everywhere and in every part worthy of care, profitable to it for its being and its beauty, as far as it could share in being, and harmless to the one who presides, since he presides staying above, it is ensouled in such a way, having a soul not its own but for it, mastered, not mastering, held, not holding. For it lies in the soul that holds it up, and no part of it is without a share in it, as a net soaked in the waters might live, but cannot make what it is in its own. But the net, as the sea is already spread out, is spread out with it as far as it can; for each of its pieces cannot be anywhere but where it lies. But soul is so great by nature, because it is not of so much, that it takes in the whole body by the same, and wherever that is spread out, it is there; and if that were not, size would be no concern of soul's; for it is what it is. For the whole is as great as where soul is, and its size is bounded by how far, as it goes on, it has soul keeping it safe. And the shadow is as great as the rational form from the soul; and the rational form was such as to produce a magnitude as great as its form wanted to produce "magnitude".
!flag that it rannot with so much propriety be said to have a soul of its own, as to have a soul presiding over it | The Greek is "having a soul not its own, but for itself" (οὐχ αὑτοῦ, ἀλλ’ αὑτῷ); Taylor's "presiding over it" is his own.

@ch IV.3.10 | Soul orders the world by nature, not by art
Think of it all at once: air and light, sun and moon, in order first, second and third, with soul standing always, and its light reaching to the dim edge. Soul orders body by its rational forms, as forms in seeds shape animals like little worlds, not by deliberation, which belongs to art, the weak late imitator. Soul is master of bodies; whatever it makes it makes from what it has, and as fire warms, soul gives life, a living rational form, an image of its own life, and the forms of all things, gods too.

@p
Having heard it so, we must go back again to what is always so and take all as being at once: as the air, the light, the sun; or the moon, and again the light and the air, all together, but with an order of first, second and third; and here, soul always standing still, then the first things and those after, like the outermost parts of fire, with what is after the first thought of from the shadow of the last fire, and then this too lit up at the same time, so that a form runs, as it were, over what was set beneath, which came first and was wholly dim. It was ordered by rational form, by a soul that has the power in itself, through the whole of it, to order by rational forms; such as the forms in seeds, which shape and give form to living beings like little worlds. For whatever touches soul is made in such a way as the being of soul's nature has it.
!flag such as the air, light, the sun, and the moon. And, likewise, that we should again consider light and the sun | The Greek gives two images: air, light and sun, or moon, light and air; Taylor's "light and the sun" for the second is not quite it.

@p
@greek ἡ δὲ ποιεῖ οὐκ ἐπακτῷ γνώμῃ
And soul makes not by a purpose brought in from outside, nor waiting for counsel or inquiry; for then it would make not according to nature but by an art brought in. For art is later than soul and imitates it, making dim and weak imitations, toys of a kind, not worth much, using many devices to make images. But soul, by the power of its being, is master of bodies, so that they come to be and are as it leads them, since what came from the beginning cannot oppose its will. In later things, hindering one another, they are often deprived of getting their own shape, which the rational form in small wants; but there, since the whole shape too is produced by it, and what is produced has its order together with it, what comes to be is beautiful without toil and without hindrance. It made ready in it statues of gods, dwellings of men, and other things for others. For what should come to be from soul but what it has the power to make? Fire makes things warm, and another thing makes cold; but of soul, one thing goes from it into another and another stays in it.
!flag matter 1 is frequently deprived of the attainment of the appropriate form | The Greek has no "matter" here; the subject is the later things themselves, which hinder one another.

@p
@greek τοῖς μὲν γὰρ ἀψύχοις
In things without soul, what is from them sleeps, as it were, lying in them, and what goes to another is to make like itself what can be affected. And this is common to every being, to lead into likeness with itself; but soul's work is both what is in it, something awake, and what goes into another in the same way. So it makes the others live, those that do not live of themselves, with such a life as it lives itself. Living in rational form, it gives a rational form to the body, an image of what it has (for what it gives the body is an image of life), and the shapes of bodies, whose rational forms it has; and it has those of gods and of all things. That is why the world too has all things.

@ch IV.3.11 | Statues of the gods
The ancient sages, who made temples and statues wanting the gods to be present, saw that soul is easy to draw anywhere, and easiest to receive in something made to be in sympathy, like a mirror catching a form. Nature made everything in imitation of the forms, linking each to the god after whom it came to be. That god is intellect, the sun there; soul hangs from it and links the sun here to the sun there, as an interpreter between them. The heavenly gods are gods because they never stand apart from that one.

@p
And it seems to me that the wise men of old, who wanted gods to be present to them and made temples and statues, looking to the nature of the universe, grasped in their intellect that the nature of soul is everywhere easy to draw, and would be easiest of all to receive if one made something in sympathy, able to receive some portion of it. Whatever imitates in any way is in sympathy, like a mirror able to catch a form. For the nature of the universe too, which made everything skilfully in imitation of what it had the rational forms of, when each had come to be thus a rational form in matter, shaped after the one before matter, joined it to that god after whom it came to be, and to whom the soul looked and whom it had in making. And so it was not possible for it to be without a share in him, nor again for him to come down into it.
!flag But i. e. Of divine souls, each of which is a God according to participation. See the first book of my translation | Taylor's footnote is printed into his text in the scan.

@p
@greek ἦν δὲ νοῦς ἐκεῖνος
That one was intellect, the sun there (let this be our pattern for the argument), and next to it soul, hanging from it, staying as intellect stays. This soul gives its edges, those next to this sun, to this sun, and makes it joined to that one through itself, becoming a kind of interpreter of what comes from that one to this and of what goes from this to that, so far as it reaches that one through soul. For nothing is far or distant from anything, and again it is distant by difference and mixture; but it is possible to be by itself, not separate in place, and again to be with another while apart. These are gods by never standing off from those, and by being fastened to the soul from the beginning, the soul that, as it were, went away, and by this, by which they are what they are called, by looking to intellect, with their soul looking nowhere but there.
!note "Intellect, the sun there" recalls the Republic (508b-c), where the sun is the offspring of the Good.
!flag These, however, [i. e. the mundane spheres] are Gods, because they are never deserted by intellect and soul | The Greek says they are gods "by never standing off from those", the intelligible gods; that they are "never deserted by intellect and soul" is not quite it.

@ch IV.3.12 | Souls of men come down, heads above heaven
Seeing their images as Dionysus saw his in the mirror, men's souls rushed down, still not cut off from intellect: they reach the earth, but their head is set above heaven. Their middle part had to care for what they came to. Zeus, pitying their labours, made their bonds mortal and gave them rests free of bodies. The order of the whole joins descents and ascents in one harmony, signified by the stars; souls come down more or less, each into what is ready for it, by likeness.

@p
The souls of men, seeing their images, as Dionysus did in the mirror, came to be there, rushing from above, but not even they were cut off from their own principle and from intellect. For they did not come with intellect; they reached as far as the earth, but their head is set firm above the heaven. It befell them to come down further because their middle part was compelled to care for what they had reached, which needed care.
