But father Zeus, pitying them in their toil, made the bonds mortal about which they toil, and gives them rests at times, making them free of bodies, so that they too may come to be there, where the soul of the universe always is, not turning at all to the things here.
!note In the Orphic story the child Dionysus, looking into a mirror the Titans gave him, was seized and torn apart. The "head above the heaven" is from the Phaedrus (248a), and the "middle" of the soul perhaps from the Timaeus.
!flag in consequence of the first establish an image of herself in the body | The scan has a long footnote of Taylor's (from Olympiodorus, on Dionysus and the mirror) printed into the middle of his sentence, which goes on, after it, "nature into which it arrives requiring solicitous attention".

@p
@greek ὃ γὰρ ἔχει τὸ πᾶν
For what the universe has is already enough for it, and is and will be, brought to completion in times according to rational forms that always stand, and, by times, always restored to the same, in the measures of fixed lives; bringing these things into concord with those and after those, as these things are brought to completion, all ordered under one rational form, in the descents and ascents of souls and in everything else. The harmony of souls with the order of this universe bears witness too: they are not cut off, but join themselves in their descents and make one harmony with the circuit, so that their fortunes and lives and choices are signified by the figures of the stars, and give out, as it were, one voice in tune; and this is the more riddlingly meant by its being said "musically and harmoniously". This would not be unless the universe did and underwent each thing after those, in the measures of periods and orders and lives, by the kinds of passage that the souls go through, now there, now in the heaven, now turning to these places here.
!flag That the universe, likewise, utters as it were one voice harmonically and aptly, is asserted by the ancients more than any thing else | The Greek says it is the souls and the stars that give out one voice in tune, and that this is "riddled" in the musical language of the ancients.

@p
@greek νοῦς δὲ πᾶς ἀεὶ ἄνω
Every intellect is always above and will never come to be outside its own, but, settled whole above, sends to the things here through soul. Soul, from being near, is more disposed according to the form from there, and gives to what is under it, one soul always in the same way, another now one way, now another, holding its wandering in order. It comes down not always equally, but now more, now less, even if it goes to the same kind. Each comes down into what is ready for it, by likeness to its disposition; for it is carried to what it has become like there, one into a man, another into another animal.
!note The souls that hold "wandering in order" are the planets' (πλάνη, "wandering").

@ch IV.3.13 | The descent as a law of nature
What is inescapable, and justice, lie in a ruling nature: each goes in order to what it has become an image of. No one sends a soul down; at its time it goes as of itself, as if called by a herald, as if drawn by magic, as in an animal beard and horns come at their times, and trees bloom. It is neither willing nor sent: willing as a leap by nature, not by choice. The law is not outside; those who carry it fulfil it when the time comes, weighed down by it and longing to go where it calls.

@p
For the inescapable and justice lie in this way in a ruling nature: that each goes in order to what it has become, an image of a choice and of an archetypal disposition; and every form of soul is near that to which it has the disposition in itself; and there is no need of anyone to send it then and bring it in, neither so that it comes into a body then nor into this one; but when the time comes, it comes down as of itself and goes in where it must. Different souls have different times, and when it comes, as if a herald called, it comes down and sinks into the fitting body, so that one might liken what happens to being moved and carried by the powers of magicians and by strong drawings; just as in each one living thing the government of the animal is fulfilled, moving and producing each thing in time, as beards and the growth of horns, and impulses now towards this, and blossomings that were not there before; and the government of trees comes about at set terms.
!note "The inescapable" is Adrasteia, whose ordinance is told in the Phaedrus (248c).
!flag For justice, which is said to be inevitable | The Greek has "the inescapable and justice", two things, not "justice which is inevitable".
!flag And that whole form of the soul, is similar to that to which it has in itself a disposition, and which then sends and introduces it | The Greek says every form of soul is near that to which it is disposed, and that there is no need of anyone to send it: Taylor makes that very thing the sender.

@p
@greek ἴασι δὲ οὔτε ἑκοῦσαι
They go neither willing nor sent; or not willing in the sense of choosing, but as a leap by nature, or as to the natural eagerness for marriage, or as some are moved to fine deeds not by reasoning; but for such a soul such a thing is fated always, for one now, for another later. The intellect before the world has its fate, to stay there and to send as much as it does; and the particular, falling under the universal law, is sent. For the universal lies within each, and the law does not have its strength for fulfilment from outside, but is given to be in those who will use it and carry it about with them. And if the time comes, what it wants to come about comes about then by those who have it, so that they fulfil it themselves, since they carry it about and it has grown strong by being settled in them, weighing upon them, as it were, and putting into them an eagerness and a labour to go where the one within them, as it were, calls them to go.
!flag The destiny, indeed, of the intellect which is prior to the world, is to remain in the intelligible region, and from thence to impart something | Taylor is close. The Greek says the intellect before the world has as its fate to stay there "and to send as much as" it sends.

@ch IV.3.14 | Pandora and Prometheus
With souls coming down, the world has many lights and is adorned with gifts from the gods and the other minds. The myth riddles this: when Prometheus had shaped the woman, the other gods adorned her, each giving a gift, and she was named for all the gifts. Prometheus refusing the gift means that the choice of the intelligible is better; the maker is bound because he touches what he made, and Heracles frees him because he has the power.

@p
When this happens, this world, with many lights and shone upon by souls, is adorned further, beyond its first adornments, taking one world from one and another from another, from those gods and from the other minds that give souls. It is likely that the myth riddles this: when Prometheus had shaped the woman, the other gods adorned her too, and Aphrodite gave something, and the Graces, and each another gift, and they named her from the gift and from all the givers. For all gave to this thing shaped, which came about by a kind of forethought. Prometheus refusing their gift, what would it mean but that the choice of what is in the intelligible is better? But the maker himself is bound, because in a way he is in touch with what he made, and such a bond is from outside; and the freeing is by Heracles, because there is power in him, so that even so he is freed. Let anyone think of this as he likes; but it does show the giving to the world, and it agrees with what has been said.
!note The story is Hesiod's (Works and Days 60-82, Theogony 521-616). "Pandora" means "all gifts", and Prometheus' name means "forethought".
!flag It is likewise said, that he mingled earth with water* and inserted the human voice | Taylor adds details from Hesiod not in Plotinus' Greek. His long footnote from Olympiodorus on the myth is printed into his text in the scan.
!flag by Prometheus warning his brother Epimetbeus, not to accept the gift4 [Pandora] | The Greek says only "Prometheus refusing their gift". Taylor's footnote says Ficino mistook it.

@ch IV.3.15 | Down through the heavens
Souls peep out of the intelligible first into heaven, take a body there, and go on into earthier ones, as far as they stretch. Some go from body to body, too weighed with forgetfulness to rise. They differ by bodies, fortunes, upbringing, or of themselves. Some are wholly under the fate here, some at times, and some bear what must be borne but keep their own works their own, living by the other law that governs all beings.

@p
They go, peeping out of the intelligible, first into heaven, and taking a body there, they go on through it to earthier bodies, as far as they are stretched in length. Some go from heaven into lower bodies, others pass from some bodies into others: those whose power was not enough to lift them from here, because of the heaviness and forgetfulness they drag along, by which they were weighed down. They come to differ by the changes of the bodies they entered, or by fortunes, or by upbringing, or they bring the difference themselves from themselves, or by all of these or some. Some fall wholly under the fate here; some now so, now their own; some accept what must be endured, but are able to be their own in what are their own works, living by the other law-giving of all beings together, having given themselves to another ordinance. This is woven from all the rational forms and causes here, and from the motions of souls, and from the laws from there, agreeing with those, taking its principles from there, and weaving what follows together with them; keeping unshaken whatever can keep itself in the state of those, and leading the rest round as is their nature, so that the cause lies in those that came down, that some are placed here and others lie there.
!flag Souls, therefore, fall from the intelligible world | The Greek has "peeping out" (ἐκκύψασαι) of the intelligible, not "falling"; the word is Plato's, for souls that lift their heads above the heaven (Phaedrus 248a).

@ch IV.3.16 | Undeserved sufferings
Punishments of the wicked are justly put down to the order. What of evils that befall the good, without justice? Perhaps they come from past wrongs; or they are not from the leading rational forms but follow on them, like a man killed by a falling house; or the wrong is no evil to the sufferer and useful to the whole. Nothing is left loose: what is unjust for the doer is not unjust in the whole, and if the sufferer is good, it ends in good. The causes are hidden, and give the ignorant cause to blame.

@p
The punishments that come upon the wicked it is right to put down, with justice, to the order, as leading things as they should go. But what befalls the good outside justice, like punishments or poverty or diseases: must we say it comes from earlier wrongs? For these things are woven together and signified beforehand, so that they too come about by rational order. Or these are not by natural rational forms, nor were they among the leading things, but follow on them: as when a building falls, whoever is under it dies, whatever he is like; or when two things are carried along in order, or even one, what falls in its way is wounded or trampled. Or this injustice is no evil to the one who suffers it, and useful to the weaving of the whole. Or it is not unjust, having its justice from what went before. For we must not think that some things are ordered together and others left loose to their own power. If things must come about by causes and natural sequences, by one rational form and one order, we must think the smaller things too are ordered and woven together. And the unjust act of one against another is unjust for the doer, and the one who did it is not freed from blame; but, ordered in the universe, it is not unjust in it, nor for the one who suffered, but it had to be so. If the sufferer is good, these things end in good. For we must think this ordering is not godless, nor unjust, but exact in giving what is due; it has hidden causes, and gives those who do not know a cause for blame.
!flag may be said to take place through offences committed in a former life | The Greek asks whether we must say so ("ἆρα... λεκτέον"); Taylor asserts it, and the Greek has no "former life", only "earlier wrongs".

@ch IV.3.17 | Light from light, and the pilots
Souls go first into heaven because it is the best of the perceptible and borders on the intelligible; earth is last. All souls shine on heaven and give it most; those that go further down light up lower things, not to their own good. A centre, a circle of light round it, another light from light, then a sphere with no light of its own. Souls tending what they light up are like pilots in a storm who forget themselves and go down with the ship, held at last by the spell of caring for nature.

@p
That the souls go from the intelligible first into the region of heaven one might reason from such things as these. If heaven is the better in the perceptible region, it would border on the last of the intelligibles. So these things are ensouled first from there, and share, being more fitted to share. The earthy is last and less fitted by nature to share in soul, and far from the nature without body. All of them shine upon heaven and give it, as it were, most of themselves and the first part, and light up the rest with what is later; those going further down light up what is more below, but it is not better for them to go far. For there is something like a centre, and upon it a circle shining out from it, and upon these another, light from light; and outside these there is no longer another circle of light, but this one needs its own light for lack of light from another. Let this be a wheel, or rather a sphere of this kind, which takes its light from the third, being next to it, as much as that one is lit.
!flag That souls, however, first descend from < Conformably to this, it is divinely said by Plato in the Republic | Taylor's footnote on chapter 16, quoting the Republic (613a) on the just man, is printed into this sentence in the scan.
!flag of such a kind as to participate of the second of these circles | The Greek has "from the third"; and Taylor's "the celestial souls" and "below the heavens" earlier in the chapter are his own glosses.

@p
@greek τὸ μὲν οὖν μέγα φῶς
The great light, then, stays and shines in, and the brightness goes through from it by rational order; the others shine along with it, some staying, others drawn further on by the splendour of what is lit. Then, as what is lit needs more care, as pilots of ships in a storm put more and more of themselves into caring for the ships and without noticing forget themselves, so that they are often in danger of being dragged down with the wreck of the ships, these souls too leaned further and did not attend to their own; and then they were held down, fettered by the bonds of a spell, held by their care for nature. If each living being were like the universe, a complete and sufficient body in no danger of being affected, the soul said to be present would not be present to it, and would give it life staying wholly above.

@ch IV.3.18 | Do souls reason there, or speak?
Reasoning comes in here, where soul is in difficulty and full of care: needing it is a lessening of intellect, as craftsmen reason only when stuck. Souls there are rational because they could reason if needed, or if reasoning means the settled activity from intellect. Nor do they use voices in the intelligible, or even with bodies in heaven; they know each other's minds, as we know much from the eyes of the silent, for there every body is pure and each is like an eye. Daemons and souls in the air may well use voice.

@p
Does the soul use reasoning before it comes, and again when it has gone out? Or does reasoning come in here, when it is already in difficulty and filled with care and weaker? For to need reasoning is a lessening of intellect's sufficiency; as in the arts too reasoning is for craftsmen when they are stuck, but when it is not hard the art is master and does the work. But if they are there without reasonings, how would they still be rational? Because, one might say, they are able to think a matter through when they are at a loss in some situation. But we must take reasoning of this kind; for if one takes reasoning to be the disposition always coming from intellect and being in them, and a standing activity, and a kind of reflection, they would use reasoning there too.
!flag and if in the intelligible souls also use the reasoning power, yet as it appears to me, we must not think that voice is employed by them there | Taylor runs two sentences together. The Greek says that, if reasoning is a settled activity from intellect, souls would use it there too; then, as a new point, that we must not think they use voices.

@p
@greek οὐδὲ δὴ φωναῖς
Nor, I think, must we suppose they use voices, while they are in the intelligible, though they have bodies in heaven. And all the talking that is done here through need or in disputes would not be there; doing each thing in order and by nature, they would not give orders or advice, but would know what comes from one another by understanding. For even here we know much from the eyes of people who are silent; but there all body is pure, and each is like an eye, and nothing is hidden or pretended, but before one speaks to another, the other has seen and known. It is not absurd for daemons and souls in the air to use voice; for they are living beings of that kind.
!flag But when they have bodies in the heavens, theydo not use the dialect | The Greek says they do not use voices while in the intelligible, though they have bodies in heaven; Taylor's "but when they have bodies" divides what the Greek joins.

@ch IV.3.19 | Undivided and divided in one soul?
Are the undivided and the divided in soul mixed in the same thing, or is the divided a second part? Plato calls one simply undivided, the other "becoming divided about bodies". The perceiving, growing and desiring powers are divided as the body is; reasoning and intellect do not give themselves to body, which only hinders their work. So they are two, a whole of pure parts; yet the divided may get its undividedness from the power above, and so be both.

@p
Are the undivided and the divided in the same thing, as if mixed; or is the undivided in one place and by one thing, and the divided next after it, another part of the soul, as we say the reasoning part is one thing and the unreasoning another? It may be known when we have grasped what we mean by each. He has spoken of the undivided simply, and of the divided not simply, but he says it "becomes divided about bodies", not that it has become so. We must look at the nature of body, what kind of soul it needs for living, and what of soul must be present to the body everywhere and to the whole. All the perceiving power, if it will perceive through the whole, comes to be divided: being everywhere, it might be said to be divided; but appearing whole everywhere, it might be said not to be divided altogether, but to become divided about bodies.
!flag and that the soul is not on this account generated | Taylor misreads. The Greek says Plato speaks of soul as "becoming divided about bodies", not as having become divided.

@p
@greek εἰ δέ τις λέγοι ἐν ταῖς ἄλλαις
If someone says it is not divided in the other senses, but only in touch, we must say it is in the others too; for if what shares is a body, it must be divided so, but less than in touch. And the growing part and the increasing part likewise. And if desire is about the liver and spirit about the heart, the same account holds for these too. But perhaps he did not take these into that mixture; perhaps they come in another way, from one of the things taken in. But reasoning and intellect no longer give themselves to body; for their work is not accomplished through an organ of the body, which is a hindrance if one uses it in inquiries. So the undivided and the divided are different, and not mixed as one, but as a whole from parts, each pure and separate in power. If, however, what becomes divided about bodies has its undividedness from the power above, this same thing can be undivided and divided, as if mixed from itself and the power that came into it from above.
!note Desire about the liver and spirit about the heart is the Timaeus (70a-71b).

@ch IV.3.20 | Soul is not in body as in a place
Is soul, or any part of it, in a place? If no part has a place, the body is soulless; if some do and some not, not all our soul is in us. Soul is not in body as in a place, which holds a body part by part; nor as in a vessel; nor as in an empty extension; nor as an attribute in a subject; nor as a part in a whole or a whole in its parts; nor as a form in matter. We say it is in body because body is seen and soul not. If soul were seen, we would say the body is in it.

@p
Whether these and the other so-called parts of the soul are in a place, or these not at all, and the others in a place and where, or none at all, is right to consider. For if we do not mark off a place for each of the parts of the soul, putting none anywhere and making them no more inside the body than outside, we shall make it soulless, and shall be at a loss where to say the works done through bodily organs come about. If we give place to some and not to others, we shall seem not to have in us those we do not give it to, so that not all our soul is in us. In general, then, we must say that no part of the soul, nor the whole, is in the body as in a place. For place is what holds around, and holds a body; and where each thing divided is, there it is, so that the whole is not in any one place. But soul is not a body, and is no more held than holding.
!flag In short, there* fore, we must neither assert that any one of the parts of the soul, nor that the whole of it is in body | The Greek adds "as in a place" (ὡς ἐν τόπῳ): soul is in body, but not in that way.

@p
@greek οὐ μὴν οὐδ’ ὡς ἐν ἀγγείῳ
Nor is it as in a vessel; for the body would come to be soulless, whether it held soul as a vessel or as a place holds; unless soul, gathered to itself, were in it by some passing on, and what the vessel took would be lost to it. Place, strictly, is without body and is not a body; so why would it need soul? And the body would come near soul by its own limit, not by itself. Many other things would oppose its being as in a place. The place would always be carried along with it, and there would be something itself carrying the place around. But even if place were an extension, still less would soul be in the body as in a place; for an extension must be empty, but the body is not empty; but perhaps that in which the body is will be, so that the body is in the empty. Nor will it be in the body as in a subject; for what is in a subject is an affection of that in which it is, like colour and shape; and soul is separable. Nor as a part in a whole; for soul is not a part of the body.
!note "Place" as the limit of the containing body is Aristotle's (Physics IV.4, 212a); "interval" (διάστημα) as place is the view of others he reviews.

@p
@greek εἰ δέ τις λέγοι, ὡς ἐν ὅλῳ μέρος
If someone says it is as a part in the whole living being, first the same difficulty would remain, how it is in the whole: not as wine in a wine-jar, or as the jar, nor as something will be in itself. Nor as a whole in its parts; it is laughable to call the soul the whole and the body parts. Nor as form in matter; for form in matter cannot be separated, and the form comes after matter is there. But soul makes the form in matter, being other than the form. If they will say it is not the form that comes to be, but the form that is separated, how this form is in the body is not yet plain; "and soul is separable". How, then, is soul said by everyone to be in the body? Because soul is not visible, but the body is. Seeing a body, then, and understanding it to be ensouled because it moves and perceives, we say it has a soul. So we would naturally say that the soul is in the body. But if soul were visible and perceptible, embracing it wholly with life and going equally as far as the extremes, we would not say that the soul is in the body, but that what is not such is in what is more sovereign, what is held together in what holds it together, and what flows in what does not flow.
!flag or as a vessel in a vessel | The Greek has only "or as the jar": not as wine is in a wine-jar, nor as the jar itself is (in its place).

@ch IV.3.21 | Not like a pilot in a ship
How, then, is soul present? The pilot in a ship is well said for its separability, but the pilot is not in the whole ship. Perhaps as the steering skill would be in the rudder, if the rudder had a soul: a pilot gone into the rudder, as soul is in body as in a natural instrument. That is another way, but still we must go nearer.

@p
What, then? If someone asks how it is present, saying nothing himself about how, what shall we say? And whether all of it in a like way, or one part one way and another another? Since none of the ways of being in something now spoken of seems to fit the soul's relation to the body, it is said that soul is in the body as the pilot is in the ship. This is well said, so far as soul can be separate; but it would not show at all the way we are now looking for. As a passenger he would be in it incidentally; but as a pilot, how? For he is not in the whole ship, as the soul is in the body.
!note The pilot in the ship is from Aristotle (On the Soul II.1, 413a), who leaves it as an open question.
!flag For the pilot, so far as he is a sailor, will be from accident a pilot in the ship | The Greek says "as a passenger" (πλωτήρ) he would be in the ship incidentally; it is as pilot that the question arises.

@p
@greek ἀλλ’ ἆρα οὕτω φατέον
Must we say, then, that it is as the art is in the instruments, as in the rudder, if the rudder were ensouled, so that the pilot's skill moving it with art were inside? But as it is, the difference is that the art is outside. If, then, following the pattern of the pilot gone into the rudder, we set down that soul is in the body as in a natural instrument (for it moves it so in whatever it wants to do), would we get any further with what we are looking for? Or again we shall be at a loss how it is in the instrument, though this way is other than the earlier ones; but still we long to find it out and come closer.
!flag Shall we, therefore, | Taylor's chapter, as the scan gives it, breaks off here; the rest of his chapter is missing.

@ch IV.3.22 | As light is present to air
Perhaps soul is present to body as fire, or light, is present to air: present without being mixed, staying while the air flows past. So it is more right to say the air is in the light than the light in the air, as Plato set the body of the world in its soul. Only the powers the body needs are present to it, and not settled in its parts: the perceiving power to all that perceives, and different powers for different works.

@p
Must we say, then, that when soul is present to body it is present as fire is present to the air? For this too, present, is not present, and present through the whole is mixed with nothing; and it stays itself, while the air flows past; and when the air comes to be outside where the light is, it goes away having nothing, but while it is under the light, it is lit; so that here too it is right to say that the air is in the light rather than the light in the air. That is why Plato did well not to set the soul in the body, in the case of the universe, but the body in the soul; and he says that there is something of the soul in which the body is, and something in which there is no body: plainly the powers of soul that the body does not need. And the same account holds for the other souls.
!note Plato sets the body of the world inside its soul in the Timaeus (36d-e).
!flag it is present in the same manner as light is with the air | The Greek has "fire", by which Plotinus means its light, as the next words show; Taylor writes "light" from the first.

@p
@greek τῶν μὲν οὖν ἄλλων δυνάμεων
Of the other powers, then, we must not even say that there is a presence of the soul to the body; those it needs are present, and present not settled in its parts, nor again in the whole; for perceiving, the perceiving power is present to all that perceives, but for its activities, then, one is present to one part and another to another. I mean this:

@ch IV.3.23 | Where the powers are said to be
The ensouled body is lit by soul, and each part takes the power fitting its organ: sight in the eyes, hearing in the ears, touch everywhere. The nerves start from the brain, so the ancients put the source of perception and impulse there: not the power, which is everywhere, but the start of its activity. Reason sits above the perceiving and imagining soul, which borders on it, so they set it in the head. Desire is at the liver, where blood and growth begin; spirit at the heart, the spring of the fine, hot blood.

@p
When the ensouled body is lit by soul, each part of it shares in it differently; and according to the organ's fitness for the work, soul gives the power fitting the work, and so the power in the eyes is said to be the power of sight, that in the ears of hearing, that of taste in the tongue, smell in the nostrils, and the power of touch present in the whole; for for this perception the whole body is present to the soul as an organ. Since the organs of touch are first in the nerves, which also have the power for the motion of the living being, such a power giving itself there; and since the nerves begin from the brain, they put the principle of perception and impulse, and of the whole living being in general, there, setting what will use the organs where, plainly, the beginnings of the organs are. It is better to say that the beginning of the activity of the power is there; for where the instrument was to be moved from, there the craftsman's power fitting the instrument had, as it were, to set itself firmly; or rather not the power, for the power is everywhere, but the beginning of the activity is there, where the beginning of the instrument is.
!flag this power imparts itself from the nerves | The Greek says the power gives itself "there", in the nerves, not that it goes out from them.

@p
@greek ἐπεὶ οὖν ἡ τοῦ αἰσθάνεσθαι δύναμις
Since, then, the power of perceiving and that of impulse, belonging to the soul that is of a perceiving and imagining nature, had reason above them, as bordering on what is below that above which it is, in this way the ancients set it at the top of the whole living being, at the head; not as being in the brain, but as in this perceiving part, which was settled in the brain. For one had to give it to body, and to what in the body most receives its activity, while the other, sharing in body nowhere, had to share in that, which was a form of soul, and of a soul able to take in what comes from reason. For the perceiving power is in a way a judging one, and the imagining power is as it were intellectual, and impulse and desire follow on imagination and reason. So the reasoning part is there not as in a place, but because what is there enjoys it. How what is there is so, in the case of the perceiving part, has been said. And as the growing, increasing and feeding power is lacking nowhere, and feeds by the blood, and the blood that feeds is in the veins, and the beginning of the veins and of the blood is in the liver, it was assigned for the desiring portion of the soul to live there, as the power is fixed there. For what produces, feeds and makes grow must also desire these things. And since the thin, light, quick and pure blood is an organ fitted for spirit, its spring (for such blood is separated out there) has been made a fitting dwelling for the boiling of spirit.
!note The places are those of the Timaeus (44d, 69d-71b); "the boiling of spirit" is from 70b.
!flag where this principle is in the supreme part, there reason w?as placed by the ancients in the summit of the whole animal | The Greek places the perceiving power in the head, "bordering on" the reason above it; Taylor's "reason was placed in the head" makes reason the thing placed.

@ch IV.3.24 | Where the soul goes after death
Out of the body, soul goes where there is something to receive it, or to a body drawn by something unreasoning in it. Its disposition and the justice in things send it to its place; no one escapes, for the divine law carries out its own judgment, and the wrongdoer, carried unknowing, falls at last where he must, by a willing motion into unwilling suffering. Pure souls, having no body, are where being and the divine are, in God. Ask where those are, and not with the eyes.

@p
But where will it come to be when it has gone out of the body? It will not be here, where there is nothing at all to receive it, nor can it stay with what is not made by nature to receive it, unless it has something of it that draws it, being without sense. It is in that, if it has another, and follows where it is the nature of that to be and come to be. As there are many places, and each, the difference must come from the disposition, and from the justice in beings too. No one will ever escape what he ought to suffer for unjust deeds; for the divine law is inescapable, having in itself at once the carrying out of what has been judged. The one who suffers is himself carried, not knowing, to what he ought to suffer, tossed about everywhere in his wanderings by an unsteady course, and at last, as if worn out by what he strained against, he falls into the place that is fitting for him, having by a willing course what he does not will to suffer. It is told in the law how much, and how long, he must suffer, and again there come together the slackening of the punishment and the power of escaping from those places, by the power of the harmony that holds all things.
!flag unless the recipient has something of an unwise and insane nature which attracts the soul to it | The Greek says it is the soul that is "without sense" (ἄφρονα) when something of the body draws it.

@p
@greek ἔχουσαι δὲ σῶμα
Those that have a body have also the perceiving of bodily punishments; but to the souls that are pure, drawing nothing of the body to themselves in any way, it will necessarily belong not to be anywhere in body either. If, then, they are nowhere in body (for they have no body), where being is, and what is, and the divine, in God, there and with these and in this such a soul will be. If you still ask where, you must ask where those are; but in asking, do not ask with the eyes, nor as one asking for bodies.
!flag Souls, however, still having a body undergo corporeal punishments | The scan prints this sentence, which belongs later in the chapter, at its head as well.

@ch IV.3.25 | What in us remembers?
Do souls that leave remember, and what, and for how long? First ask what remembers. Memory is of something acquired, so not of God, being or intellect, which are in eternity, with no before and after; intellect knows the changes of the world without remembering, and does not remember its own thoughts, which never came or go. When the ancients spoke of soul's remembering and recollection, they meant its acting on what it has, a memory without time. Is memory the higher soul's, a lower one's, or the compound's?

@p
About memory: whether the souls themselves, when they have gone out of these places, can remember, or some can and some not, and all things or some, and whether they remember always or for some time near their going away, it is worth inquiring likewise. But if we are to make the inquiry rightly, we must grasp what it is that remembers. I mean not what memory is, but in which of the beings it is in its nature to be found. What memory is has been said elsewhere and often gone over; what is by nature able to remember must be grasped more exactly. If memory is of something acquired, a lesson or an experience, remembering would not come to be in the beings that are not affected, nor in those not in time. So memory must not be set about God, nor about being and intellect; for nothing comes to them, nor time, but eternity is about being, and there is neither before nor after, but it is always as it is in the same, not admitting change. How could what is in the same and like state come to be in memory, not having or getting another state after the one it had before, or one thought after another, so as to be in one and remember another it had before?
!note What memory is was discussed by Aristotle in On Memory, and by Plotinus himself perhaps in IV.6, On Perception and Memory.

@p
@greek ἀλλὰ τί κωλύει τὰς ἄλλων
But what prevents it from knowing the changes of other things, not changing itself, such as the periods of the world? Because it would think one thing earlier and another later, following the changes of what turns, and remembering is something other than thinking. It must not be said to remember its own thoughts; for they did not come, that it should hold them fast lest they go; or in that way it would fear lest its own being went away from it. Nor, then, must we say that the soul remembers in the way we say remembering is of what it has inborn; but since it is here, it has them and is not active in accordance with them, especially when it has come here. To the being active as well, in those that are active by what they had, the ancients seem to apply memory and recollection: so this is another kind of memory. That is why time does not attach to memory said in this way.
!flag Shall we say it is because it intellectually perceives one thing as prior, but another as posterior | Taylor makes a question of the answer: the Greek says intellect would have to think one thing earlier and another later, following the changes, and remembering is other than thinking.
!note Plato's "recollection" (ἀνάμνησις) is the soul's coming to know again what it knew before birth (Meno 81c-d, Phaedo 72e).

@p
@greek ἀλλ’ ἴσως εὐχερῶς
But perhaps we are taking this lightly and without examination. For perhaps someone might be puzzled whether such so-called recollection and memory belong not to that soul but to another, dimmer one, or to the compound of the two. If to another, when or how did it get them? If to the living being, when or how? So we must ask which of the things in us has memory, which is what we were asking from the beginning; and if it is the soul that remembers, which power or which part; if the living being, which some have thought is also what perceives, what the manner is, and what we must call the living being; and further whether we must set down that the same thing grasps what is perceived and what is thought, or one the one and another the other.

@ch IV.3.26 | Memory belongs to soul, not body
Perceiving is shared, as boring or weaving is, soul the craftsman and body the tool; but memory need not be, since soul keeps or lets go of the impression. Body only helps or hinders. The living being is not a third thing in which soul is potential; if it were, memory would still be soul's, as sweetness in mead is from the honey. Impressions are not like seals in wax, and soul remembers its own desires and thoughts, which never reached the body. Body, flowing, causes forgetting: that is the river of Lethe.

@p
If, then, it is the compound that is in perceptions in act, perceiving must be of this kind. That is why it is called common, like boring and weaving, so that the soul in perceiving is as the craftsman and the body as the tool, the body being affected and serving, the soul receiving the imprint of the body, or what comes through the body, or the judgment it formed from the body's affection. Of this, perception might be called a common work in this way; but memory would not be compelled to belong to the common thing, since the soul has already taken in the imprint and kept it or thrown it away; unless one were to infer that remembering too is common, from our coming to have good memories or to be forgetful by the blends of our bodies. But even so the body might be said to be a hindrance or not a hindrance, and remembering would be no less the soul's. And with things learned, how will the common thing, and not the soul, be what remembers?
!note "Common" to soul and body is Aristotle's term (On Sense 436a, On Memory 450a).

@p
@greek εἰ δὲ τὸ ζῷον συναμφότερον
If the living being is the compound in such a way that it is something different from both, first it is absurd to call the living being neither body nor soul. For it will not be something different when both have changed, nor again when they are blended, so that the soul is in the living being potentially; for even so remembering will be no less the soul's, as in a blend of wine and honey, if anything is sweet, it will be from the honey. What, then, if soul does not remember itself, but by being in body, by not being pure but as it were given quality, can take the imprints of what is perceived, and, by having a kind of seat in the body, can receive them and not let them, as it were, flow past? But first, the imprints are not magnitudes, nor like seal-impressions, nor resistances or stampings, because there is no pushing, nor as in wax; but the manner is like thinking, even with what is perceived. And with thoughts, what resistance could be spoken of? Or what need of a body, or of bodily quality along with it?
!flag nor again, both being mingled, will the soul be in the animal in capacity only ; though even in this case | Taylor reverses the sense: the Greek says that even if they are blended so that the soul is in the living being potentially, memory will still be the soul's.
!flag she is able to impress in the body the types of sensibles | The Greek says soul can "take the imprint" (ἀναμάττεσθαι) of what is perceived, not "impress" it in the body.

@p
@greek ἀλλὰ μὴν καὶ τῶν αὐτῆς κινημάτων
Moreover, soul must remember its own motions too, such as what it desired and did not enjoy, where the object of desire never came into the body. How could the body speak of what never came into it? Or how will soul remember with the body what the body is by nature quite unable to know? We must say that some things end in the soul, those that come through the body, and others are the soul's alone, if the soul is to be something and have a nature and a work of its own. If so, it has longing, and memory of the longing, and of getting and not getting, since its nature is not among the things that flow. If not, we shall grant it neither awareness of itself, nor following along, nor any putting together and, as it were, understanding. For it does not get these in bodies without having any of them in its nature; it has some activities whose works need organs to be carried out, and of some it comes bringing the powers, of others the activities too. And memory has the body as a hindrance; even now, when some things are added there is forgetting, and with taking away and purification memory often comes up again. Since memory is a staying, the nature of the body, moving and flowing, must be the cause of forgetting, not of memory. That is how the river of Lethe might be understood. Let this experience, then, be the soul's.
!note Lethe, "forgetting", is the river the souls drink from before birth in the myth of Er (Republic 621a).
!flag nor a power of following the conceptions of intellect | The Greek has "following along" (παρακολούθησις), awareness of what one is doing; "the conceptions of intellect" is Taylor's own.

@ch IV.3.27 | Which soul? The shade of Heracles
Each soul, the more divine one that we are and the other from the whole, has its memories, some its own, some shared. Apart, each keeps its own longer and the other's briefly. Heracles' shade in Hades remembers all the deeds of his life, since the life was chiefly its; Heracles himself, what he said is not told. The soul freed from body remembers this life, and with time earlier lives too, letting some go; in another body it forgets much. But first: which power remembers?

@p
But which soul's: the one we call more divine, by which we are we, or the other, from the whole? We must say there are memories of each, some private and some shared; and when they are together, all at once; when they come to be apart, if both should be and remain, each keeps its own for longer, and the other's for a short time. The shade of Heracles in Hades, at any rate (for I think we must take this to be the shade), remembers all that was done in his life; for the life was above all its. The other souls, being the compound, still had nothing more to say than the things of this life; for, having become the compound, they knew these; or anything having to do with justice. What Heracles himself, without the shade, said, is not told. What, then, would the other soul say, freed and alone?
!note In the Odyssey (11.601-604) Odysseus sees "the image of Heracles" in Hades, while "he himself" is with the immortal gods.
!flag The image, therefore, of Hercules, is in Hades.1 | Taylor makes a statement of what in the Greek begins an example: the shade of Heracles "remembers" all his deeds.

@p
@greek ἡ γὰρ ἐφελκομένη
The soul that drags something with it would tell all that the man did or suffered; but as time goes on after death, memories of other things would appear too, from earlier lives, so that it may even dismiss some of these, thinking little of them. For, become purer of body, it will go over again things it did not have in memory here. If it goes out, having come into another body, it will tell the things of the life outside, and will tell what it has just left, and many of the earlier things. But in time it will always be forgetting many things that were brought in. And the soul that has come to be alone, what will it remember? Or first we must consider by which power of the soul remembering comes about.
!flag But wrhen she becomes situated in another body, she will then indeed departing [from an intellectual] speak of the concerns of an external life | The Greek says if it goes out, having been in another body, it will tell of the life outside (the body it left) and of many earlier things. Taylor's bracket and his "superior life" are not there.

@ch IV.3.28 | Not the desiring or spirited part
Is it the power by which we perceive and learn, or the desiring and spirited parts, since what enjoys should remember? The desiring part is stirred again by what it enjoyed, but that is not memory: sight sees and desire is moved by passing on, without knowing what moved it, as a dog stirs at the scent of a wolf the shepherd has seen. The desiring part keeps a trace as a disposition; something else saw the enjoyment and remembers it, since memory often does not know what desire shared in.

@p
Is it that by which we perceive and by which we learn? Or is it also that by which we desire the things desired, and the things that anger us, by the spirited part? For it is not one thing that will enjoy, someone will say, and another that will remember what that one enjoyed. The desiring part, at least, is moved again by what it enjoyed, when the object of desire is seen, plainly by memory. Otherwise why not by something else, or not in this way? What, then, prevents giving it perception of such things too, and to the perceiving part desire, and altogether everything, so that each is named by what dominates? Or perception belongs to each in a different way: sight saw, not the desiring part, and the desiring part was moved by the perception as by passing on, not so as to say what kind the perception was, but so as to be affected without following it. And with spirit, sight saw the wrongdoer, and spirit rose up; as when a shepherd sees a wolf at the flock and the dog is roused by the smell or the noise, though it has not seen with its eyes.
!flag And why may we not, therefore, attribute desire to the sensitive power | Taylor is close; the Greek joins them, giving perception to the desiring part and desire to the perceiving, and everything to everything.

@p
@greek καὶ τοίνυν ἀπέλαυσε μὲν
So the desiring part enjoyed, and has a trace of what happened from it, not as memory but as a disposition and an affection; but what saw the enjoyment and has the memory of what happened in itself is another. The evidence is that memory often does not know the things the desiring part shared in, though, if it were in it, it would.
!flag though they still reside in it | Taylor misreads. The Greek says memory, "if it were in" the desiring part, would know them.

@ch IV.3.29 | Memory belongs to imagination
Is memory the perceiving power's? But then, if the shade remembers, there would be two perceiving powers; and the same people are not best at perceiving and at remembering. Rather, what is perceived becomes an image for the imagining power, where perception ends; it keeps the sight when the thing is gone, and that is remembering. A strong imagination remembers long. People differ in memory by their powers, by practice, and by bodily blends.

@p
Shall we, then, assign memory to the perceiving part, and will what remembers and what perceives be the same for us? But if the shade too remembers, as was said, the perceiving part will be twofold; and if what remembers is not the perceiving part but something else, what remembers will be twofold. Further, if the perceiving part is of what is learned too, the perceiving part will be of what is thought too. Or there must be another for each. Shall we, then, make the grasping power common and give it the memory of both? If what grasps perceptible and intelligible things is one and the same, perhaps something would be said; but if it is divided in two, there would none the less be two. And if we give both to each soul, there would be four. In general, what need is there that we remember by what we perceive, and that both come about by the same power, and that by what we think we remember our thoughts? For the same people are not best at thinking and at remembering, and those who perceive equally do not remember equally, and some perceive well, while others remember who are not sharp in perception.

@p
@greek ἀλλὰ πάλιν αὖ
But again, if each must be a different thing, and a different thing will remember what perception perceived before, that too must perceive what it is to remember. Or nothing will prevent what is perceived from being an image for what will remember, and memory and holding from belonging to the imagining power, being another thing; for this is where perception ends, and when it is no longer there, the sight is present to this. If, then, the image of what is already absent is with it, it is already remembering; and if it stays a little while, the memory is little, and if long, people have better memories, this power being stronger, so that, not easily turned, it does not let the memory be shaken off. Memory, then, belongs to the imagining power, and remembering will be of such things. We shall say people differ in memory either by the powers themselves differing, or by practice or not, or by bodily blends being present or not, and altering or not, and as it were disturbing. But that elsewhere.
!flag which is something different from memory | The Greek says the imagining power is another thing than perception, not "different from memory".

@ch IV.3.30 | Memory of thoughts
What of the memory of thoughts? If an image goes with every thought, memory would be of that. Or rather the thought, partless and within, is unnoticed until a verbal expression unfolds it into the imagining power, which shows it as in a mirror; then we grasp it, keep it and remember it. So we are always thinking, but do not always grasp that we are, because what receives receives perceptions as well, from the other side.

@p
What of the memory of thoughts? Is the imagining power for these too? If an image goes along with every thought, perhaps, this image staying, as an image of the thought, there would in this way be memory of what was known; if not, something else must be looked for. Perhaps it would be the reception of the verbal expression that goes along with the thought into the imagining power. For the thought is partless, and, not yet come out, as it were, into the open, being within, it goes unnoticed; but the verbal expression, unfolding it and leading it from the thought into the imagining power, has shown the thought as in a mirror, and so comes the grasp of it, its staying and its memory. That is why, though the soul is always moving towards thinking, we grasp it when it comes to be in this. For thinking is one thing and the grasping of thinking another, and we are always thinking, but do not always grasp it; this is because what receives receives not only thoughts but also perceptions, from the other side.
!flag Perhaps, however, memory will be a reception into the phantastic power of reason following the conception | Taylor's "reason" is λόγος here in its sense of the words or verbal expression that goes with a thought.

@ch IV.3.31 | Two imaginations
If memory is imagination's and both souls remember, there are two imaginations. In us, when they agree, the better rules and one image comes about, the other following like a shadow, a small light under a greater; when they conflict, the lower shows itself, and we notice the doubleness of soul. Going out, the higher keeps some memories and lets go of the other's, as we remember little of poor company once we have found better.

@p
But if memory belongs to the imagining power, and each soul has been said to remember, there are two imagining powers. Apart, let each have its own; but in the same thing, in us, how are there two, and in which of them does memory come about? If in both, the images will always be double; for it is not that one soul's is of intelligibles and the other's of perceptibles; for so there would be two living beings with nothing in common with each other. If in both, then, what is the difference? And then how do we not know it? When the one agrees with the other, the imagining powers not even being apart, and the better one's ruling, the image becomes one, as if a shadow followed the other, and as if a small light ran under a greater. But when there is a fight and discord, the other too shows itself on its own; it goes unnoticed in the other, because the doubleness of souls goes unnoticed altogether. For both came into one, and the one rides upon the other. The one, then, saw everything, and when it goes out keeps some things and lets go some of what belongs to the other; as when we have taken up with poorer companions, and at some time changed them for others, we remember little of theirs, but more when they have been better.
!flag For both coalesce in one, and the one is diffused but not the other | The Greek says the one soul "rides upon" (ἐποχεῖται) the other, not that it is "diffused".
!flag possesses some things indeed, proceeding from it, but dismisses others, as pertaining to the other power | The Greek says the higher soul, when it goes out (ἐξελθοῦσα), keeps some things and lets go of some of the other's.

@ch IV.3.32 | The good soul forgets
What of friends, children, wife and country? The lower soul remembers them with feeling, the good soul without. The lower longs to remember the higher's doings; the higher gladly forgets the lower's. The more it hastens upward, the more it forgets, unless its whole life here was such as to remember only the better. So the good soul is rightly called forgetful: it flees the many and gathers them into one. Heracles' shade may tell his brave deeds; the one who has gone to the intelligible, stronger than Heracles in the contests of the wise, what will he remember?

@p
What, then, of friends and children and wife? Of country, and the things it is not absurd for even a good man to remember? One remembers each of these with feeling, the other would have their memories without feeling; for the feeling was perhaps there from the beginning in that one, and the decent feelings in the good soul, as far as it had some fellowship with the other. It is fitting that the worse should long for memory of the other's activities too, especially when it is itself decent; for one might be better from the beginning and by the education from the better. But the better should be willing to forget gladly what comes from the worse. For it may be that, while one soul is good, the other is worse by nature, held down by force by the first. So the more it hastens upward, the more it forgets, unless its whole life even here was such that its memories were of the better things only; for here too it is well to stand away from human concerns, and so of necessity from the memories of them; so that whoever calls the good soul forgetful, in such a way, would speak rightly.
!flag [and this is the work of perfect virtue *] | Taylor's words in square brackets are his own.
!flag A forgetfulness, therefore, of such pursuits, is necessary in another life | The Greek says only that standing away from human concerns means standing away from their memories too; "in another life" is Taylor's.

@p
@greek ἐπεὶ καὶ φεύγει ἐκ τῶν πολλῶν
For it flees from the many, and gathers the many into one, letting go of the unlimited. So it is not with many, but light and by itself; for even here, when it wants to be there, while still here it lets go of all that is other; so there it has little from here, and more when it is in heaven. That Heracles might speak of his brave deeds; but the one who thinks these small, and has been moved to a holier place, and has come to be in the intelligible, strong in the contests the wise contend in, beyond Heracles, what will he remember?
!flag And Hercules, indeed, [when in Hades] may speak of his own fortitude ; but in the intelligible world, he will consider these things as trifling | The Greek has two figures: "that Heracles", the shade, tells his deeds; "the other", Heracles himself in the intelligible, thinks them small. Taylor makes them one, and leaves out the closing question, "what will he remember?"

@treatise IV.7 | On the immortality of the soul
Is each of us immortal? A man is soul and body, and the body, being composite, perishes; the man himself is the soul. Most of the treatise argues, against the Stoics and the atomists, that the soul is not a body: no body has life of itself, nor can a heap of bodies make it; perceiving needs one undivided thing that takes in the whole, and pain in the finger is felt by what is the same everywhere; thinking grasps what has no size; virtue, the beautiful and justice are not bodies. Nor is the soul a harmony, as the Pythagoreans said, nor an entelechy, as Aristotle said: it is a being, and what is is from itself and lives of itself. Being of the divine and everlasting kind, as its love of wisdom shows, it is immortal; and so are all souls, even those of animals and plants.

@ch IV.7.1 | Man is soul and body; the soul is the man
Are we immortal, or do we perish whole, or does part of us go and part stay? Man is not simple: he has a soul and a body. The body is composite, and both reason and perception show it coming apart; even simple bodies have form and matter and can be cut. So if the body is a part of us we are not wholly immortal; if it is a tool, it was given for a time. What is most sovereign, the man himself, is the soul, whether as form to matter or as user to tool.

@p
Whether each of us is immortal, or perishes whole, or part of him goes off to scattering and destruction while the rest remains for ever, which is he himself, one might learn by looking into it according to nature. A man would not be something simple: there is a soul in him, and he has a body too, whether it is our tool or fastened to us in some other way. Let it be divided in this way, and let us study the nature and being of each. The body, being itself composite, cannot by reason remain, and perception sees it dissolving and wasting and taking every kind of destruction, as each of the things in it goes off to its own, and one destroys another and changes and ruins it into something else, especially when the soul that makes them friends is not present to the masses. And even if each is left alone, it is not one, since it can be dissolved into shape and matter, from which even the simple bodies must have their make-up. Moreover, having magnitude, as bodies, they can be cut and broken into small pieces, and in this way would undergo destruction. So if this is a part of us, we are not wholly immortal; if a tool, it had to be of this nature, since it was given for some time. But the most sovereign part, the man himself, would be, as form to matter, or as user to tool; in either way the soul is he.
!flag Whether each [part] of us is immortal | Taylor's bracket is wrong: the Greek asks whether each of us is immortal.
!flag That, however, which is the most principal thing, and the man himself, will be that with reference to the body which form is with reference to matter, since this according to form is as body to matter | Taylor's words "since this according to form is as body to matter" seem to come from a variant; the Greek says only that the man is the soul, as form to matter, "the body".
!note "Form to matter" is Aristotle's account of soul (On the Soul II.1); the "tool" or "instrument" is Plato's (Alcibiades I 129c-130a).

@ch IV.7.2 | No body has life of itself
If the soul is a body, it can be broken up. Into what? If into bodies, either one has life of itself, and that is soul, or none does. Fire, air, water and earth have no life of their own, nor do the elements before them. A coming together of lifeless bodies cannot make life, nor mindless things mind; and a mixture needs something to order it, which has the rank of soul. Even a simple body needs a rational form coming to matter, and that can come only from soul.

@p
What nature, then, has this? If it is a body, it can be wholly broken up, for every body is composite. If it is not a body but of another nature, that nature too must be considered, in the same way or another. But first we must consider into what we must break up this body that they call soul. Since life is necessarily present to soul, this body, the soul, if it is of two bodies or more, must either have life inborn in each, or in one and not the other, or in neither. If life belongs to one of them, this would be the soul. What body, then, would have life of itself? Fire and air and water and earth are soulless of themselves, and whatever of them has soul present uses a life brought in from outside; and there are no other bodies besides these. Those who think there are elements other than these said they were bodies, not souls, and did not have life.
!note The other elements are the atoms of Democritus and Epicurus.
!flag And those to whom it appears that there are other bodies the elements of these, do not assert that they are souls | Taylor is close; the Greek says those who hold there are other elements "said they were bodies, not souls".

@p
@greek εἰ δὲ μηδενὸς αὐτῶν ζωὴν
If, when none of them has life, their coming together has made life, that is absurd; or rather it is impossible that a heap of bodies should produce life, and mindless things give birth to intellect. And besides, they will not say these come about however they are blended. There must, then, be what orders and is the cause of the blending, so that this would have the rank of soul. For not only would there be no composite body, but not even a simple body would there be among beings without soul in the universe, if it is a rational form coming to matter that makes a body; and a rational form could come from nowhere but soul.
!flag And if each of them has life, one will be sufficient. | These words, which the scan gives, are not in the Greek.
!flag For that which is compounded will not be that which arranges and produces the mixture | The Greek says "not only would there be no composite body, but not even a simple one" without soul; Taylor's sentence is not in it.

@ch IV.7.3 | Not atoms, not form as an affection of matter
Atoms put side by side make no unity and no sympathy, and partless things make no body. If life comes from the form in a body, either the form is a being, and that is soul and no body, or it is an affection of matter, and then something gave it. So the giver of life is outside all bodily nature. Without soul's power, body would flow away; even the world would come apart if left to a body, such as their scattered breath, called soul in name only.

@p
If someone says, not so, but that atoms or partless things, coming together, make the soul by their union and their being affected together, he would be refuted by their being only side by side, since nothing becomes one through the whole, nor in sympathy, from bodies that are not affected and cannot be united; but soul is in sympathy with itself. And from partless things no body or magnitude could come about. Moreover, if of a simple body they will not say that what is of matter has life of itself (for matter is without quality), but that what is ordered by form brings life: if they say this form is a being, not the compound, the soul will be one of these two, which would no longer be a body; for this too is not from matter, or we will break it up again in the same way. If they say it is an affection of matter and not a being, they must say from what the affection and the life came into matter. For matter does not give itself shape, nor put soul into itself.
!flag such a one will be confuted by similitude of passion, and by apposition | The Greek says the atoms' union is refuted by their being merely side by side (παράθεσις): they cannot be affected together.

@p
@greek δεῖ ἄρα τι εἶναι
So there must be something that supplies life, if the supply is neither from matter nor from any of the bodies, being outside and beyond all bodily nature. For there would not be any body at all if there were no power of soul. For body flows, and its nature is in motion, and it would perish very quickly if all things were bodies, even if one named one of them soul; for it would undergo the same as the other bodies, since they have one matter. Or rather, it would not even come to be, but all things would stop at matter, there being nothing to shape it. Perhaps not even matter would be there at all, and this universe too will come apart, if one entrusted it to the holding of a body, giving it the rank of soul down to the names, to air and breath, most easily scattered, and having its being one not of itself. For when all bodies can be cut, how can one who assigns this universe to any of them not make it mindless and carried at random? What order is there in breath, which needs order from soul, or what rational form or intellect? But when there is soul, all these serve it for the make-up of the world and each living being, one power from one thing and another from another contributing to the whole; but if it is not present in the whole, these would be nothing, still less in order.
!note The "breath" (πνεῦμα) that is soul is the Stoics'.
!flag The universe 1 also would rapidly perish | The Greek says body would perish, not "the universe".

@ch IV.7.4 | The Stoics' breath "in a certain state"
Even the Stoics, led by truth, admit something before bodies, an intelligent breath and an intellectual fire, but then look for a place for the better, when bodies should be set in soul. If soul is breath "in a certain state", that state is either nothing, and only matter is real, soul and God mere names; or a being, a rational form, not a body. Besides, a body has one quality and does one thing, as fire heats; soul does contrary things in the same animal.

@p
They themselves bear witness, led by the truth, that there must be, before bodies, a form of soul better than they, setting down the breath as possessing intellect and the fire as intellectual, as if the better portion could not be among beings without fire and breath, and were looking for a place to be settled in; when they should be looking for where they will settle the bodies, since these must be settled in the powers of soul. If they set down life and soul as nothing but the breath, what is this "in a certain state" of theirs, so much talked of, in which they take refuge when they are forced to set down another, active nature besides bodies? If, then, not all breath is soul, since there are countless breaths without soul, but they will say it is the breath in a certain state, this "in a certain state" and this relation they will call either one of the beings or nothing.
!note The Stoics called God "intelligent breath" and "craftsmanlike fire", and soul "breath in a certain state" (πνεῦμα πως ἔχον).
!flag and without a place in which it might be established | Taylor joins what the Greek gives as the Stoics' further mistake, that the better portion was "looking for a place to be settled in".

@p
@greek ἀλλ’ εἰ μὲν μηδέν
If nothing, there would be only breath, and the "in a certain state" a name. And so it will follow for them to say that nothing else is but matter, and soul and God are all names, and that alone is. If the relation is one of the beings, something besides the substrate and matter, in matter but itself immaterial by not being composed again from matter, it would be a rational form and not a body, and a nature of another kind. Further, it is no less plain from these things that it is impossible for the soul to be any body. For it is either hot or cold, hard or soft, fluid or solid, black or white, and all the qualities of bodies, different in different ones. And if hot, it will only heat; if cold, only cool; and the light will make things light by being added and present, and the heavy make them heavy, and the black blacken, and the white make white. For it is not fire's work to cool, nor the cold's to make warm. But soul does different things in different animals, and contrary things in the same one, making some solid and pouring out others, making some dense and others rare, black, white, light, heavy. Yet it ought to make one, according to the quality of the body, and its colour too; but as it is, it makes many.
!flag And its subsistence after a certain manner will be also merely a name, and thus it will be an accident to beings | Taylor's "and thus it will be an accident to beings" is not in the Greek.

@ch IV.7.5 | Growth, memory, and parts of soul
A body has one motion, but soul moves in many, by choice and by rational forms, which are not bodily. A body cannot make itself grow; and if soul were a growing body, what is added would be a new, ignorant soul, and we would keep no memories, since nothing in it would stay the same. Cut a quantity and it is no longer what it was; but each part of soul is a whole soul, and from one seed come twins. So soul and the rational forms are without quantity.

@p
And how are the motions different, not one, when every body has one motion? If they put some down to choices and others to rational forms, that is right; but choice is not a body's, nor the rational forms, being different, when the body is one and simple and has no share in such a form, except what it was given by its maker, to be hot or cold. And making things grow at their times, and up to such a measure, where would that come to the body itself, whose part is to be made to grow, and which itself has no share in making grow, except as much as it may be taken on in a mass of matter, serving what produces growth through it? For if the soul, being a body, makes things grow, it too must grow, plainly by the addition of like body, if it is to keep pace with what grows by it. What is added will be either soul or a soulless body. If soul, from where and how does it come in, and how is it added? If what is added is soulless, how will this be ensouled, and agree with what was before, and be one, and share the same opinions as the first, and not, like a stranger soul, be ignorant of what the other knows? And, like the rest of our mass, will some of it flow away and some come in, and nothing be the same? How, then, have we memories? How do we know our own, never using the same soul?
!flag Nor can such a body be full of productive power, except so far as this is imparted to it by that which makes it to be hot or cold | The Greek says the body has no share in such a rational form except what its maker gave it, "to be hot or cold"; it is the maker who gives, not something that "makes it hot or cold" later.

@p
@greek καὶ μὴν εἰ σῶμά ἐστι
Moreover, if it is a body, and it is the nature of a body divided into many that each of its parts is not the same as the whole: if soul is a magnitude of so much, and if it is less it will not be soul, as every quantity changes what it was by taking away; or if something with magnitude, lessened in mass, stays the same in quality, it is different as body and as quantity, but can keep the same by its quality, which is other than its quantity: what, then, will those say who call the soul a body? First, about each part of the soul in the same body: is each a soul such as the whole is? And again the part of the part? Then magnitude contributed nothing to its being; yet it should, if it is a quantity. And it is whole in many places, which cannot belong to a body, the same whole being in many things and the part being what the whole is. If they will say each of the parts is not a soul, the soul will be made for them of soulless things. And further, if the magnitude of each soul is fixed, then either way, less or more, it will not be soul.
!flag The whole soul, likewise, is every where present with the body | Taylor is close. The Greek says it is whole in many places, which cannot be true of a body.

@p
@greek ὅταν τοίνυν ἐκ συνόδου μιᾶς
When, then, from one coupling and one seed twins are born, or, as in other animals, very many, the seed being divided into many places, where each is whole, how does this not teach those who want to learn that where the part is the same as the whole, it has gone beyond being a quantity in its very being, and must of necessity be without quantity? For so it would stay the same when quantity is stolen from it, since quantity and mass do not concern it, its being being something else. So the soul and the rational forms are without quantity.

@ch IV.7.6 | Perception needs something undivided
If the soul were a body, there would be no perceiving, thinking, knowing, virtue or anything fine. What perceives must be one, taking in a face whole, and sight and hearing together, like a centre where the lines from a circle meet. If it had size, it would be divided with its object, and no one would perceive the whole; or each piece would perceive the whole, making images without limit. And impressions in a body would either run together, as in water, so that there is no memory, or block new ones.

@p
That, if the soul were a body, there would be no perceiving, no thinking, no knowing, no virtue, nor anything fine, is plain from what follows. If something is to perceive anything, it must itself be one, and grasp everything by the same, even if many things come in through many sense organs, or many qualities about one thing, or something varied through one, such as a face. For it is not one thing for the nose and another for the eyes, but the same for all together. And if one comes through the eyes and another through hearing, there must be one thing into which both come. Or how would it say these are different, unless the perceptions came together into the same? This, then, must be like a centre, and the perceptions from every side, like lines meeting from the circumference of a circle, must end at it; and what grasps them must be such, truly one.

@p
@greek εἰ δὲ διεστὼς τοῦτο γένοιτο
If this were extended, and the perceptions struck it as at the two ends of a line, either they will run together again into one and the same, like the middle, or one part will perceive one thing and another another, as if I perceived one thing and you another. And if the thing perceived is one, like a face, either it will be drawn together into one (which plainly happens: it is drawn together even in the pupils themselves, or how could the greatest things be seen through them?), so that, still more, going into the ruling part, they become like partless thoughts, and this will be partless; or, this being a magnitude, it would be divided along with it, so that one part has the grasp of one part, and none of us of the whole of what is perceived. But the whole is one; how would it be divided? Equal will not fit equal, since the ruling part is not equal to everything perceived. Into how many pieces, then, the division? Will it be divided into as many as the number of the variety that the incoming perception has? And will each of those parts of the soul perceive by its own pieces too? Or will the pieces of the pieces be without perception? But that is impossible. If any part whatever perceives the whole, since magnitude is by nature divisible without limit, it will follow that there are unlimited perceptions of each perceived thing for each part, as it were unlimited images of the same thing in our ruling part.
!flag or they must be collected into one. And this indeed appears to be the case | Taylor's order is confused here; the Greek's alternatives are: drawn into one and partless, or divided with the object.

@p
@greek καὶ μὴν σώματος ὄντος
Moreover, if what perceives is a body, perceiving could happen in no other way than as seals from rings stamped in wax, the perceptions being stamped either into blood or into air. And if it is as in fluid bodies, which is reasonable, they will run together as in water, and there will be no memory; if the imprints stay, either others cannot be stamped while those hold, so that there will be no further perceptions, or, when others come, the earlier will be destroyed, so that there will be no remembering. But if there is remembering, and perceiving one thing after another without the earlier hindering, it is impossible for the soul to be a body.
!flag than as if certain images were impressed from wax in a seal | The Greek has seals from rings stamped in wax; Taylor has it the wrong way round.

@ch IV.7.7 | Pain in the finger
When a man has a pain in his finger, the pain is in the finger but the perceiving is in the ruling part. If the Stoic breath passes the affection along, each stage would feel its own pain, and the ruling part would feel only the pain next to it, not the finger's. Since one piece of a mass cannot know what another undergoes, what perceives must be the same with itself everywhere: something other than body.

@p
One might see this same thing from the perception of pain too. When a man is said to have a pain in his finger, the pain is about the finger, surely, but the perception of the pain, they will admit, comes about at the ruling part. Though the suffering part of the breath is another, the ruling part perceives, and the whole soul is affected in the same way. How, then, does this happen? By passing on, they will say: the soul-breath about the finger is affected first, passes it to the next, and this to another, until it reaches the ruling part.
