Then, if the first in pain perceived, the perception of the second must be another, if perception is by passing on, and of the third another, and there will be many perceptions, without limit, of one pain; and the ruling part will perceive all of these afterwards, and its own besides these. But in truth each of those would not be of the pain in the finger, but the one next to the finger would be that the palm hurts, and the third that another part higher up hurts, and there would be many pains; and the ruling part would perceive not the pain at the finger but the one next to itself, and know only this, and let the rest go, not knowing that the finger hurts.
!flag but one sensation perceives that the part of the palm of the hand which is next to the finger is pained | Taylor is right; ταρσός is the flat of the hand.

@p
@greek εἰ τοίνυν κατὰ διάδοσιν
If, then, perception of such a thing cannot come about by passing on, and in a body, which is a mass, one part cannot know what another undergoes (for in every magnitude this is one and that another), we must set down what perceives as such that it is everywhere the same with itself; and to do this belongs to some other of the beings than body.

@ch IV.7.8 | Thinking, virtue; not a harmony, not an entelechy
Thinking grasps without body what has no size, and abstracts circle and line from matter, so what thinks is no body; the beautiful, the just and the virtues are without size and everlasting, and need something lasting to be in. Bodies act by bodiless powers; qualities are not bodies; a body cannot pass whole through a body, but soul does. The Stoics put nature before soul and intellect last, the worse before the better. Nor is soul a harmony, which comes after and cannot rule; nor an inseparable entelechy, for soul withdraws in sleep, opposes desire, thinks, and moves from body to body. It is a true being.

@p
That it cannot think either, if the soul is any body, must be shown from what follows. If perceiving is the soul's grasping of perceptibles by using the body, thinking would not be taking hold through the body, or it will be the same as perceiving. If, then, thinking is grasping without body, still more must what thinks not be a body. For perception is of perceptibles, thinking of intelligibles. If they do not want this, still there will be thoughts of some intelligibles and grasps of things without size. How, then, will what is a magnitude think what is not a magnitude, and think the undivided by what is divided? By some partless part of itself? If so, what thinks will not be a body; for there is no need of the whole to touch; one point is enough. If, then, they grant that the first thoughts are of what is wholly the purest of body, each by itself, as is true, what thinks must know by being or becoming pure of body. If they say thoughts are of the forms in matter, still they come about with the bodies separated, as intellect separates them. For the separating of circle and triangle and line and point is not done with flesh, or with matter at all. So the soul too must separate itself from body in such a case; so it must not itself be a body.
!flag it is necessary that the nature which intellectually perceives the form separate from body of each thing, should know either real being, or that which is becoming to be | The Greek says what thinks must know "by being or becoming pure of body" (καθαρὸν ὂν ἢ γιγνόμενον); "real being" and "that which is becoming to be" are not there.

@p
@greek ἀμέγεθες δέ, οἶμαι
The beautiful and the just are without magnitude, I think, and so is the thinking of them. So the soul will receive them, as they come to it, by its partless part, and they will lie in it in the partless. And how, if the soul were a body, would its virtues be, self-control and justice and courage and the rest? Self-control or justice or courage would be some breath or some blood; unless courage were the hardness of the breath to affect, and self-control its good blend, and beauty a good shape in impressions, by which we say, when we see them, that people are fair and beautiful in body. It might well suit a breath to be strong and beautiful in impressions; but what need has breath of self-control? Rather the opposite, to be well treated in embraces and touches, where it will be warmed, or long for measured coolness, or come near soft and tender and smooth things. And what would it care about giving each his due?
!flag How also, if the soul is body, can temperance and justice be the virtues of it, which are its saviours, so far as they are received by it ? | Taylor's chapter, as the scan gives it, ends here: the rest of chapter 8, on virtue, on bodily powers, on mixture, on harmony and on entelechy, is missing from it. "Which are its saviours" is not in the Greek.

@p
@greek πότερον δὲ ἀιδίων ὄντων
Does the soul touch the theorems of virtue, and the other intelligibles, as everlasting things, or does virtue come to be in someone, help him, and perish again? But who makes it, and from where? For so that would remain again. So they must be of everlasting and lasting things, like those of geometry. If of everlasting and lasting things, not of bodies. So what they will be in must be of this kind too: so it must not be a body. For all the nature of body does not remain but flows. If, seeing the actions of bodies that heat and cool and push and weigh down, they set the soul here, as settling it in an active place, first they do not know that bodies themselves do these things by the bodiless powers in them; then, that we do not think these are the powers about soul, but thinking, perceiving, reasoning, desiring, and caring wisely and well for all things, which looks for another kind of being. So, shifting the powers of bodies over to bodiless things, they leave none to them.

@p
@greek ὅτι δὲ καὶ τὰ σώματα
That bodies too can do what they can by bodiless powers is plain from what follows. They will agree that quality and quantity are different, and that every body is a quantity, and further that not every body has quality, as matter has not. Agreeing on this, they will agree that quality, being other than quantity, is other than body. For how will it be a body, not being a quantity, if every body is a quantity? And, as was said somewhere above, if every body and mass, when divided, loses what it was, while when the body is cut up the same whole quality stays in each part, as the sweetness of honey is no less sweetness in each, then sweetness would not be a body; and the others likewise. Then, if the powers were bodies, strong powers would have to be great masses, and those able to do little small ones. But if great masses have small powers, and not a few of the smallest masses have the greatest, acting must be assigned to something other than magnitude: to what has no magnitude. And their saying that matter is the same, being body, as they say, but makes different things by taking on qualities, how does it not show that what is added is rational forms, and without body?

@p
@greek μηδ’ ὅτι πνεύματος
Let them not say that living beings die when the breath or the blood leaves them. For one cannot be without these, nor without many other things, none of which would be the soul. And neither breath nor blood goes through everything, but soul does. Further, if soul, being a body, went through everything, it would be blended in the way blending is for other bodies. But if the blending of bodies leaves none of the blended in act, the soul would no longer be in the bodies in act, but only potentially, having lost its being soul; as, if sweet and bitter were blended, the sweet is not there: so we have no soul. And for a body to be blended with a body whole through whole, so that where one is the other is too, both having equal mass and the whole, with no growth when the other is put in, will leave nothing it does not cut. For the blending is not by large pieces side by side (for then, they say, it would be juxtaposition), but what was put in has gone through everything, though smaller, which is impossible, that the less should become equal to the greater. But it cuts the whole at every point, having gone through. Then, if it is at every point and there is no body between that is not cut, the body must have been divided into points, which is impossible. And the cutting being without limit (for whatever body you take is divisible), the unlimited will be not only potential but in act. So a body cannot go whole through a whole; but soul goes through wholes; so it is bodiless.
!note "Total blending" (κρᾶσις δι’ ὅλων) of soul through body was Stoic doctrine; Plotinus' argument follows Aristotle's and Alexander's criticisms of it.

@p
@greek τὸ δὲ καὶ φύσιν μὲν προτέραν
And their saying that the same breath was first nature, and became soul when it came into cold and was tempered, becoming finer in the cold (which itself is absurd, for many living beings are born in heat and have a soul not cooled): at any rate they say nature was before soul, which comes about by outside chances. So it follows for them to make the worse first, and before it another, lesser thing, which they call "holding", and intellect last, plainly come from soul. Or if intellect is before all, they should have made soul next, then nature, and always the later worse, as is natural. If, then, God too for them is later, by intellect, and born, and has thinking brought in, it might be that there is neither soul nor intellect nor God, if the potential, with nothing in act before it, would never come into being or reach act. For what will lead it, if there is nothing else before it? If it leads itself into act, which is absurd, it will lead itself looking at something, which will be not potentially but in act. Yet, if what is potential is to have its always remaining the same, it will come into act of itself, and this will be better than what is able, as being what it longs for. So the better is first, and has a nature other than body, and is always in act: so intellect and soul are before nature. So the soul is not as breath, nor as body. That it might be said not to be a body, others have given other arguments; these are enough.
!note "Holding" (ἕξις), "nature" (φύσις) and "soul" are the Stoic grades of breath: what holds a stone, what makes a plant grow, what makes an animal live.

@p
@greek ἐπεὶ δὲ καὶ ἄλλης φύσεως
Since it is of another nature, we must ask what this is. Is it something other than body, but something of body, like a harmony? Those around Pythagoras, saying this in another way, thought it was something like the harmony about strings. As there, when the strings are stretched, there comes upon them something like an affection, which is called harmony, so too, since our body comes about in a blending of unlike things, a certain blend makes life, and the soul is the affection upon the blend. That this is impossible has already been said in many ways against this view: that soul is before and harmony after; that soul rules and presides over the body and often fights it, which a harmony would not do; that soul is a being and harmony not; that the blend of the bodies we are made of, being in proportion, would be health; that each part, blended differently, would be a different soul, so that there would be many; and, most of all, that there must be, before this soul, another soul that makes this harmony, as with instruments there is the musician putting the harmony into the strings, having in himself the rational form by which he will tune them. For neither there will the strings, nor here the bodies, be able to bring themselves into harmony of themselves. In general these too make ensouled things out of soulless, and ordered things out of disordered by chance, and the soul's own existence come from a spontaneous order, not order from soul. But this cannot happen in the particular or in the whole. So the soul is not a harmony.
!note The harmony view is Simmias' in Plato's Phaedo (85e-86d), refuted there (92a-95a); Aristotle discusses it in On the Soul I.4, 407b.

@p
@greek τὸ δὲ τῆς ἐντελεχείας
The entelechy one might examine thus, how it is said of soul. They say the soul in the compound has the rank of form, with the ensouled body as matter; form not of any body, nor of body as body, but of a natural body equipped with organs, having life potentially. If, then, it is made like it by its relation, as the shape of a statue to the bronze, then when the body is divided the soul must be divided with it, and when a part is cut off, a portion of soul must go with what was cut off; and the withdrawal in sleep would not happen, since the entelechy must be grown on to what it is of; and in truth there would be no sleep. Moreover, if it is an entelechy, there will be no opposition of reason to desires, but the whole will be affected one and the same way through and through, not at odds with itself. Perceptions alone might perhaps come about, but thoughts could not. That is why they themselves bring in another soul, intellect, which they set down as immortal. So the reasoning soul must be an entelechy in another way than this, if one must use this name.
!note The definition is Aristotle's: "the first entelechy (actuality) of a natural body equipped with organs" (On the Soul II.1, 412a-b); the immortal intellect is his "active intellect" (III.5).

@p
@greek οὐδ’ ἡ αἰσθητική
Nor is the perceiving soul, if it too keeps the imprints of perceptibles when they are absent, keeping them, then, not with the body; if not, they will be in it as shapes and images; but it could not receive others, if they were in it so. So it is not an inseparable entelechy. Nor is the desiring part, the one that desires not food and drink but things other than the body's: it is not an inseparable entelechy either. The growing part would be left, which might seem open to dispute, whether it is an inseparable entelechy in this way. But it does not seem so either. For the principle of every plant is about the root, and when the rest of the body withers about the root and the lower parts, in many plants the soul has plainly left the other parts and drawn together into one; so it was not in the whole as an inseparable entelechy. And before the plant grows, it is in the little mass. If, then, it goes into a little from a larger plant, and from a little to the whole, what prevents it from being separated entirely? And how, being partless, would it become a divided entelechy of the divided body? And the same soul comes to be another animal's from one: how would the soul of the first become the next's, if it were the entelechy of one? This is plain from animals that change into other animals. So it does not have its being by being the form of something, but is a being that does not take its being from being settled in a body, but is before it comes to be this one's, of the living being, say, whose body will produce the soul.
!note In this edition the Greek has "when the rest of the body grows" (αὐξανομένου); "withers" follows the later correction αὐαινομένου, which the argument needs.

@p
@greek τίς οὖν οὐσία αὐτῆς
What, then, is its being? If it is neither body nor an affection of body, but action and making, and much is both in it and from it, being a being besides bodies, what kind is it? Plainly what we call real being. For everything bodily might be called becoming, not being, coming to be and perishing, never really being, but kept safe by sharing in being, as far as it shares in it.

@ch IV.7.9 | Being from itself, life from itself
The other nature has being from itself: all that really is, which neither comes to be nor perishes, and which keeps all else safe. Soul is a principle of motion, moving itself and giving life to the ensouled body, having its life from itself, so it never loses it. Not all life can be borrowed; there must be something living first, imperishable. What is from itself is always, as whiteness is never not white; and it is not dead like a stone, but lives a pure life, hindered when mixed with the worse, but never losing its nature.

@p
The other nature, which has being of itself, is all that really is, which neither comes to be nor perishes; or all the rest will be gone, and could not come to be afterwards, if this had perished, which gives them their safety, both the others and this universe, kept safe and ordered through soul. For this is the principle of motion, supplying motion to the others, and itself moved of itself, and giving life to the ensouled body, and having life of itself, which it never loses, since it has it of itself. For not all things use a life brought in from outside; or it will go on without limit; but there must be some nature that is living first, which must of necessity be indestructible and immortal, as being the principle of life for the others too.
!note "The principle of motion, moved of itself" is Plato's proof of immortality in the Phaedrus (245c-e).
!flag There must, therefore, be another nature which possesses existence from itself | Taylor begins his chapter with "There must, therefore, be", as if it followed on his shortened chapter 8; the Greek begins "The other nature".

@p
@greek ἔνθα δὴ καὶ τὸ θεῖον ἅπαν
There all that is divine and blessed must be settled, living of itself and being of itself, being first and living first, without share in change in its being, neither coming to be nor perishing. For from where could it come to be, or into what perish? And if the name of being is to be truly said, it will have to be not sometimes being and sometimes not; as white, the colour itself, is not sometimes white and sometimes not white. If white were also being, along with being white it would be always; but it has only white. But that to which being is present, of itself and first, will be always. So this, being first and always, must be not dead, like a stone or wood, but living, and use a pure life, as much of it as stays alone; and what is mixed with the worse has a hindrance towards the best, but has not lost its own nature, and takes back its ancient state when it runs back up to its own.

@ch IV.7.10 | The soul is akin to the divine
Soul is akin to the divine and everlasting: it has no body, shape or colour. Take soul not with the desires and passions of the body, but purified: then wisdom and the other virtue are seen to be its own, and evils added from outside. If all souls were like this, no one would doubt immortality. Look at yourself, with the additions taken away: you will see intellect seeing the everlasting, lit by truth from the Good, and say "Farewell: I am an immortal god to you." Like living gold cleaned of earth, it wonders at itself.

@p
That the soul is akin to the more divine nature and the everlasting is made plain by its having been shown not to be a body. And it has neither shape nor colour and cannot be touched. But it can be shown from these things too. Since we agree that all that is divine and really is uses a good and wise life, we must look next at what our soul is like in nature, starting from it. Let us take the soul, not the one in the body that has taken on unreasoning desires and passions and admitted other affections, but the one that has wiped these off and, as far as it can, has no fellowship with the body. This shows plainly that evils are additions to the soul, and from elsewhere, while in it, purified, the best things are present, wisdom and the other virtue, being its own. If, then, the soul is such when it goes back up to itself, how is it not of that nature which we say belongs to all that is divine and everlasting? For wisdom and true virtue, being divine, would not come to be in a poor and mortal thing, but such a thing must be divine, as sharing in divine things, through kinship and being of one being.
!flag through an alliance and similitude of essence to a divine nature | Taylor is right; the Greek has ὁμοούσιον, "of one being" with the divine.

@p
@greek διὸ καὶ ὅστις τοιοῦτος ἡμῶν
That is why whoever of us is like this would differ little from those above in soul itself, falling short only in this, so far as he is in a body. And so, if every man were like this, or a great many had such souls, no one would be so unbelieving as not to believe that their soul was wholly immortal. But as it is, seeing the soul in most people maimed in many ways, they do not think of it as a divine or immortal thing. But one must look at the nature of each thing by looking at it pure, since what is added is always a hindrance to knowing what it was added to. Look, then, taking away; or rather, let the one who takes away look at himself, and he will believe he is immortal, when he sees himself come to be in the intelligible and in the pure. For he will see an intellect that sees nothing perceptible, nothing of these mortal things, but thinks the everlasting by the everlasting, all things in the intelligible, having become itself an intelligible world and full of light, lit by the truth from the Good, which shines truth on all the intelligibles; so that he will often think it well said: "Farewell: I am an immortal god to you", having gone up to the divine and fixed his gaze on likeness to it.
!note The line is from the opening of Empedocles' Purifications (fragment 112).
!flag Farewell, a God immortal now am 1/ having ascended to divinity | The scan's "am 1/" is "am I"; the words are Empedocles', which Taylor does not name.

@p
@greek εἰ δ’ ἡ κάθαρσις ποιεῖ
If purification makes one be in knowledge of the best things, the kinds of knowledge within too come to light, which are really knowledge. For the soul does not run somewhere outside to see self-control and justice, but by itself, in thinking itself and what it was before, seeing them set up in itself like statues, which time had filled with rust, it makes them clean; as if gold had a soul, and then, knocking off all the earth in it, having been ignorant of itself before because it did not see gold, it would then wonder at itself, seeing itself alone, and would think that it had no need at all of beauty brought in, being itself best, if one let it be by itself.

@ch IV.7.11 | Life that cannot be lost
Who with sense would doubt that such a thing is immortal? Its life is not acquired, nor present as heat to fire, which belongs to the fire's matter, so that fire can be put out. Either life is the soul's being, a being that lives of itself, which they will grant is immortal; or, if they break it up, they must come at last to something immortal, moved of itself; or, if life is an affection brought into matter, what brought it must be immortal, unable to take the opposite of what it brings.

@p
About such a thing, who that has intellect would dispute that it is immortal? Life is present to it from itself, which cannot be lost: how could it be, not being acquired, nor present in the way heat is present to fire? I do not mean that heat is brought into fire from outside, but that, even if not to the fire, it is to the matter under the fire; and that is how the fire is dissolved. But the soul does not have life in such a way that it is matter lying under, and life coming upon it shows it to be soul. Either life is a being, and such a being living of itself, which is just what we are looking for, and they will agree this is immortal; or they will break it up as composite, and this again, until they come to something immortal, moved of itself, for which it is not lawful to receive the portion of death; or, saying life is an affection brought into matter, they will be forced to agree that whatever this affection came into matter from is itself immortal, since it cannot receive the opposite of what it brings. But there is one nature, living in act.
!note "Cannot receive the opposite of what it brings" is the last argument of the Phaedo (105d-106e): soul brings life and cannot admit death.
!flag but life acceding, demonstrates the presence of soul | Taylor has the sense backwards: the Greek denies that soul is matter lying under, with life coming upon it to make it soul.

@ch IV.7.12 | All souls are immortal
If every soul perished, all would long since have perished. If the world's soul is immortal and ours not, why? Each is a principle of motion, lives of itself, and thinks the same things, in heaven and beyond, up to the first principle. Its knowledge, by recollection, gives it being before the body, and its everlasting knowledge shows it everlasting. It is simple, so it is not dissolved; it has no mass, so it is not cut up; and alteration takes away form and leaves matter, which belongs to composites.

@p
Further, if they say every soul is perishable, all things should have perished long ago; if one is and another not, the soul of the universe immortal, say, but ours not, they must say the reason. For each is a principle of motion, each lives of itself, and each touches the same things by the same, thinking what is in the heaven and what is beyond the heaven, seeking all that has being and going up as far as the first principle. And its understanding of each thing, from itself, from the sights within it, which comes about by recollection, gives it being before the body, and, using everlasting knowledge, being itself everlasting. And everything that is dissolved, having taken composition for its being, is by nature dissolved in the way it was put together. But soul is one and simple, being in act a nature in living; so it will not perish in this way. But perhaps, divided and cut up, it might perish? But the soul is not a mass or a quantity, as was shown. But altered, it will come to perishing? But alteration that destroys takes away the form and leaves the matter; this is the affection of a composite. If, then, it cannot perish in any of these ways, it must be imperishable.
!flag it is evident the soul gave being to itself prior to the body | The Greek says its understanding by recollection "gives it" (δίδωσι) being before the body: the argument shows it existed before, not that the soul made itself.

@ch IV.7.13 | Why soul comes into body
If the intelligible is separate, why does soul come into body? Intellect alone stays above, without impulse or desire. What has desire, next to intellect, goes out further: longing to order things by what it saw in intellect, as if pregnant, it hastens to make. With the soul of the whole it presides from outside; wanting to govern a part, it comes into it, yet not wholly, keeping something outside body. It is now in body, now out, set in motion from the first, going to the third, by the act of intellect, which stays and fills all with beauty through soul.

@p
How, then, if the intelligible is separate, does this soul come into body? Because as much as is intellect alone stays always there, unaffected among the intelligibles, having only an intellectual life; for there is no impulse or desire in it. But what takes on desire, being next to that intellect, by the addition of desire goes out, as it were, further, and, longing to order according to what it saw in intellect, as if pregnant from them and in labour to give birth, it hastens to make and to craft. And stretched by this eagerness towards the perceptible, with the whole soul of all things it rises above what is governed, outside it, and shares in the care of the universe; but when it wants to govern a part, it is alone, and comes to be in what it is in, not becoming wholly and all of it the body's, but having something outside body too. So not even its intellect is affected. It is now in a body, now outside a body, set in motion from the first things and going out to the third, by the act of intellect, while intellect stays in the same, and through soul fills all things with beauty and orders them, immortal through immortal, since it too will be always, through unceasing activity.
!flag it abides ■which are more daemoniacal, and others approximate nearer to our essence | Taylor's long footnote (from Olympiodorus and others, on why souls come down) is printed into the middle of his sentence in the scan, which goes on, after it, "there eternally".
!flag being an immortal adorner through an immortal soul | Taylor is right; the Greek has "immortal through immortal".

@ch IV.7.14 | Souls of animals and plants
Souls that went astray as far as beasts' bodies are immortal too; and if there is another kind of soul, in animals and plants, it comes from the living nature, the same principle, and is bodiless, partless and a being. If the human soul, being in three parts, is broken up with the compound, the pure souls leave behind what was moulded on in birth, and the others keep it long; and even the worse part is not destroyed while its source is. Nothing that is perishes.

@p
About the soul of the other living beings: those souls that went astray and came as far as the bodies of beasts must be immortal too. If there is another kind of soul, it too must come from nowhere but the living nature, and be itself a cause of life for the living beings, and so too the soul in plants. For all have set out from the same principle, having a life of their own, and these too are bodiless, partless and beings. If they say that the human soul, being in three parts, will be dissolved because composite, we too shall say that the pure ones, set free, will let go what was moulded on in birth, and others will be with it for a very long time; and the worse part, let go, will not itself perish, as long as that from which it has its origin is. For nothing of what is will perish.
!note The three parts of the soul are Plato's (Republic IV, 439d-441a); "moulded on" (προσπλασθέν) recalls the Timaeus (42c).
!flag If, however, it is requisite that the soul of man being tripartite should be dissolved with the composite | The Greek gives this as the opponents' claim, "if they say"; and Taylor's "this is accomplished by others in long periods of time" is not quite it: the others stay with what was added for a very long time.

@ch IV.7.15 | Evidence from oracles and the dead
That is the proof for those who need proof. For those who want belief mixed with perception, there is much in history: the gods' oracles bidding men appease the wrath of wronged souls and honour the dead as still perceiving, as all men do; and many souls that were once in men have not stopped doing good to men out of the body, giving oracles and showing by themselves that other souls have not perished either.

@p
What had to be said to those who need demonstration has been said. What must be said also to those who need belief mixed with perception may be picked out from the history about such things, which is plentiful: from the answers the gods gave, bidding men appease the wrath of souls that were wronged, and give honours to the dead as being in perception, as all men do for those who have gone; and many souls, once in men, after coming out of bodies, have not ceased doing good to men: these, setting up oracles and giving answers, help in other things too, and show through themselves that the other souls too have not perished.
!flag those who stand in need of the evidence arising from faith mingled with sensible information | Taylor is right; πίστις here is "belief", trust, not religious faith.

@treatise V.1 | On the three principal hypostases
Why have souls forgotten their father, God? Their daring, their coming to birth, and their wish to be their own carried them far away, and admiring what is here they despised themselves. The cure is to remind the soul of its race and worth. Plotinus leads it up step by step: the soul that makes and moves the whole heaven, and that our soul is like; above it intellect, the true world, in which all is present and eternal, and which is being; above that the One, which produces intellect without moving, as the sun gives its light. These three, the One, intellect and soul, were already in Plato, Parmenides and others, more or less clearly, and they are in us too. To see the One we must turn inward and wait, as one waits for the sun to rise.

@ch V.1.1 | Why souls forget their father
Souls forget God their father, and themselves, because of daring, birth, the first otherness, and the wish to be their own. Delighting in moving by themselves, they ran the other way, like children taken from their parents, and, admiring what is here, they despised themselves. Two lessons are needed: one showing how worthless what they now honour is, the other reminding the soul of its race and worth, which comes first.

@p
What is it, then, that has made the souls forget their father, God, and, though they are portions from there and wholly his, be ignorant both of themselves and of him? The beginning of evil for them was daring, and coming to birth, and the first otherness, and the wish to be their own. Since they appeared delighting in their freedom, using much their moving of themselves, running the opposite way and making the greatest distance, they did not know even that they were from there; as children torn away from their fathers at once and brought up far away for a long time do not know who they or their fathers are. No longer seeing him or themselves, despising themselves through ignorance of their race, honouring other things and admiring everything rather than themselves, struck and delighted by them and hanging on them, they broke themselves away, as far as they could, from what they turned from and despised. So it happens that honouring the things here and despising themselves is the cause of their utter ignorance of him.
!flag and the wish to exercise an unrestrained freedom of the will | The Greek has "the wish to be their own" (τὸ βουληθῆναι ἑαυτῶν εἶναι); the "freedom" (τὸ αὐτεξούσιον) comes in the next sentence.
!flag Hence, the honour which they pay to sensible objects, and the contempt of themselves, happen to be the causes of their all-perfect ignorance | Taylor is right; the Greek says ignorance "of him", God.

@p
@greek ἅμα γὰρ διώκεται ἄλλο
For at once something else is pursued and admired, and what admires and pursues admits it is worse; and putting itself lower than things that come to be and perish, and taking itself to be the least honoured and most mortal of all it honours, it could never take to heart the nature or power of God. So the account must be twofold for those so disposed, if one is to turn them to the opposite and to the first things, and lead them up to the highest, the one and first. What is each? One shows how little the things now honoured by the soul are worth, which we will go through more fully elsewhere; the other teaches the soul and makes it recollect its race, as it were, and its worth, which comes before that one, and, made clear, will make that one plain too. This must be spoken of now, for it is near what we are seeking and useful for it. For what seeks is soul, and it must know what it is that seeks, so that it may first learn about itself, whether it has the power to seek such things, and whether it has an eye such as to see, and whether it is fitting for it to seek. If the things are foreign, why should it? If akin, it is fitting, and it can find them.
!flag One of them, indeed, is that which shows the cause why the soul honours these sensible objects | The Greek says the first account shows how little the things the soul now honours are worth (τὴν ἀτιμίαν), not "the cause why" it honours them.

@ch V.1.2 | The soul that animates the heaven
Let every soul remember that it made all living things, the sun and this great heaven, and orders and moves them, being other than they and more honourable, since they perish when soul leaves them and it does not leave itself. Let a quiet soul, with body, earth, sea and sky at rest, think of soul flowing into the heaven from outside like sunlight into a dark cloud, giving it life and immortality. It is whole everywhere, and makes the world a god, and the sun and stars. Ours is of the same form: admire yourself.

@p
Let every soul, then, first think of this: that it made all living things, breathing life into them, those the earth feeds and those the sea feeds and those in the air and the divine stars in heaven; it made the sun, it made this great heaven, and it ordered it, and it carries it round in order, being a nature other than what it orders and moves and makes live. And it must be more honourable than these, since they come to be and perish when the soul that supplies life leaves them, but it always is, because it does not leave itself. How life is supplied, in the whole and in each thing, let it reason thus. Let another soul, not small, made worthy to look, freed from deceit and from what has bewitched the others, look at the great soul, in a quiet state. Let not only the body around it and the body's tossing be quiet, but all that surrounds it: let the earth be quiet, and quiet the sea and the air and the heaven itself, the better.
!note "The better" (ἀμείνων) is in quotation marks in the editions: perhaps Plato's phrase for the heaven, compared with the elements below.
!flag and the heavens themselves which are more excellent than the elements.1 | Taylor's "than the elements" is his own explanation of "the better".

@p
@greek νοείτω δὲ πάντοθεν εἰς αὐτὸν
Let it think of soul as if flowing into it from outside on every side while it stands still, pouring in and coming in on every side and shining in: as the rays of the sun, lighting up a dark cloud, make it shine and give it a golden look, so soul, coming into the body of the heaven, gave it life, gave it immortality, and woke it as it lay. And it, moved with an everlasting motion by a soul that leads it wisely, became a happy living being, and the heaven got its worth when soul came to dwell in it, being before soul a dead body, earth and water, or rather the darkness of matter and not-being, and "what the gods hate", as someone says.
!note "What the gods hate" is from the Iliad (20.65), of the halls of the dead.
!flag But heaven being all gods, the unity of all unities, and beyond the first adyta | Taylor's long footnote from Proclus is printed into his sentence in the scan, which goes on, after it, "moved with a perpetual motion".

@p
@greek γένοιτο δ’ ἂν φανερωτέρα
Its power and nature would become plainer and clearer if one thought here how it holds and leads the heaven by its own will. For it has given itself to all this magnitude, as great as it is, and every extension, great and small, has been ensouled, one body lying here and another there, and some opposite one another, and others hanging apart from one another in other ways. But soul is not so, nor does it make things live by being cut up into a portion of soul for each piece; all things live by the whole, and it is present all of it everywhere, becoming like the father who begot it, both in being one and in being everywhere. And the heaven, many as it is and different in different places, is one by its power, and this world is a god through it. The sun too is a god, because it is ensouled, and the other stars, and we, if we are anything, through this; for "corpses are more fit to throw out than dung."
!note "Corpses are more fit to throw out than dung" is Heraclitus (fragment 96).

@p
@greek τὴν δὴ θεοῖς αἰτίαν
The cause for the gods of their being gods must be a god older than they. Ours is of the same form, and when you look at it without what has come upon it, taking it purified, you will find the same thing, honourable, which soul was, and more honourable than all that is bodily. For all that is earth; and if it is fire, what would be its burning? And all that is put together from these, even if you add water to them, or air. If it is to be pursued because it is ensouled, why does one pass over himself and pursue another? Admiring the soul in another, admire yourself.
!flag For [perhaps without soul T] all things would be earth | Taylor supplies "perhaps without soul" from Ficino (his footnote says so); the Greek says simply "all that is earth".

@ch V.1.3 | Soul is an image of intellect
Soul, honourable and divine as it is, is an image of intellect, as spoken words are of the thought in the soul: it is the rational expression and activity of intellect, as fire has heat in itself and heat it gives. So it is intellectual, its intellect working in reasonings, and perfected by intellect as by a father. Its own activities are the intellectual ones; the worse come from elsewhere. Nothing comes between them but otherness: soul receives, intellect is form.

@p
Since the soul is so honourable and divine a thing, trusting now that by such a thing you will go after God, go up to him with such a cause: you will surely not reach far, and there is not much between. Take, then, what is more divine than this divine thing, the soul's neighbour above, after which and from which the soul is. For though it is such a thing as the argument showed, it is an image of intellect; as speech that is uttered is an image of the speech in the soul, so it too is the expression of intellect, and the whole activity by which it sends out life to make another exist; as of fire, one heat is in it and another is what it gives. But there one must take it not as flowing out, but as the one staying in it and the other coming into existence.
!flag but partly abiding in intellect, and partly giving subsistence to another life | Taylor is close; the Greek says one activity stays within, the other comes into existence, without "life".

@p
@greek οὖσα οὖν ἀπὸ νοῦ
Being from intellect, then, it is intellectual, and its intellect is in reasonings, and its completion is from intellect again, as from a father who brought it up, whom he did not beget complete as compared with himself. So its existence is from intellect, and its rational form in act comes when intellect is seen by it. For when it looks into intellect, it has within itself, as its own, what it thinks and does. And these alone must be called activities of the soul, all that it does intellectually and from its own home; the worse come from elsewhere and are affections of a soul of this kind. Intellect, then, makes it still more divine, both by being its father and by being present to it. For there is nothing between but their being different, one next in order and as the receiving, the other as form; and even the matter of intellect is beautiful, being of intellect's form and simple. What intellect is like is plain from this very thing, that it is better than a soul that is such as this.
!flag The great excellence, however, of intellect, is manifest from this | Taylor is close; the Greek has "what intellect is like", without "great excellence".

@ch V.1.4 | Intellect, the true world
Admire this world, its size and order, its gods, daemons, animals and plants, then go up to its archetype: all things there are intelligible and everlasting, with pure intellect over them, a life truly under Cronos. It has all things at rest, needs no change, thinks by having, not seeking; it is eternity, which time imitates. Each thing there is intellect and being: intellect makes being exist by thinking it, being gives intellect its thinking. Their cause is another. The first kinds are intellect, being, otherness, sameness, motion and rest; from them number and quality.

@p
One might see this from what follows too. If someone admires this perceptible world, looking at its size and beauty and the order of its everlasting course, and the gods in it, some seen and some unseen, and daemons, and all animals and plants, let him go up to its archetype and the truer, and see there all things intelligible, and everlasting of themselves, in their own understanding and life, and pure intellect presiding over them, and wisdom beyond reach, and the life truly under Cronos, a god who is fullness and intellect. For it holds in itself all the immortals, every intellect, every god, every soul, standing always. Why would it seek to change, when it is well? Where would it go over to, having all things with it? Nor does it seek to grow, being most complete. So all things with it are complete, so that it may be wholly complete, having nothing that is not so, and nothing in it that it does not think; and it thinks not by seeking but by having.
!note Plotinus reads the name Kronos as κόρος, "fullness", "satiety", and νοῦς: intellect full of itself (as in Plato's Cratylus 396b).
!flag and a life which is in reality under Saturn, nourishes there ; Saturn being a God, and a pure intellect | The Greek has "a god who is fullness (κόρος) and intellect": Taylor's "pure" misses the play on Kronos' name.

@p
@greek καὶ τὸ μακάριον αὐτῷ
Its blessedness is not acquired, but all things are in eternity, and it is the real eternity, which time imitates, running round the soul, letting some things go and fastening on others. For there are other and other things again about soul: now Socrates, now a horse, always some one of the beings; but intellect is all. So it has all things in itself standing in the same, and only is, and its "is" is always, and nowhere is there "will be", for then too it is; nor "has been", for nothing there has passed, but it always stands present, since they are the same things, as if content with themselves being so. Each of them is intellect and being, and the whole is all intellect and all being: intellect, by thinking, making being exist, and being, by being thought, giving intellect its thinking and its being.
!flag Conformably to this, Aristotle also, in his Metaphysics says of intellect | Taylor's footnotes (on Aristotle, Metaphysics XII.7, 1072b, "it is active by having", and on a correction of the Greek) are printed into his text in the scan.

@p
@greek τοῦ δὲ νοεῖν αἴτιον ἄλλο
But of thinking the cause is another, which is the cause for being too. So of both at once there is another cause. For they are together and exist together and do not leave each other, but this one is two: intellect and being, thinking and thought; intellect by thinking, being by being thought. For there could be no thinking without otherness, and sameness too. So the first things are intellect, being, otherness and sameness; and we must take motion and rest too: motion if it thinks, rest so that it is the same. Otherness, so that there is thinking and thought; or, if you take away otherness, it will become one and be silent. And the things thought must have otherness towards one another. Sameness, since it is one with itself, and there is something common in all; and their difference is otherness. These, becoming many, make number and quantity; and the quality is the peculiar character of each of these, from which, as principles, come the rest.
!note The five kinds, being, sameness, otherness, motion and rest, are the "greatest kinds" of Plato's Sophist (254d-255e).
!flag in order that it mav be intellective and intelligible | The scan's "mav" is "may".

@ch V.1.5 | Number and the indefinite two
This god, intellect, is many, and the soul, joined to it, asks: who begot it, the simple one before it, the cause of its being many, maker of number? Number is not first: before the two is the One, and the two, indefinite of itself, is defined by the One and becomes number, number as being. Soul too is number. In the intelligible the two and number are rational forms and intellect: the indefinite two is like the substrate, and the number from it and the One are the forms. Intellect is shaped both by the One and by itself, like sight in act.

@p
This god, then, is many, and belongs to this soul, if it is joined to these things, unless it wants to stand off. Coming near it, then, and as it were becoming one, it asks: who, then, begot this, the simple one, and the one before such a thing, the cause of its being and of its being many, the maker of number? For number is not first. Before the two is the One; the two is second, and, born from the One, has it as what defines it, being itself indefinite of itself; when it is defined, it is number already; number as being. And soul too is number. For masses and magnitudes are not the first things: these thick things, which perception thinks are beings, are later. Nor in seeds is the wet what is honourable, but what is not seen: this is number and rational form. So what is called number there, and the two, are rational forms and intellect; the indefinite two, taken as a substrate, as it were, and the number from it and the One each a form, as intellect is shaped by the forms come to be in it. It is shaped in one way by the One, in another by itself, like sight in act; for thinking is a seeing that sees, and both are one.
!note The One and the "indefinite two" are the principles that Aristotle ascribes to Plato's unwritten teaching (Metaphysics I.6, 987b); "soul is number" is Xenocrates' definition, "a number moving itself".
!flag This exuberant God, therefore, exists in the soul which is here | The Greek has "this god is many" (πολύς), many-in-one, not "exuberant".

@ch V.1.6 | How intellect comes from the One
How did the many come from the One, and why did it not stay by itself? Let us call on God, not with words but stretching the soul in prayer, alone to the alone. Set aside coming to be in time. What comes from the One comes without its moving, consenting or willing: a radiance around it, like the sun's light, while it stays. Everything that is complete produces an image of itself, as fire gives heat and perfumes their scent; the always complete produces the everlasting, and less than itself. After the One comes intellect, which sees it and needs it alone; then soul, the expression of intellect.

@p
How, then, does it see, and what, and how in general did it come into existence and come to be from that, so that it sees? For now the soul has the necessity that these things are; but it longs for what was so much talked of by the ancient wise men too: how, from the One, being such as we say the One is, anything got existence, whether multitude or two or number, and that one did not stay by itself, but so great a multitude flowed out, which is seen in the beings, but which we think right to lead back to it. Let it be said thus, then, when we have called on God himself, not with spoken words, but stretching ourselves out with the soul in prayer to him, being able to pray in this way, alone to the alone. The one who would see him, as he is by himself in the inner part, as it were, of a temple, staying quiet beyond all things, must look at what are like the statues already standing towards the outside, or rather at the first statue that has come to light, which has appeared in this way.
!flag since we shall then be able to pray to him properly , when we approach by ourselves alone to the alone | Taylor is close; the Greek has "alone to the alone" (μόνους πρὸς μόνον), the phrase that ends the Enneads (VI.9.11).
!flag and quietly abiding in an eminence beyond all things | The Greek says it is God who stays quiet beyond all things, in the inner part of the temple; Taylor gives this to the beholder.

@p
@greek παντὶ τῷ κινουμένῳ δεῖ
For everything that moves there must be something it moves towards; since there is nothing for that one, let us not suppose it moves; but if something comes to be after it, it must have come to be with that one always turned towards itself. Let coming to be in time be out of our way, when we speak of things that always are; and when in speaking we attach coming to be to them, to show cause and order, we must say that what comes to be from there comes to be without that one's moving. For if something came to be when it moved, what came to be would be third from it, after the motion, not second. So if there is a second after it, it must have come into existence while it stays unmoved, not inclining or willing or moving at all. How, then? And what must we think about it as it stays? A radiance from it, but from it as it stays, like the bright light of the sun running round it, always produced from it while it stays.
!flag in consequence of that prior cause being converted to itself | Taylor is close; the Greek says the second comes to be with the One "always turned towards itself", that is, the One, not "that prior cause" of the moving thing.

@p
@greek καὶ πάντα τὰ ὄντα ἕως μένει
And all beings, while they remain, give out from their own being, necessarily, from the power present in them, an existence around them, towards the outside, hanging from them, an image, as it were, of the archetypes it grew from: fire, the heat from it; and snow does not keep the cold only inside; perfumes above all bear witness to this, for while they are, something goes out from them around them, which what is near enjoys. And all things, when they are complete, produce; what is always complete produces always, and something everlasting; and it produces what is less than itself. What, then, must we say of the most complete? That nothing comes from it but the greatest things after it. The greatest after it is intellect, and second. For intellect sees it and needs it alone; but it needs nothing of this; and what is produced from what is better than intellect is intellect; and intellect is better than all, because the rest are after it: as soul too is an expression of intellect and a kind of activity, as intellect is of that one. But the expression of soul is dim; for as an image of intellect, it must look to intellect; and intellect in the same way to that one, so as to be intellect. It sees it not as separated, but because it is after it and there is nothing between, as there is nothing between soul and intellect. Everything that has produced longs for what it produced, and loves it, especially when they are alone, the producer and the produced; and when the producer is the best, the produced is of necessity with it, so as to be separated only by otherness.
!flag Thus fire emits from itself indeed heat, and snow not only retains cold within itself [but imparts it to other things] | Taylor's bracket is his own, a fair sense of the Greek.
!flag But every thing desires its generator | Taylor has it backwards: the Greek says everything that has produced longs for what it produced (ποθεῖ πᾶν τὸ γεννῆσαν τὸ γεγεννημένον).

@ch V.1.7 | Intellect, the image of the One
Intellect is the One's image, keeping much of it, as light keeps the sun. How does the One produce intellect, not being intellect? Turning to it, intellect saw, and the seeing is intellect. The One is the power of all things; intellect, thinking, splits that power into the things, and defines its own being by it. Because the One is none of all things, all come from it. Intellect produces the beings with itself, the beauty of the forms and the intelligible gods, and keeps them, as Cronos swallowed his children; then, complete, it produces soul, as Cronos fathered Zeus.

@p
We say intellect is its image; for we must speak more clearly. First, because what is produced must in a way be it, and keep much of it, and have a likeness to it, as light to the sun. But that one is not intellect. How, then, does it produce intellect? Because by its turning to it, it saw; and this seeing is intellect. For what grasps something else is either perception or intellect: perception a line, and so on; but the circle is such as to be divided, and this is not so. Here too there is one, but the One is the power of all things. What it is the power of, the thinking, as it were split off from the power, sees; or it would not be intellect. For it has already from itself a kind of awareness of its power, that it can produce being. By itself it defines its own being, by the power from that one; and it sees that its being is as it were one of the parts of what belongs to that one, and from it, and is made strong by it, and completed into being by it and from it. It sees from there, as divided from the undivided, its life and thinking and all things, because that one is none of all things; for in this way all things are from it, because it was not held down by any shape; for it is one alone. And if it were all things, it would be among the beings. That is why it is none of the things in intellect, but all things are from it.
!flag And sense indeed may be com» pared to a line, but the other gnostic powers of the soul to a circle | The Greek is short and hard here: perception grasps a line and the like, but "the circle is such as to be divided"; Taylor's "the other gnostic powers" is his own guess.
!flag For intellect now possesses from itself a co-sensation as it were of the great extent of its power ; in which power, its essence, consists | The Greek says intellect is aware that its power can produce being (οὐσίαν γεννᾶν); "great extent" is not there.
!note "Turning to it" (ἐπιστροφῇ πρὸς αὑτό) can be read "the One's turning to itself" or "intellect's turning to the One"; the Greek allows both.

@p
@greek διὸ καὶ οὐσίαι ταῦτα
That is why these are beings: for each is already defined and has as it were a shape; and being must not be seen in the indefinite, as it were, but fixed by limit and rest; and rest in the intelligibles is definition and shape, by which they get their existence. Of this lineage, then, this intellect is worthy, of the purest, to grow from nowhere but the first principle; and, having come to be, to produce all beings with itself, all the beauty of the forms, all the intelligible gods; and full of what it produced, and as it were swallowing them again, by holding them in itself, so that they do not fall out into matter or are brought up by Rhea; as the mysteries and the myths about the gods riddle, that Cronos, the wisest god, came before Zeus, because he holds again in himself what he produces, by which he is full, intellect in fullness; and after this they say he begot Zeus, being already full. For intellect produces soul, being complete intellect. For being complete it had to produce, and not be barren, being so great a power. But here too what is produced could not be better, but being less, an image of it, indefinite in the same way, but defined by what produced it, and as it were given form.
!flag who was now a boy [i. e. pure and full] | The Greek has κόρον, which Plotinus takes as "fullness" ("being already full"); Taylor reads it as κόρος, "boy". The swallowing of the children is Hesiod's (Theogony 453-467).
!flag and an intellect characterized by purity | The Greek has "intellect in fullness" (νοῦς ἐν κόρῳ), the play on Kronos' name again.

@p
@greek νοῦ δὲ γέννημα λόγος
And the offspring of intellect is an expression and an existence, that which thinks discursively; this is what moves about intellect, the light of intellect and a trace hanging from it, on one side joined to it and so filled and enjoying it and sharing in it and thinking, on the other side touching what comes after it, or rather itself producing what must be worse than soul; of which we must speak later. And the divine things go as far as these.

@ch V.1.8 | Plato and Parmenides had the three
So Plato has his triads: "all things about the king of all, the second about the second, the third about the third". The cause is intellect, his maker, who makes the soul in the bowl, and its father is the Good, beyond intellect and being. These doctrines are not new: they were said of old, not spelled out, and ours interpret them. Parmenides joined being and thinking, and called it one, unmoved, like a sphere; Plato's Parmenides, more exactly, distinguishes the first One, the one-many, and the one and many.

@p
That is why Plato's things are threefold: "all things are about the king of all", he says, the first things, "and the second about the second, and the third about the third". And he speaks of the "father of the cause", meaning by the cause intellect; for intellect is his maker; and he says this makes the soul in that bowl. And of the cause, which is intellect, he says the father is the Good, beyond intellect and "beyond being". In many places he calls being and intellect the form. So Plato knew that intellect is from the Good, and soul from intellect; and these accounts are not new, nor of now, but were said long ago, though not unfolded, and the accounts now are interpreters of those, proving by the evidence of Plato's own writings that these views are old.
!note "All things are about the king of all" is from the Second Letter (312e); the "father of the cause" from the Sixth Letter (323d); "beyond being" from the Republic (509b); the bowl from the Timaeus (41d).
!flag so that from Plato we may know that intellect and idea are from the good | The Greek says Plato knew "intellect from the Good, soul from intellect"; "and idea" is Taylor's.

@p
@greek ἥπτετο μὲν οὖν καὶ Παρμενίδης
Parmenides before him touched on such a view, as far as he brought being and intellect into the same, and did not set being among the perceptibles. "For thinking and being are the same", he says; and he calls this unmoved, though adding thinking, taking all bodily motion away from it so that it stays the same; and he likens it to the mass of a sphere, because it holds all things embraced, and because its thinking is not outside but in itself. Calling it one in his own writings, he was open to blame, as this one is found to be many. But Parmenides in Plato, speaking more exactly, distinguishes from one another the first One, which is more strictly one; and a second, which he calls one-many; and a third, one and many. And so he too agrees with the three natures.
!note "Thinking and being are the same" is Parmenides' fragment 3; the sphere is fragment 8.43. The three ones are the first three "hypotheses" of Plato's Parmenides (137c, 144e, 155e), as the Neoplatonists read them.
!flag When, likewise, in his writings he calls it one, he alludes to the cause of it | The Greek says he "was open to blame" (αἰτίαν εἶχεν), because his one turns out to be many; Taylor reads αἰτία as "cause".

@ch V.1.9 | The other ancients
Anaxagoras' pure, unmixed intellect makes the first simple and the One separate, but he was too early to be exact. Heraclitus knew the One everlasting and intelligible; with Empedocles, love is the One. Aristotle makes the first separate and intelligible, but by making it think itself he makes it not first; and his many movers of the spheres are either from one, and so an intelligible world, or many principles by chance. The Pythagoreans and Pherecydes had this nature in view, some in writing, some in unwritten teaching.

@p
Anaxagoras, saying intellect is pure and unmixed, himself too sets the first as simple and the One as separate, but left out exactness because of his early date. Heraclitus too knows the One as everlasting and intelligible; for bodies are always coming to be and flowing. With Empedocles strife divides, and love is the One, and this too he makes bodiless; and the elements are as matter. Aristotle later makes the first separate and intelligible, but by saying it thinks itself he makes it not first; and making many other intelligibles, as many as there are spheres in the heaven, so that each moves each, he speaks of the things in the intelligible otherwise than Plato, setting down a necessity he does not have good reason for. One might stop to ask whether even with reason; for it is more reasonable that all, contributing to one ordering, should look to one and the first.
!note Anaxagoras' intellect "unmixed" is fragment 12; Aristotle's thought thinking itself and the movers of the spheres are in Metaphysics XII.7-9 and XII.8.
!flag Heraclitus, also, knew an eternal and intelligible one. For he says, that bodies are always rising into existence | The Greek does not say Heraclitus "says" this; it is Plotinus' reason, that bodies always come to be and flow.

@p
@greek ζητήσειε δ’ ἄν τις τὰ πολλὰ
One might ask whether, for him, the many intelligibles are from one, the first, or whether the principles in the intelligibles are many. If from one, it will plainly be by analogy with the spheres in the perceptible, one embracing another, and one, the outermost, ruling; so that the first there too would embrace, and there will be an intelligible world; and as here the spheres are not empty, but the first is full of stars and the others have stars, so there too the movers will have many things in them, and things truer there. If each is a principle, the principles will be by chance; and why will they be together and agree on one work, the harmony of the whole heaven? And how are the perceptible things in heaven equal to the intelligibles and movers? And how are they many in this way, being bodiless, with no matter to separate them? So of the ancients, those who kept most to the teachings of Pythagoras and his followers, and of Pherecydes, held to this nature; but some worked it out in their own writings, others not in writings, but showed it in unwritten meetings, or left it altogether.

@ch V.1.10 | The three are in us too
Beyond being is the One; next, being and intellect; third, soul. As they are in nature, so they are in us, not in the perceptible part but outside it, in what Plato calls the inner man. Our soul is divine, perfect when it has intellect; its reasoning part needs no bodily organ and should be placed among the first intelligibles, outside place. So Plato set soul around the world from outside, and our head above the heaven; and the separation he urges is not in place but in not inclining to body.

@p
That we must think it is so, that there is the One beyond being, such as the argument wanted to show, as far as it was possible to show about these things, and that next there is being and intellect, and third the nature of soul, has now been shown. As these three, as said, are in nature, so we must think they are with us too. I mean not in the perceptible things (for these are separate), but in what is outside perceptibles, and outside in the same way, as those are outside the whole heaven, so too the things of man, what Plato calls the inner man. Our soul too is something divine and of another nature, as is all the nature of soul; and the soul that has intellect is complete; and intellect is one that reasons, and another that makes reasoning possible. The reasoning part of the soul, needing no bodily organ for reasoning, but having its activity in purity, so that it can reason purely too, one would not be wrong to set, separate and unmixed with body, in the first intelligible. For we must not look for a place to settle it, but make it outside all place.
!note "The inner man" is from the Republic (589a).

@p
@greek οὕτω γὰρ τὸ καθ̓ αὑτὸ
For so it is by itself and outside and immaterial, when it is alone, having nothing from the nature of body. That is why he says of the universe that "he set the soul around it, and outside it too", showing the part of the soul that stays in the intelligible; and of us he said that we lift up our head to the top. And the bidding to separate is not said in place (for this is separated by nature), but in not inclining, and in our images, and in being estranged from the body, if one might lead up the rest of the form of soul too and bring it together to what is above, and the part of it settled here, which alone crafts and shapes the body and is busy about it.
!note "Around it, and outside it too" is from the Timaeus (34b, 36e); the head lifted up from the Phaedrus (248a); the bidding to separate soul from body from the Phaedo (67c).
!flag Concerning our soul, likewise, concealing his meaning, he says, in the Phaedrus, that it sometimes hides its head in the heavens, and sometimes elevates it beyond them | The Greek says only that "we lift our head to the top"; the rest is Taylor's.
!flag He also indicates that we should elevate the remaining [i. e. the irrational] form of the soul | Taylor's bracket is his own; and "should likewise be engaged in an employment of this kind" is not in the Greek, which ends on the part that shapes the body and is busy about it.

@ch V.1.11 | The just itself, and God, in us
The soul reasons whether this is just or that beautiful, so there must be something just that stands still, from which reasoning comes. Since the soul does not always reason, there must be in us an intellect that always has the just, and also the principle and cause of intellect, God: not divided, staying, seen in each thing able to receive it, as the centre is in itself while each radius has a point in it. By this in us we touch it and hang from it, and settle there when we incline to it.

@p
Since there is a soul that reasons about just and beautiful things, and reasoning that asks whether this is just and this beautiful, there must also be something just that stands still, from which reasoning comes about soul. Or how would it reason? And if the soul sometimes reasons about these things and sometimes not, there must be in us not the reasoning intellect, but one that always has the just; and there must be the principle and cause of intellect, God, not divided, but staying; and, not staying in a place, it is seen again in many, in each of those able to receive it, as another self; as the centre is by itself, but each of the radii of the circle has a point in it, and the lines bring their own to it. For by such a thing of what is in us we too touch it and are with it and hang from it; and we are settled in it, those of us who incline towards it.
!flag They receive him, however, as something different from themselves | The Greek says God is seen in each thing able to receive him "as another self", as if each had him for its own.

@ch V.1.12 | Why we do not notice them
If we have such great things, why do we not notice them? They are always active, intellect and what is before intellect, and soul's ever-moving part; but what is in the soul reaches us only when it comes to perception, and we are with the perceiving part, not the whole soul. To grasp them we must turn our attention inward, as one who waits for a voice he wants to hear sets the others aside and listens: we must keep the soul's power of grasping pure and ready to hear the sounds from above.

@p
How is it, then, that having such great things we do not grasp them, but are idle in such activities for the most part, and some are not active in them at all? Those are always in their own activities, intellect and what is before intellect, always in itself; and to soul belongs its ever-moving in this way. For not everything in the soul is at once perceived, but it comes to us when it comes into perception; and when each active thing does not pass itself on to the perceiving part, it has not yet gone through the whole soul. So we do not yet know it, being with the perceiving part, and being not a portion of soul but the whole soul. And further, each of the soul's powers, living always, is always active by itself at its own work; but knowing comes when there is passing on and grasping.
!flag and are not a part of the soul, but the whole soul | Taylor is right; the Greek says it is because "we" are the whole soul, not just its highest part, that we notice only what reaches perception.
!flag each of the psychical animals in us | The Greek has "each of the things of the soul" (τῶν ψυχικῶν), its powers, not "animals".

@p
@greek δεῖ τοίνυν, εἰ τῶν οὕτω
If, then, there is to be a grasp of what is present in this way, what grasps must turn inward and fix its attention there; as if someone, waiting to hear a voice he wants, standing away from other voices, wakes his ear to the best of what can be heard, for when it comes; so here too, letting go of perceptible hearing, except as far as is necessary, one must keep the soul's power of grasping pure and ready to hear the sounds from above.

@treatise V.2 | On the generation and order of things after the first
A short treatise on the going out of all things from the One. The One is all things and none of them: because nothing is in it, all comes from it. Perfect, it overflowed, and what came from it turned back and looked to it, and so became being and intellect. Intellect in turn poured out power, and soul came to be; soul, moving, produced its image, perception and the nature in plants. Each stays in its own seat while what it produces takes a lower rank, so that all is like one long life stretched out, each part different, nothing cut off.

@ch V.2.1 | The One overflows
The One is all things and not one of them: their principle, not they. How does the varied come from what is simple? Because nothing is in it, all comes from it; to produce being it is not being. Perfect, needing nothing, it overflowed; what came to be turned to it, was filled, and looking at it became intellect: its standing towards the One made being, its looking made intellect. Intellect poured out power in turn, and soul came to be while intellect stayed; soul, moving, produced its image, perception and the nature in plants, nothing cut off from what is before it.

@p
The One is all things and not even one of them; for the principle of all things is not all things, but all things are its; for there they have, as it were, run back; or rather they are not yet, but will be. How, then, from a simple one, with no variety showing in it, no doubleness of any kind? Because there was nothing in it, all things are from it; and so that being might be, it itself is not being, but begetter of it; and this is, as it were, the first begetting. For, being perfect, by seeking nothing and having nothing and needing nothing, it overflowed, as it were, and its overfullness has made something else. What came to be turned to it and was filled and came to be looking to it, and so is intellect. Its standing towards that made being, and its looking at it made intellect. Since it stood towards it in order to see, it becomes intellect and being at once.
!flag but the one is all, because all things run as it were into it | Taylor's "the one is all" is not in the Greek, which says all things "are its" (ἐκείνης), belong to the principle.
!flag and was generated looking to it. But this is intellect | The Greek says it "came to be looking to it, and so is intellect": the looking is what makes it intellect.

@p
@greek οὕτως οὖν ὢν οἷον ἐκεῖνος
Being, then, as that one is, it makes the like, pouring out much power; and this too is a form of it, as what was before it poured out before. And this activity from its being is soul, which came to be while that stayed; for intellect too came to be while what is before it stayed. But soul makes not staying; moving, it produced an image. Looking there, where it came from, it is filled; going out into another, opposite motion, it produces an image of itself, perception, and the nature in plants. Nothing is detached or cut off from what is before it. That is why the soul of man too seems to reach as far as plants; for in a way it does reach, since what is in plants is its. Not all of it is in plants; but, coming to be in plants, it is so because it went out so far downward, making another existence by its going out, and by its eagerness for the worse; since the part before this, hanging from intellect, lets intellect stay by itself.
!flag Hence, being in the same manner as the one produces things similar to itself | Taylor makes the One the subject; the Greek says intellect, being "as that one is", makes the like.
!flag permits intellect to abide in itself | Taylor is close; the Greek says the higher soul lets intellect stay "by itself" (ἐφ’ ἑαυτοῦ), undisturbed.

@ch V.2.2 | A long life stretched out
Each thing stays in its seat while what it produces takes a lower rank, and each becomes the same as what it follows, while it follows. In a plant the boldest, least sensible part of soul has come this far; in a beast perception rules; in man reason or intellect. Cut a branch or burn a root, and the soul there goes back to its source, not to another place, since none of these are in place. All of this is that one and not that one. It is like one long life stretched out, each part other, all continuous.

@p
It goes out, then, from the principle to the last, each being always left in its own seat, and what is produced taking another, worse rank; but each becomes the same as what it follows, as long as it follows. When soul comes to be in a plant, it is as if another part in the plant, the boldest and least sensible, which has come out so far; when in an unreasoning animal, the power of perceiving has won and brought it; when into a man, the motion is either wholly in the reasoning part, or from intellect, since it has an intellect of its own and of itself a will to think, or in general to move. Let us turn back again: when someone cuts off the shoots of a plant, or the tops of its branches, where has the soul in them gone? To where it came from; for it did not stand off in place. It is one, then, with the principle. If you cut out or burn the root, where is what was in the root? In soul, which has not gone into another place. But even if it is in the same place, it is in another, if it ran back; if not, it is in another plant soul, for it is not cramped; if it ran back, it is in the power before it.
!flag When, therefore, soul [i. e. the human soul] is ingenerated in a plant | Taylor's bracket is his own, from the Neoplatonists he quotes in his footnote: that the rational soul never becomes the soul of a plant or beast. The Greek says only "soul".
!flag where is the life which was in the root ? | The Greek has "what was in the root", the soul's part, not "the life".

@p
@greek ἀλλ’ ἐκείνη ποῦ
But where is that? In the one before it; and that up to intellect, not in place, for none of it was in place; and intellect much more is not in place, so not this either. Being nowhere, then, but in what is nowhere, it is also in this way everywhere. If, going out upward, it stopped in between before coming wholly to the highest, it has a middle life, and stands in that part of itself. All these are that one and not that one: that one, because from it; not that one, because it gave, staying by itself. So it is like a long life stretched out in length, each of its parts in turn other, but the whole continuous with itself, and other and other by difference, the earlier not lost in the second. What, then, of the soul that has come to be in plants? Does it produce nothing? It produces that in which it is. But how, we must consider, taking another starting point.
!flag In the power prior to presiding dcemons attend us in their beneficent operations | Taylor's long footnote (from Olympiodorus, on Syrianus and Proclus) is printed into his sentence in the scan, which goes on, after it, "itself".
!flag Intellect, however, is all these, and yet it is not | The Greek has "that one" (ἐκεῖνος), the first principle; Taylor makes it intellect.
!flag Hence, there is an abundant life in the universe, which is as it were extended into length | The Greek has "a long life stretched out in length" (ζωὴ μακρὰ), not "abundant life in the universe".

@treatise V.3 | On the knowing hypostases, and what is beyond
Must what thinks itself be complex, seeing the rest of itself with one part, or can the simple think itself? Plotinus begins with the soul: its perceiving part looks outward, its reasoning part judges by rules it has from intellect, and we are this reasoning part, with perception our messenger and intellect our king. True self-knowledge belongs to intellect, in which seeing and seen, thinking and thought, are one; we know it by becoming it. But intellect, being two in one, cannot be first: before it is the One, which does not think, is not any of the things that are, and is known only by a touch beyond thought. All that we say of it says what it is not; we are like people possessed, who know there is something greater in them without knowing what. The treatise ends with the cry: "Take away everything."
