@ch V.3.1 | Can the simple think itself?
Must what thinks itself be varied, seeing the rest of itself by one part, as if the wholly simple could not turn to itself? If it thinks itself because it is composite, seeing the rest by one part, it does not really think itself: one thing sees another. We cannot give up the view that something truly thinks itself: intellect would be absurd if it knew all else and not itself. Perception knows the outside; intellect knows the intelligibles. Does it know itself as well, and how?

@p
Must what thinks itself be varied, so that, seeing the rest with some one of the things in it, it may be said in this way to think itself, as if what is wholly simple could not turn to itself and its own understanding? Or can what is not composite too have thought of itself? For what is said to think itself because it is composite, because with one of the things in it it thinks the others, as if we grasped by perception our own shape and the other nature of the body, would not have the true thinking of itself; for the whole would not be known in such a case, unless that which thought the others with it had also thought itself; and we shall have not what we are seeking, the same thinking itself, but one thinking another. We must, then, grant understanding of itself to the simple too, and see how, if it is possible; or give up the view that something really thinks itself.
!flag just as if by sense we should apprehend the form * of our- s The word used here by Plotinus is μορφή | Taylor's footnote on μορφή is printed into his text in the scan.

@p
@greek ἀποστῆναι μὲν οὖν τῆς δόξης
To give up this view is not quite possible, since many absurdities follow. For even if we did not give this to the soul, as being quite absurd, not to give it to the nature of intellect would be wholly absurd, if it has knowledge of other things but will not be in knowledge and understanding of itself. For perception, not intellect, will grasp what is outside, and, if you like, discursive thought and opinion; whether intellect has knowledge of these we must consider; but the intelligibles, plainly, intellect will know. Will it know them only, or itself too, which will know them? And will it know itself in this way, that it knows them only, but not know who it is? Will it know that it knows what is its, but no longer who it is that knows? Or will it know both what is its and itself? And what the manner is, and how far, we must consider.
!flag For if we do not admit that to assign this power to the soul is not very absurd | Taylor's double negative muddles the Greek, which says that even if we did not give self-knowledge to the soul, "as being quite absurd", not to give it to intellect would be wholly absurd.

@ch V.3.2 | Does the soul know itself?
First the soul. Its perceiving part knows only what is outside, even when it perceives the body's affections. Its reasoning part judges the images from perception, joins and divides them, and looks at the imprints coming from intellect, fitting new ones to old: these are the soul's recollections. Does this part turn to itself and know itself? If so, it is intellect, and we must ask how it differs from intellect above; if not, we must go up to that.

@p
But first we must inquire about the soul, whether we must grant it knowledge of itself, and what it is in it that knows, and how. Its perceiving part we would say at once is only of what is outside; for even if there is awareness of what happens inside in the body, the grasp is of what is outside it there too, since it perceives the affections in the body by itself. The reasoning part in it makes its judgment from the images from perception set before it, and joins and divides; or it looks also at what comes from intellect, the imprints, as it were, and has the same power about these too; and it further takes on understanding, as if recognising, and fitting the new and lately arrived imprints to those in it from long ago: which we would call the soul's recollections. And the soul's intellect stops here in its power.
!flag surveys the impressions as it were which are derived from intellect, and possesses about these the same power [as intellect] | Taylor's bracket is wrong: the Greek says the reasoning part has the same power about the imprints from intellect as it has about the images from perception.

@p
@greek ἢ καὶ εἰς ἑαυτὸ στρέφεται
Or does it also turn to itself and know itself? Or must this be taken up to intellect? For if we give knowledge of itself to this part, we shall say it is intellect, and ask how it will differ from the one above; if we do not give it, we shall come to that one going step by step in our argument, and consider what "itself thinking itself" is. And if we give it here too, in the lower, we shall consider what the difference of thinking itself is; for if there is none, this is already unmixed intellect. Does this thinking part of the soul, then, turn to itself as well? No: it has the understanding of the imprints it receives from both sides. And how it has the understanding we must first inquire.
!flag And if we grant that this is also the province of the reasoning power, we must investigate what the dif- | Taylor's chapter, as the scan gives it, breaks off here, in the middle of a word.

@ch V.3.3 | We are the reasoning part
Perception sees a man and gives the imprint to thought, which knows him and, with memory, says "Socrates"; asked whether he is good, it judges by a rule of the good it has from intellect shining on it. It is still soul, for soul is in reasonings. Pure intellect is ours and not ours: above the reasoning part, used and not used. We are the reasoning part, the middle between perception below and intellect above; perception is always ours, intellect is disputed, because it does not lean towards us, but we look up to it. Perception is our messenger, intellect our king.

@p
Perception saw a man and gave the imprint to thought. What does thought say? It will say nothing yet, but only knew, and stopped; unless it asks itself "who is this?", if it has met him before, and says, using memory, that it is Socrates. If it unfolds his shape, it divides what imagination gave; if it says whether he is good, it has spoken from what it knew through perception, but what it said about them it would have from itself, having a rule of the good in itself. How does it have the good in itself? It is of the form of the good, and has been strengthened for perceiving such a thing by intellect shining on it; for this pure part of the soul receives the traces of intellect laid on it. Why is this not intellect, and the rest, starting from perception, soul? Because soul must be in reasonings, and all these are the works of a reasoning power. But why do we not give this part thinking of itself and be done? Because we gave it the looking at what is outside and the busying with it, and we think it right for intellect to look at what is its own and in itself.
!flag Perhaps it does not yet say any thing, but only knows that it is a man | Taylor adds "that it is a man"; the Greek says only that thought "knew and stopped".

@p
@greek ἀλλ’ εἴ τις φήσει
But if someone says, "What prevents this from looking at what is its own by another power?", he is not looking for the thinking or the reasoning part, but takes pure intellect. What, then, prevents pure intellect from being in the soul? Nothing, we shall say. But must we still say it is the soul's? We shall not say it is the soul's, but we shall say it is our intellect, other than what thinks discursively and gone up above it, but still ours, even if we do not count it with the parts of the soul; or it is ours and not ours. That is why we use it and do not use it, but thought always; and it is ours when we use it, not ours when we do not. What is using it? Is it ourselves becoming that, and speaking as that one, or in accordance with it? For we are not intellect. In accordance with it, then, by the reasoning part, which receives it first.
!flag But what is it to use a pure intellect? Is it to pronounce ourselves as becoming either such an intellect, or conformable to | Taylor's long footnote (from Proclus, on the partial intellect and the daemons) is printed into his sentence in the scan, which goes on, after it, "it? For we are not intellect."

@p
@greek καὶ γὰρ αἰσθανόμεθα δἰ αἰσθήσεως
For we perceive through perception, and we are the ones who perceive. Do we think discursively in this way too? We are the ones who reason, and we ourselves think the thoughts in discursive thought; for this is what we are; but the activities of intellect come from above in the way those of perception come from below; we are this, the sovereign part of the soul, in the middle of a double power, a worse and a better, the worse being perception, the better intellect. Perception seems to be always agreed to be ours, for we always perceive; but intellect is disputed, both because we do not always use it and because it is separate; separate in that it does not lean towards us, but we rather towards it, looking upward. Perception is our messenger, but that is our king.
!flag Are we therefore thus dianoetically perceived, and do we thus dianoetically energize ? | The Greek asks only whether we think discursively "in this way too", through something else, as we perceive through perception.

@ch V.3.4 | Two ways of knowing ourselves
We are kings too when we are in accordance with intellect, by its laws written in us, or filled with it, or seeing it present. So knowing oneself is double: knowing the nature of discursive thought, or becoming that intellect and thinking oneself not as a man but as something else, carried up with the best part of the soul. Discursive thought knows it is from intellect and its image, with all things written in it; but to know intellect thinking itself we must become intellect, letting go the rest and seeing it by itself.

@p
We too are kings, when we are in accordance with it. In accordance with it in two ways: either by what are like letters, laws written in us, or by being as it were filled with it, or able to see and perceive it present. And we know ourselves by learning the rest by such a sight; or by learning the power that knows such a thing by the power itself; or by becoming that, so that the one who knows himself is double: one knowing the nature of the soul's discursive thought, the other above this, knowing himself in accordance with that intellect, becoming it; and by it thinking himself again, not as a man any longer, but become altogether another, and snatching himself up to the height, drawing up only the better part of the soul, which alone can grow wings for thinking, so that one may deposit there what he saw.
!flag either because letters as it were, which are as laws, are inscribed in us by intellect ; or be1- cause we are filled with it ; or when it is present | Taylor is close; the Greek gives two ways, the second divided into being filled with it or seeing it present.

@p
@greek τὸ δὴ διανοητικὸν ὅτι
Does the thinking part, then, not know that it is the thinking part, and that it takes understanding of what is outside, and that it judges what it judges by the rules in it which it has from intellect, and that there is something better than it, which does not seek but surely has everything? But does it not know what it is itself, knowing what it is like and what its works are like? If, then, it says that it is from intellect, and second after intellect, and an image of intellect, having everything in it as if written, as there the writer and the one who wrote, will one who knows himself so stop at this? And shall we, using another power, look on intellect knowing itself; or, sharing in that, since it is ours and we are its, shall we in this way know intellect and ourselves? This is necessary, if we are to know what "itself thinking itself" is in intellect. One has become intellect when, letting go of the rest of oneself, one sees this by this, and oneself by oneself: as intellect, then, sees itself.
!flag he beholds intellect through intellect, and by it also surveys himself | Taylor is close; the Greek says one sees this by this, and "oneself by oneself".

@ch V.3.5 | In intellect, thinking and thought are one
Does intellect see one part of itself with another? Then seer and seen are different, and it does not see itself whole; and who would divide it? If it has the things it sees, not images of them, it had them before any division, and the seeing is the seen. Otherwise there is no truth, only imprints other than the beings. So intellect, the intelligible and being are one; the intelligible is an activity, the first, and the activity is intellect's being. Thinking, thought and intellect are all one, so intellect thinks itself.

@p
Does it, then, see with one part of itself another part of itself? But in this way one will be seeing and the other seen; and this is not itself seeing itself. What, then, if all of it is of like parts, so that the seer does not differ from the seen? So, seeing that part of itself which is the same as itself, it would see itself; for the seer does not differ from the seen. First, the division of itself is absurd: how will it divide? Not by chance. And who is the divider? The one who puts himself among the seeing, or among the seen? Then how will the seer know himself, having put himself among the seen by his seeing? For the seeing was not in the seen. Or, knowing himself so, as seen and not as seeing, he will not know all of himself, nor the whole; for what he saw he saw as seen, not as seeing; and so he will have seen another, not himself. Or he will add from himself the one who has seen, so as to have thought himself completely. But if the one who has seen, then together the things seen too.

@p
@greek εἰ οὖν ἐν τῇ θεωρίᾳ
If, then, the things seen are in the seeing, if imprints of them, it does not have them themselves; if it has them themselves, it does not have them by seeing them from dividing itself, but was, before dividing itself, both seeing and having. If so, the seeing must be the same as the seen, and intellect the same as the intelligible; for if not the same, there will be no truth; for what has the beings will have an imprint other than the beings, which is not truth. Truth, then, must be not of another, but what it says, that it must also be. So in this way intellect and the intelligible and being are one, and this is the first being; and the first intellect has the beings, or rather is the same as the beings. But if thinking and the intelligible are one, how will what thinks think itself by this? For thinking will, as it were, embrace the intelligible, or be the same as the intelligible, but it is not yet plain that intellect thinks itself.

@p
@greek ἀλλ’ εἰ ἡ νόησις καὶ τὸ νοητὸν
But if thinking and the intelligible are the same (for the intelligible is an activity; for it is not potential, nor without life, nor is life brought in, nor thinking, for something else, like a stone or something soulless; and the intelligible is the first being): if, then, it is an activity, the first and most beautiful activity, it would be thinking, a thinking that is being; for it is the truest. And thinking of this kind, being first, and first intellect, would be the first intellect; for this intellect is not potential, nor is it one thing and its thinking another; for so again its being would be potential. If, then, it is activity, and its being is activity, it would be one and the same as the activity; and being and the intelligible are one with the activity; so all will be one at once, intellect, thinking, the intelligible. If, then, its thinking is the intelligible, and the intelligible is itself, it will think itself: it will think by the thinking, which is itself, and think the intelligible, which is itself. It will think itself both ways, as the thinking was itself and as the intelligible was itself, which it thought by the thinking, which was itself.
!flag Since, however, being and the intelligible are one and the same with energy | Taylor is right; but his sentence after it, "If, therefore, the intelligence of it is the intelligible, but it is the intelligible", leaves out the step that the intelligible is intellect itself.

@ch V.3.6 | Proof, persuasion, and the soul
The argument has shown that something thinks itself strictly; soul thinks itself as another's, intellect as itself. But argument gives necessity, not persuasion, which belongs to soul. While we were above in intellect we were content; now we want to see the archetype in an image. So let the thinking part learn that it knows what it sees and says, by fitting what comes from above to the traces in it, and carry the image over to true intellect, which is the same as its objects and cannot be outside itself. Free of action and desire, its life is to know itself.

@p
The argument, then, has shown that there is something that strictly thinks itself. It thinks in one way when it is in soul, more strictly in intellect. For the soul thought itself as belonging to another, but intellect as itself, and what kind, and who, and from its own nature, turning to itself. For seeing the beings it saw itself, and seeing it was in act, and the activity was itself; for intellect and thinking are one; and it sees whole by whole, not one part by another part. Has the argument shown it to be such as to have a persuasive force? It has necessity, but not persuasion; for necessity is in intellect, persuasion in soul. We seem to be seeking rather to be persuaded than to see the truth by pure intellect. For as long as we were above, in the nature of intellect, we were content, and thought, and saw, bringing all into one; for it was intellect that thought and spoke about itself, and the soul kept quiet, giving way to intellect's activity. But since we have come to be here, and in soul again, we seek for some persuasion to come about, as if wanting to see the archetype in an image.
!flag For soul, indeed, knows itself, that it is the progeny of another thing ; but intellect knows that it is from itself | The Greek says intellect knows itself "as itself", not "that it is from itself".

@p
@greek ἴσως οὖν χρὴ τὴν ψυχὴν
Perhaps, then, we must teach our soul how intellect sees itself; teach this to that part of the soul which is intellectual in a way, calling it the thinking part (διανοητικόν) and hinting by the name that it is a kind of intellect, or that it has its power through intellect and from intellect. It is fitting, then, for this to know that it too knows what it sees and knows what it says; and if it were what it says, it would know itself in this way. But since what it sees is above, or comes to it from there, from where it too comes, it would follow for it too, being a rational form and taking in what is akin and fitting it to the traces in itself, to know itself so. Let it carry the image over to the true intellect, then, which was the same as the true things thought, really being and first; and see that such a one cannot be outside itself; so that, if it is in itself and with itself, and this which it is, is intellect (and intellect could never be without thinking), the knowledge of itself must be with it, and that it is in itself, and that its work and being are nothing but to be intellect only.
!flag how its intellect once beheld itself | The Greek says "how intellect sees itself"; "once" and "its" are not there.

@p
@greek οὐ γὰρ δὴ πρακτικός
For this is not a practical intellect; for the practical, looking outside and not staying in itself, there would be a knowledge of what is outside, but no necessity, if it were wholly practical, to know itself. But for one that has no action (for there is no desire in pure intellect, since nothing is absent), its turning to itself shows that its knowledge of itself is not only reasonable but necessary. For what would its life be, freed from action and being in intellect?

@ch V.3.7 | Intellect's activity is turned to itself
If intellect sees God, it must know itself too, since it knows what it has from him, and it is itself what he gave. Its rest is not standing outside itself but its own activity, free of other things; where being is activity and there is nothing else for it to be directed to, the activity is directed to itself. What comes from it comes as fire warms from the fire within it. Soul is like intellect inwardly and, even when it acts and makes, still contemplates, so all things are traces of intellect, the last keeping only a faint image.

@p
But, we might say, it contemplates God. Yet if someone agrees that it knows God, he will be forced to grant in this way too that it knows itself. For it will know all it has from him, and what he gave, and what he has power to do. And learning and knowing these it will know itself in this way too; for it is itself one of the things given, or rather it is all the things given. If, then, it knows him and his powers, learning them it will also know itself, as having come from there and carrying off what it has power for; and if it cannot see him clearly, since the seeing is perhaps the very thing seen, in this way above all it would be left to it to see and know itself, if the seeing is being the very thing seen. For what else could we give it? Rest, by Zeus. But for intellect rest is not standing out of intellect; the rest of intellect is an activity at leisure from other things: since for other things too, which have rest from others, their own proper activity is left, and most of all for those whose being is not potential but actual. Its being, then, is activity, and there is nothing to which the activity is directed: it is directed to itself. Thinking itself, then, it holds its activity so, towards itself and into itself. For if anything comes from it, that is by its activity being into itself, in itself. It had to be in itself first, then towards another, or something else had to come from it made like it: as with fire too, being first fire in itself and having the activity of fire, it is so able to make a trace of itself in another. For again, intellect is activity in itself, but soul is, in the part of it towards intellect, as it were within, and in the part outside intellect, towards what is outside. In the one way it is made like that from which it comes; in the other, though made unlike, it is still made like it here too, whether it acts or makes: for even in acting it still contemplates, and in making it makes forms, like completed thoughts, so that all things are traces of thought and intellect going forth after their archetype and imitating it, those near it more, the last keeping a faint image.
!flag For divinity is one of the things | The Greek says that intellect itself is one of the things given, or rather all of them; Taylor makes "divinity" the subject.
!flag The quiet of intellect however, is not mental alienation | "Standing out" (ἔκστασις) means intellect leaving itself; Taylor's "mental alienation" hides the play on words.

@ch V.3.8 | Light that sees itself
What does intellect see? Not colour or shape: the intelligibles come before these, and even the forming principles in seeds are invisible. The soul that holds such principles does not see them, being an image, but in intellect seeing and seen are one, as sight here, united with light, sees light. That light shining in soul makes it intellectual and like itself, and turns it to itself. Imagine its trace in soul, greater and clearer, and you come near intellect, which we know through itself. So the soul, seeing itself as its image, can see it in some way.

@p
What kind of thing does intellect see the intelligible to be, and what kind of thing itself? With the intelligible we need not even look for something like the colour or shape on bodies; for before these exist, those are. And the forming principle in the seeds that make these things is not these things; for these too are invisible by nature, and those still more; and there is the same nature of those and of what has them, as of the principle in the seed and the soul that has these things. But the soul does not see what it has. For it did not generate them either; it is itself an image, and so are the principles; but that from which it came is the clear, the true, the primary, from which it is also of itself and for itself; while this, unless it comes to belong to another and to be in another, does not even last: for an image, being of another, belongs to come to be in another, unless it hangs from that other. So it does not see either, as not having enough light; and if it does see, perfected in another, it sees another and not itself. But there is none of this there: there seeing and its visible are together, and the visible is like the seeing, and the seeing like the visible. Who, then, will say what it is like? The one who has seen; and intellect sees. For here too sight, being light, or rather united with light, sees light: for it sees colours; but there it sees not through another, but through itself, because there is nothing outside. With one light, then, it sees another light, not through another. Light, then, sees another light: it sees itself, then.

@p
@greek τὸ δὲ φῶς τοῦτο ἐν ψυχῇ
And this light, shining in the soul, lit it up, that is, made it intellectual, that is, made it like itself, the light above. Then, thinking of it as being like the trace of light that has come to be in the soul, and still more beautiful and greater and clearer, you would come near the nature of intellect and the intelligible. And again, this light, shining on it, gave the soul a clearer life, but not a generative life; on the contrary, it turned the soul to itself and did not let it scatter, but made it content with the splendour in it; nor a perceptive life, for that looks outside and perceives more; but one who has taken that light of the true things sees, so to speak, the visible things not more, but the opposite. It is left, then, that it has taken on an intellectual life, a trace of the life of intellect; for the true things are there. The life and activity in intellect is the first light, shining first for itself and a radiance turned to itself, shining and shone upon at once, the truly intelligible, thinking and thought, seen by itself and needing nothing else in order to see, enough for itself for seeing: for what it sees it is itself; known by us too through that very thing, so that our knowledge of it also comes through it; or from where should we have the means to speak of it? It is such as to grasp itself more clearly, and us through it; and by such reasonings our soul too is led up to it, setting itself down as its image, so that its life is a likeness and an image of it, and when it thinks it becomes godlike and intellect-like. And if anyone asks it what that intellect is like, the perfect and complete, knowing itself first, it should first come to be in intellect, or give way to intellect's activity, and, keeping in itself the memory of the things it had, show itself as having them, so that through itself, being an image, it can see that one somehow, through what in it has been made more exactly like that one, as far as a part of soul can come to likeness with intellect.
!flag by conceiving supernal light | Taylor's text as the scan gives it loses several lines here, running from "by conceiving supernal light" straight into the end of a later sentence; the Greek's clearer, turning life of the soul is missing.

@ch V.3.9 | The ascent through soul
To know what intellect is, look at the soul and its most divine part. Strip away the body, the soul that shapes it, perception, desires and angers: what is left is the image of intellect, a light around it, as the sun's light surrounds the sun, but this light is not in another and needs no place. Soul must reason about intellect, but intellect is always present to itself. If you cannot hold the purely thinking soul, take opinion and climb from there; if not that, perception, or even go down to the generative power and its works, and climb from the last forms to the first.

@p
One who is to know what intellect is must, then, as it seems, look closely at the soul and the most divine part of soul. This might come about in this way too: if you take away first the body from the man, and plainly from yourself, then the soul that shapes it, and perception too, and desires and angers and the other such nonsense, since they lean so much to the mortal. What is left of the soul is this, which we said was an image of intellect, keeping some of its light, as with the sun the light shining around its great sphere from it. Now no one would grant that the sun's light is by itself around the sun, sprung from it and staying around it, while one light always comes forth from another before it, until it reaches us and the earth: he will place all of it, even what is around the sun, in something else, so as not to leave the space after the sun empty of body. But the soul, having come from intellect as a light around it, hangs from it, and is not in another, but around it; nor has it a place, for intellect has none either. So the sun's light is in air, but soul is pure, so that it is seen by itself, both by itself and by another soul of the same kind. And it must reason about intellect, what it is like, looking from itself; but intellect does not reason about itself: for it is always present to itself, and we are when we go to it; for our life is divided, and there are many lives, but it needs no other life or others, but the lives it provides it provides to others, not to itself; for it needs nothing worse, nor does it provide itself with the less when it has the all, nor have the traces when it has the first things, or rather it does not have them but is them. But if someone cannot hold the first soul, which thinks purely, let him take the soul that forms opinions, and climb up from this. And if not even that, perception, which brings in the forms in broader outline, and perception by itself too, with what it can do, being already among the forms. And if he wishes, let him go down to the generative soul as far as what it makes; then let him climb back from there, from the last forms to the forms that are last in the other direction, or rather to the first.
!flag ought to consider what that is which intellect may know | The Greek says that one who is to know what intellect is must look at the soul and its most divine part; Taylor turns the sentence around.

@ch V.3.10 | What thinks must be many
Intellect has the forms as makers, not things made, so maker and made are one in it. Does it need to see itself? It has the seeing of itself because it is many, and of another; seeing needs more than one, for the wholly one has nothing to be active on and would stand still. Thinking needs two, in otherness and sameness, and each object of thought is varied. Faced with the wholly partless, intellect would be speechless: even "I am this" says many things. Knowing is a kind of longing and finding; what has no difference in itself stays at rest and seeks nothing about itself.

@p
So much for this. But if there were only things made, they would not be last; but there the first are the makers, which is why they are first. The maker, then, must be there too, and the two be one; otherwise again another will be needed. What then? Will there not again be need of another beyond this? Or is intellect this? Why, then, does it not see itself? Or this needs no seeing. But this for later; now let us speak again: for our inquiry is not about a chance thing. We must say again that this intellect needed to see itself, or rather has the seeing of itself, first in being many, then too in being of another, and necessarily being a seer, and a seer of that, and its being being seeing: for if there is something else, there must be seeing, and if there is not, it is in vain. There must, then, be more than one, for there to be seeing, and the seeing must coincide with the visible, and what it sees must be a multitude, not one in every way: for what is one in every way has nothing to be active on, but, being alone and solitary in every way, will stand still; for in so far as it acts, there is this and that. And if there were not this and that, what would it do? Or where would it go forward? So what is active must either be active about another, or itself be something many, if it is to be active in itself. And if it does not go forward to another, it will stand still; and when it stands wholly still, it will not think.

@p
@greek δεῖ τοίνυν τὸ νοοῦν
What thinks, then, must, when it thinks, be in two, and either one outside or both in the same, and thinking must always be in otherness, and necessarily in sameness too; and what is strictly thought must, relative to intellect, be both the same and other. And again, each of the things thought brings this sameness and otherness with it: or what will it think that does not have this and that? For if each is a rational form, it is many. It learns itself, then, by being a varied eye, or of varied colours. For if it applied itself to the one and partless, it would be left speechless: for what would it have to say about it, or to understand? For even if the wholly partless had to speak itself, it must first say what it is not: so that in this way too it would be many, in order to be one. Then, when it says "I am this", if it says "this" as something other than itself, it will lie; if as an accident of itself, it will say many things, or it will say "I am am" and "I I". What, then, if there were only two and it said "I and this"? It is necessary that there be many already: for they are other, and other in some respect, and already number and many other things. What thinks, then, must take this and that, and what is thought, being thought through, must be varied; or there will be no thinking of it, but a touching and a kind of contact only, unspeakable and unthinking, thinking beforehand, intellect not yet having come to be and what touches not thinking. And what thinks must not itself stay simple either, most of all as far as it thinks itself: for it will divide itself, even if it gives silence as its understanding. Then, the wholly simple will not need to busy itself about itself, as it were: for what will it learn by thinking? For before thinking it is already what it is to itself. And again, knowledge is a kind of longing, and like a finding by one who has sought. What is in every way without difference, then, stays by itself, and seeks nothing about itself; but what unfolds itself would also be many.
!flag And in the first place, it sees that it is manifold | The Greek says intellect has the seeing of itself because it is many and of another; Taylor makes its being many what it sees.

@ch V.3.11 | Intellect comes from the One
So intellect is many when it wishes to think what is beyond. It goes out towards it as a sight not yet seeing and comes back having made it many, and so becomes intellect, being and thinking, having the intelligible. What is before these is their source, not in them: the source of each thing is none of them but other than all. So it is not one of all things but before all, and before intellect; we must not call it intellect, nor the good if that means one of all things. If thinking makes intellect many, the wholly simple first must be beyond intellect.

@p
That is why this intellect too is many, when it wishes to think what is beyond. It does think that very thing; but, wishing to apply itself to it as simple, it comes out always taking something else, made many in itself: so that it rushed towards it not as intellect but as a sight not yet seeing, and came out having what it had itself made many: so that it desired one thing, having some vague image in itself, and came out having taken another in itself, having made it many. For again it has an imprint of what it saw, or it would not have let it come to be in itself; this became many out of one, and so, knowing it, it saw it, and then became a sight that has seen. This is already intellect, when it has, and has as intellect; before this it was only longing and a sight without imprint. This intellect, then, applied itself to that one, and taking it became intellect, always taking it in and becoming intellect and being and thinking, when it thought: for before this it was not thinking, not having the intelligible, nor intellect, not yet having thought. What is before these is their source, not as present in them: for that from which things come is not present in them, but that out of which they are made; and that from which each thing comes is not each thing, but other than them all. It is not, then, one of all things, but before all things, so before intellect too: for again all things are within intellect; so in this way too it is before intellect; and if what comes after it has the rank of all things, in this way too it is before all things. It must not, then, be one of the things it is before, and you will not call it intellect: nor, then, the good, if the good means one of all things, "not even this"; but if it means what is before all things, let it be so named. If, then, intellect is many, and its thinking, as it were coming in as well, makes it many even if it is from itself, what is wholly simple and first of all must be beyond intellect. For if it thinks, it will not be beyond intellect, but intellect; and if it is intellect, it will itself be a multitude too.
!flag and thus as intellect it sees itself | The Greek says that, knowing it, intellect "saw it", the One it was reaching for, not itself.

@ch V.3.12 | The source stays still
Could the first be many in its activities while one in being? If its being is its activity and its activity many, its being is many: true of intellect, not of the source. The many must come from one. If the activities are lasting realities they are other than their source, which stays simple; it did not act or will to make intellect, for then it would have been incomplete. What comes after it comes while it stays as it is, like light from the sun, the whole intelligible world being that light. What comes from it knows itself; it is beyond knowing, needing nothing.

@p
And what prevents its being many in this way, so long as it is one being? For the multitude is not by composition, but its activities are the multitude. But if its activities are not beings, and it comes from potency to act, it is not many, but it is incomplete in its being before it acts. But if its being is activity, and its activity the multitude, its being will be as great as the multitude. This we grant to intellect, to which we gave thinking itself as well, but no longer to the source of all. The one must be before the many, from which the many come too: for in every number one is first. But for number, they say, this is so, since what follows is composition: but for beings, what necessity is there here too for there to be one from which the many come? Because the many will be torn apart from each other, each coming from a different place to the composition by chance. But they will say that the activities came forth from one, intellect, being simple. Then they already posit something simple before the activities; then they will posit the activities as always remaining and as realities: but being realities they will be other than that from which they are, that remaining simple, and what is from it being by itself a multitude and hanging from it. For if these came to be when that acted in some way, there too there will be multitude; but if these are the first activities, which made the second, and made that which is before these activities stay by itself, they gave up the activities to the second, which is constituted of the activities: for it is one thing, and the activities from it another, because they are not from its acting. Otherwise intellect will not be the first activity: for it did not, as it were, desire intellect to come to be, and then intellect came to be, the desire coming between it and the intellect born; nor did it desire at all, for so it would have been incomplete, and the desire would not have had anything to desire; nor again did it have part of the thing and not part: for there was nothing towards which the reaching went. It is plain that if anything came to be after it, it came to be while it stayed in its own way. For something else to come to be, then, that one must be at rest by itself in every way; otherwise it will be moved before being moved, and think before thinking, or its first activity will be incomplete, being only an impulse.

@p
@greek ἐπὶ τί οὖν ὧδε
Towards what, then, would it so rush, finding nothing? Rather, in reason we shall posit the activity as flowing from it, as light from the sun. We shall posit the whole intelligible nature as a kind of light, and it as standing at the summit of the intelligible and reigning over it, not thrusting what appeared away from itself. Or we shall make another light before the light, always staying and shining upon the intelligible. For what is from it is not cut off from it, nor again the same as it, nor such as not to be being, nor again as blind; but seeing and knowing itself, and first knowing. But as that one is beyond intellect, so it is beyond knowing, needing nothing, just as it needs nothing, so not knowing either: knowing is in the second nature. For knowing is also one thing; but that one is without the "a": for if it were a one, it would not be the one itself: for "itself" is before the "a".
!flag Our opponents, however, may say | Taylor turns "they say" into an objection from opponents; Plotinus is reporting what people say about number.

@ch V.3.13 | It does not think itself
So the One is truly unspeakable: whatever you say, you say "a something". It has no name, and we only try to point to it for ourselves. When we puzzle that it would then be unaware of itself, we turn ourselves to the opposite: to make it know and think is to make it many and needy. Thinking is the awareness of a whole made of many things together; what is wholly simple and self-sufficient needs nothing, while what is self-sufficient in the second way needs itself and so thinks itself. Even "I am being" says many things in one word. So the simplest does not think itself.

@p
That is why it is truly unspeakable: whatever you say, you will say "a something". But what is beyond all things, and beyond the most august intellect, being alone true among all things, is neither one of all things nor has a name, because nothing is said of it; but as far as we can, we try to point to ourselves about it. But when we are at a loss, "then it is unaware of itself, and does not even follow itself, nor know itself", we must bear in mind that in saying this we turn ourselves round to the opposite. For we make it many by making it knowable, and making knowledge and giving it thinking we make it need thinking: and even if thinking is together with it, the thinking will be superfluous to it. For thinking seems on the whole to be a joint awareness of a whole, many things having come together into it, when something thinks itself: which is thinking strictly; but each one thing is itself something and seeks nothing. And if the thinking is of what is outside, it will be lacking, and not thinking strictly. But what is wholly simple and really self-sufficient needs nothing; while what is self-sufficient in the second way, but needs itself, this needs to think itself: and, being lacking towards itself, it has made itself self-sufficient by the whole, becoming enough out of all things, being with itself and thinking towards itself. For joint awareness is the awareness of something many: the name bears witness to this too. And thinking, being prior, turns within to itself, plainly as being many: for even if it says this alone, "I am being", it says it as having found something, and reasonably, for being is many: since, when it applies itself to the simple and says "I am being", it has hit neither itself nor being. For when it speaks truly it does not speak of being as of a stone, but it has said many things in one word. For this being, which is said to be really being and not to have a trace of being, and which for that reason would not even be called being, being like an image to its archetype, has many things. What then? Will each of them not be thought? If you wish to take it bare and alone, you will not think it: but being itself is many in itself, and even if you say something else, it has being. And if so, if there is something simplest of all, it will not have thinking of itself: for if it has, it will have being many. So it neither thinks itself, nor is there thinking of it.
!flag it would also have a subsistence somewhere | The Greek says that if the simplest had thinking of itself it would have being many; Taylor's bracket follows a reading of his own.

@ch V.3.14 | Speaking about what we cannot say
How do we speak of it? We speak about it, but do not say it; we have neither knowledge nor thought of it. Yet we are not wholly without it: we say what it is not, from what comes after it. Like the inspired, who know there is something greater in them without knowing what, and gain some sense of what moved them from what they say, we, when we have pure intellect, use it and know it is the intellect within that gave being, while the source itself is greater than being, reason, intellect and perception, giving them without being them.

@p
How, then, do we speak about it? We do say something about it, but we do not say it, nor have we knowledge or thought of it. How, then, do we speak about it, if we do not have it? If we do not have it by knowledge, we do not altogether not have it, but we have it so as to speak about it and not to say it. For we say what it is not; what it is, we do not say: so that we speak about it from what comes after it; and we are not prevented from having it, even if we do not say it. But as people who are inspired and possessed may know this much, that they have something greater in themselves, even if they do not know what, and from what has moved them and what they say they gain some perception of what moved them, it being other than they: so we too are likely to stand towards that one, when we have pure intellect, using it, as being the intellect within, which gave being and the other things of this order, while it itself is such as not to be these, but something better than what we call being, more and greater than what we say, because it is itself better than reason and intellect and perception, having given these, not being them.
!flag perceive indeed, that they have something greater in | Taylor's footnote on V.3.13 (with its Greek and its comment on "in itself") is printed into the middle of this sentence in the scan, which goes on, after it, "themselves, though they do not know what it is".

@ch V.3.15 | How the One gives what it does not have
How does it give? If by having, it is not simple; if by not having, how do the many come from it? What comes from it is not the same as it, nor better, so worse and lacking: not one, so many, yet longing for one, so one-many. Everything not one is kept by the one and is what it is by it. What comes next is many yet one everywhere, all together. The source is the power of all things, not as matter is, by receiving, but as maker. What comes from the One is other, so not one, but two, and so many: same, other, quality.

@p
But how did it give them? By having them or not having them? But how did it give what it does not have? If by having, it is not simple; if by not having, how does the multitude come from it? One simple thing coming from it perhaps someone might grant (though even this might be asked, how it can come from what is wholly one; yet one can say, as the light shining round from light); but how many? What comes from that one was not going to be the same as it: and if not the same, then not better; for what could be better than the one, or beyond it at all? Worse, then: that is, more lacking. What, then, is more lacking than the one? What is not one: many, then; yet longing for the one: one-many, then. For all that is not one is kept by the one and is what it is by this: for if it has not become one, even if it is made of many, one would not yet say "it is"; and if one can say what each thing is, one says it by each of them being one, and by this same thing again. But what does not have many in itself is not one by sharing in one, but the one itself, not as said of another, but because it is this, from which the others come in some way, some by being near, some by being far. Since what is the same, and that it comes after it, it makes plain by its multitude being one everywhere: for being a multitude it is still in the same, and you could not separate it, because all are together: since each of the things from it too, as long as it shares in life, is one-many: for it cannot show itself as one-all. But that one itself is one-all, because it is a great source: for the source is really one and truly one; and what comes after the source, the one weighing on it, as it were, is all, sharing in the one, and any part of it is again all and one.

@p
@greek τίνα οὖν πάντα
What all, then? Those of which that one is source. How is that one source of all? Is it because it keeps them, making each of them be one? Or also because it brought them into existence. How? By having them before. But it has been said that so it will be a multitude. Did it have them, then, as not distinguished, while in the second they were distinguished by rational form? For that is already activity; but this is the power of all things. But what is the manner of the power? For it is not as matter is said to be in potency, because it receives: for that is passive; but in the way opposite to this, as making. How, then, does it make what it does not have? For not at random, nor having considered what it will make, will it make all the same. It has been said, then, that if anything comes from the one, it must be other than it; and being other, it is not one: for that was what that one was. And if not one, but two, it must already be many too: for it is already other and same and quality and the rest. That what is from that one is not one, then, would have been shown; but that it is many, and a multitude of the kind we see in what comes after it, is worth puzzling over; and the necessity of what comes after it must still be inquired into.
!flag They are contained in it, therefore, in such a way | Taylor turns Plotinus' question ("Did it have them, then, as not distinguished?") into a statement.

@ch V.3.16 | The Good is above life and intellect
There must be something after the first, its immense power shown by the fact that everything generates. Generation goes down and towards more multitude, and each source is simpler than what it makes: the maker of the perceptible world is intellect and the intelligible world, and what made that is simpler still. But how does reason come from what is not reason, the good-like from the good? Intellect lives always the same, content, with everything present; it has life and intellect complete. Its good must be beyond these, and its life and intellect, imitations of what is in that, turn to it.

@p
That there must be something after the first has been said elsewhere, and in general that it is power, an immense power, and that this must be believed from all other things, because there is nothing, even among the last, that does not have a power to generate. But now we must say this: since among things generated it is not possible to go upward but downward, and to go more into multitude, the source of each thing too is simpler than they are. What made the perceptible world, then, would not itself be a perceptible world, but intellect and the intelligible world; and so what is before this and generated it is neither intellect nor intelligible world, but simpler than intellect and simpler than the intelligible world. For many does not come from many, but this many comes from what is not many: for if that too were many, it would not be the source, but another before it. All must be gathered, then, into what is really one, outside all multitude and any simplicity whatever, if it is really simple. But how is what came from it a rational form, many and whole, while it was plainly not a rational form? And if it was not this, how does rational form come from what is not rational form? And how does the good-like come from the good? For what does it have from it that it is called good-like? Is it having sameness and constancy? And what has this to do with the good? For we look for constancy among things that are good. Rather, before that is the thing from which we must not depart, because it is good; otherwise it is better to stand away. Is it, then, living constantly, staying willingly with this? If, then, living is welcome to it, it plainly seeks nothing: the constancy seems to be because what is present is enough. But living is welcome to it, all things being present to it already, and present in such a way, not as being other than it. And if all life is in it, and life clear and complete, all soul and all intellect is in it, and nothing either of life or intellect stands apart from it. It is enough for itself, then, and seeks nothing: and if it seeks nothing, it has in itself what it would have sought if it were not present. It has in itself, then, the good, either being such itself, what we called life and intellect, or something else that goes with these. But if this is the good, there would be nothing beyond these: and if the good is that, plainly its life is towards that, dependent on that and having its existence from that and living towards that: for that is its source. That one, then, must be better than life and intellect: for so it will turn to that both the life in itself, an imitation of what is in that, by which this lives, and the intellect in this, an imitation of what is in that, whatever that is.
!flag will evidently be good | The Greek does not say that this life is good; it says that its life is towards the good, depending on it, since the good is its source.

@ch V.3.17 | Take away everything
What is better than the wisest, unerring life and the intellect that has all? Its maker. Each part of intellect lacks, and shares in one without being one; what makes it be and makes its multitude self-sufficient is beyond being and self-sufficiency. The soul is still in labour, so we look for a charm to ease it: but the One escapes all speech and discursive thought, which takes this and that. It is enough to touch it in intellect, and to reason about it afterwards. When the soul suddenly takes light, we have seen, and the light is it. How? Take away everything.

@p
What, then, is better than the wisest life, without stumbling and without error, and an intellect having all things, and all life and all intellect? If we say "what made these", then how did it make them? And if nothing better appears, the reasoning will not go off to another, but stop here. But we must go up, both for many other reasons and because its self-sufficiency is put together from all things outside it, while each of them plainly lacks; and because each has had a share of the same one and shares in one, not being the one itself. What, then, is that in which it shares, which makes it be, and be all things together? But if it makes each thing be, and by the presence of one the multitude of it and it itself are self-sufficient, plainly that one is the maker of being and of self-sufficiency, not being being itself, but beyond it and beyond self-sufficiency. Is it enough, then, to say this and be done? No: the soul is still in labour, and more. Perhaps, then, it must now give birth, having darted towards it, filled with labour pains. Yet we must sing a charm again, if we can find somewhere a charm against the labour. Perhaps it might come about even from what has been said already, if one sang it often. What, then, is another charm, a new one, as it were? For though the soul runs over all the true things, and the truths we share in, still it escapes, if one wishes to say and think it, since discursive thought, in order to say something, must take this and that: for so it is a going through: and in the wholly simple what going through is there? But it is enough to touch it in intellect; and having touched it, while it touches, to be wholly unable to say anything and to have no leisure to speak, and afterwards to reason about it.

@p
@greek τότε δὲ χρὴ ἑωρακέναι
We must believe we have seen it then, when the soul suddenly takes light: for this is from it, and is it; and we must think it is present then, when, like another god coming into the house of someone who calls him, it brings light: or, if it had not come, it would not have brought light. So the soul without light is without the sight of it; but lit up, it has what it sought; and this is the soul's true end, to touch that light and see it by itself, not by another light, but by itself, by which it also sees. For that by which it was lit up is what it must see: for we do not see the sun either by another light. How, then, could this come about? Take away everything.
!flag immediately translation of Ficinus is | Taylor's footnote quoting Ficinus is printed into the middle of his sentence in the scan, which goes on, after it, "flies away from him who wishes to speak".

@treatise V.6 | That what is beyond being does not think
A short, tight argument. What thinks itself is both one and two: one, so that it has what it thinks as its own; two, so that it has something to think. Intellect is this. But what is first must be wholly one, needing nothing; thinking is a need and a movement towards the good, and it brings doubleness with it, a thinker and a thought, being and life and thinking together. So the first, the good, does not think, not even itself: it is beyond being and so beyond thinking, and gives other things something better than knowing them, the power to touch it.

@ch V.6.1 | Thinking is one and two
Thinking another is double; thinking oneself flees doubleness more, because thinker and thought are not separated in being. What thinks first must be both one and two: one, or it does not have what it thinks as its own; two, or it has nothing to think. We see this best from soul: make soul a dimmer light and its intelligible a purer one, then make the seer equal to the seen, and you will count them as one. We made one out of two; intellect makes itself two out of one, by thinking.

@p
One thing thinks another, another thinks itself; and this already flees being two more. The first one named wants this too, but is less able: for it has what it sees with itself, but it is other than it. But the second is not separated in being, but, being with itself, sees itself. Both, then, become one thing. It thinks more, then, because it has, and it thinks first, because what thinks must be one and two. For if it is not one, what thinks will be one thing and what is thought another, and it would not then be thinking first, because, taking the thinking of another, it will not be what thinks first, since what it thinks it does not have as its own, and so not itself either; or if it has it as itself, so as to think strictly, the two will be one: both, then, must be one. But if it is one, and not two again, it will not have anything to think, so that it will not be thinking either. It must be simple and not simple, then. One might better grasp it as being such by climbing up from the soul: for here it is easy to divide, and one might more easily see the doubleness. If, then, someone made a double light, the soul as the lesser light and its intelligible as the purer, and then made the seer a light equal to the seen, no longer able to separate them by their difference he will count the two as one, thinking that they were two, but now seeing one: so he will grasp what thinks and the intelligible. We, then, in our reasoning, have made one out of two; but intellect, the other way round, is two out of one, because it thinks, making itself two, or rather being two, because it thinks, and one, because it thinks itself.
!flag Hence we, by a reasoning process, have made two things from one | The Greek says the opposite: in our reasoning we made one out of two, while intellect is two out of one.

@ch V.6.2 | What is beyond the first thinker does not think
If there is a first thinker and a second, what is beyond the first thinker does not think: to think it would have to become intellect and have an intelligible. The intelligible need not think too, or it would be two. Intellect could not exist without a purely intelligible being, intelligible to intellect, but neither thinker nor thought in itself; intellect is empty without the intelligible, and is complete only when it has it. What is complete before thinking needs no thinking, so it does not think. If the first thought, it would be second and many.

@p
If, then, there is the first thinker, and another thinking in another way, what is beyond the first thinker would no longer think: for it must become intellect in order to think, and being intellect it must have an intelligible too, and, thinking first, have the intelligible in itself. But not every intelligible must have a thinker in itself and think: for then it will be not only intelligible but also thinking, and it will not be first, being two. And intellect, which has the intelligible, could not exist if there were not a being that is purely intelligible, which will be intelligible relative to intellect, but in itself will be strictly neither thinker nor intelligible: for the intelligible is so for another, and intellect has its application in thinking empty, unless it takes and grasps the intelligible which it thinks: for it does not have thinking without the intelligible. It is complete, then, when it has it; but it must be complete in its being from itself before thinking. That to which completeness belongs will have it, then, before thinking: it needs thinking not at all, then, for before this it is enough for itself. It will not think, then. So one does not think, one thinks first, and one will think second. Further, if the first thinks, something will belong to it: it is not, then, first, but second too, and not one but many already, and all the things it will think: for even if it thinks only itself, it will be many.
!flag would have a vacuum | Taylor's "vacuum" makes a place of what the Greek calls intellect's "empty" reaching in thought, when it has not grasped the intelligible.

@ch V.6.3 | One before the many
If they say nothing prevents the same thing from being many, there must be one under the many, taken alone by itself. What is the same in the others would be like it but would not be it; it must be alone to be seen in others too. If nothing simple existed by itself, nothing composite could exist either, being made of what does not exist. So there must be one before the many; and if what thinks is a multitude, thinking cannot be in what is not a multitude, the first. Thinking and intellect will be in what comes after it.

@p
But if they say that nothing prevents the same thing from being many, there will be one underlying these: for there cannot be many without there being one from which or in which they are, or in general one, and this counted first of the others, which must be taken itself by itself alone. And if it is together with the others, one must take it together with the others, but still, since it is other than them, let it be as with others, and look for this which underlies the others no longer with the others, but itself by itself. For the same in the others would be like this, but would not be this: it must be alone, if it is to be seen in others too. Unless someone says that its being has its existence with the others: then it will not be simple itself, nor will the composite of many exist either: for what cannot be simple will have no existence, and what is composed of many will not exist either, if the simple does not. For if each simple thing cannot exist, if no one simple thing exists by itself, none of them being able to have existence by itself or to offer itself to be with another, because it does not exist at all, how could the composite of all be, made out of things that are not, not that are not something, but that are not at all? If, then, something is many, there must be one before the many. If, then, what thinks is a multitude, thinking must not be in what is not a multitude. But this was the first. Thinking and intellect, then, will be in what comes after it.
!flag imparticipable or exempt one, is not consubsistent with any thing | Taylor's footnote (with its reference to his Proclus) is printed into the middle of his sentence in the scan, which goes on, after it, "things. For since that which is simple".

@ch V.6.4 | Light, sun and moon
The good, simple and needing nothing, would not need thinking, and what it does not need is not present to it. Intellect is other than the good, and good-like by thinking the good. As one with another presupposes one by itself, so the simple must be by itself, with nothing in it of what goes with others. Compare the first to light, the next to the sun, the third to the moon, which takes its light from the sun: soul has intellect brought in, colouring it; intellect has its light as its own; and the first is the simple light that gives it the power to be what it is.

@p
Further, if the good must be simple and without need, it would not need thinking either: and what it does not need will not be present to it; since in general nothing is present to it. Thinking, then, is not present to it. And it thinks nothing, because there is nothing else either. Further, intellect is other than the good: for it is good-like by thinking the good. Further, as in two things, where there is one and another, it is not possible for this one with another to be the one, but there had to be one by itself before the one with another, so too the one that is not with another must be simple, this itself by itself, having nothing in itself of all that is in what is with others. For from where would another be in another, if that from which the other comes were not first apart? For the simple could not be from another, but what is many, or two, must itself hang from another. And so we must liken the first to light, the next to the sun, and the third to the moon's star, which gets its light from the sun. For soul has intellect brought in from outside, colouring it, since it is intellectual; but intellect has it in itself as its own, not being light only, but what is lit up in its own being; and what gives it the light, being nothing other than light, is simple light, giving that one the power to be what it is. What, then, would it need? For it is not the same as what is in another: for what is in another is other than what is itself by itself.
!flag [with a light derived from the good] | Taylor's bracket is his own; the Greek says only that intellect, brought into the soul, colours it.

@ch V.6.5 | Thinking is a movement towards the good
The many seeks itself and wants to come together and be aware of itself; what is wholly one has nowhere to go to itself and no need of awareness. Thinking is not first, either in being or in worth, but second, born when the good existed and moved what comes after it to itself: it is a movement towards the good by what longs for it, as seeing is the longing of sight. The good has no need to think, for its good is not something other than itself. When the other thinks itself, it does so by being good-like and looking to the good.

@p
Further, the many would seek itself, and would wish to converge and be aware of itself; but what is wholly one, where will it go to itself? Where would it need awareness? It is the same thing, and better than awareness and all thinking. For thinking is not first, either in being or in worth, but second, and come to be, when the good existed and moved what came after to itself, and that was moved and saw. And this is thinking, a movement towards the good by what longs for it: for the longing generated thinking and brought it into existence with itself: for the longing of sight is seeing. The good itself, then, has no need to think: for its good is not other than itself. For when what is other than the good thinks itself, it thinks by being good-like and having a likeness to the good, and it thinks itself as good and desirable to itself, and as taking, as it were, an image of the good. And if it is always so, it is always this. For again, in its thinking of itself it thinks itself incidentally: for it is looking to the good that it thinks itself: for it thinks itself in being active; and the activity of all things is towards the good.
!flag For looking to the good, it intellectually sees it | The Greek says that, looking to the good, intellect thinks itself; Taylor makes the good what it sees.

@ch V.6.6 | The good does not know itself
So the good has no room for thinking: the good must be other than what thinks it. It is without activity, for activity does not have a further activity; the first activity is just what it is. Thinking is not first, since it has nothing to think, and it is the thinker, not thinking, that thinks: two again. Real beings are in the intelligible place because they have the fullness of being, which includes thinking and life: being is intellect, so many. Man and the thought of man, horse and the thought of horse: all is double. What is beyond being is beyond thinking, and gives others something better than knowing them.

@p
If, then, this is said rightly, the good could have no room for any thinking at all: for the good must be other for what thinks it. It is without activity, then. And why should activity be active? For in general no activity has again an activity; but if we do ascribe one to the other activities that are directed to another, the first of all, from which the others hang, must be itself this which it is, adding nothing further to it. Such an activity, then, is not thinking: for it does not have anything it will think: for it is itself first. Then too, it is not thinking that thinks, but what has the thinking. Two, then, come to be again in what thinks; but this is in no way two. Further, one might see this still better if one grasped more clearly how this double nature belongs to all that thinks. We say that beings as beings, and each thing itself, and the true beings, are in the intelligible place, not only because some stay the same in their being, while others flow and do not stay, all that are in perception (for perhaps among perceptible things too there are things that stay), but rather because they have the completeness of being from themselves. For what is called being first must not be a shadow of being, but have being in full. And being is full when it takes the form of thinking and living. Thinking, living and being, then, are together in being. If, then, it is being, it is intellect too, and if intellect, being too, and thinking is together with being. Thinking, then, is many and not one. What is not such, then, must not have thinking either. And going through them one by one, there is the thought of man and man, and the thought of horse and horse, and the thought of the just and the just. All things are double, then, and the one is two, and again the two come into one: but what is not these is not each one, nor made of all the twos, nor two at all. How the two come from the one is said elsewhere. But for what is beyond being, it is also beyond thinking: it is not absurd, then, if it does not know itself: for it does not have in itself something to learn, being one; but it need not know the other things either. For it gives them something better and greater than its knowing them, what was the good of the others; but rather in the same, as far as they can touch it.
!flag how this twofold nature subsists in every thing which is more clearly intellective | Taylor moves "more clearly" to "intellective"; the Greek says one would see this better by grasping more clearly how the double nature belongs to all that thinks.
!flag Instead of ov yevei here, it is necessary to read | Taylor's footnote on the Greek text is printed into his sentence about man and the thought of man in the scan.

@treatise V.9 | On intellect, the forms, and being
Most people live by perception and take pleasure for the good; some rise a little and fall back into practical virtue; a divine few see the light above and stay there as in their own country. The way up is the lover's and the philosopher's: from beautiful bodies to beautiful souls, from soul to the intellect that gives it wisdom. Plotinus then argues that intellect must exist before soul, in act and always, and that it is itself the beings it thinks, the forms, all together and yet distinct, the archetypes of the world. He asks what there are forms of: of everything natural, of the arts that use reason, of man and of soul, but not of evil.

@ch V.9.1 | Three kinds of men
Everyone begins with perception before intellect. Some stay there all their lives, taking pain for evil and pleasure for good, and call this wisdom, like heavy birds that cannot fly though they have wings. Some rise a little, moved by the better part of the soul, but, unable to see what is above, fall back with the name of virtue to action and choice among the things below. A third, divine kind sees with sharp eyes the radiance above, rises over the clouds and the mist here, and stays there, as a man who comes home after long wandering to a well-governed country.

@p
All men, using perception before intellect from the start and meeting perceptible things first, by necessity, some stayed here and lived their lives taking these to be first and last, and, supposing what is painful in them to be evil and what is pleasant good, thought it enough if they spent their lives pursuing the one and arranging to keep away from the other. And those of them who lay claim to reason have made this their wisdom, like the heavy birds that, having taken much from the earth and being weighed down, cannot fly high, although they have been given wings by nature. Others were raised a little from the things below, the better part of the soul moving them from the pleasant to the finer; but, unable to see what is above, as having nothing else to stand on, they were carried down, with the name of virtue, to actions and choices among the things below, from which they had tried at first to rise. But a third kind, of divine men, by a greater power and sharpness of eyes, saw, as with keen sight, the radiance above, and was raised there, as if above the clouds and the mist here, and stayed there, looking down on everything here, delighting in the place, which is true and its own, like a man who arrives, after long wandering, at a well-governed homeland.
!flag a more ample discussion of Ideas than is contained in this treatise | Taylor's footnote (pointing to his Parmenides and his Aristotle) is printed into the middle of his sentence in the scan, which goes on, after it, "them, who pretend to a greater share of reason".

@ch V.9.2 | The lover's way up
Who reaches that place? The one who is by nature a lover and a philosopher from the start, in labour over beauty. He does not rest in the beauty of bodies but goes up to the beauties of soul, virtues, knowledge, ways of life and laws, then to their cause, and on to the first, beautiful in itself. Beauty in bodies is brought in, as forms on matter; soul makes bodies beautiful, but soul is beautiful by wisdom, which intellect gives, and intellect is beautiful in itself. Intellect stands before the first, as in its porch, announcing all things in itself.

@p
What, then, is this place? And how could one arrive at it? He would arrive who is by nature a lover, and truly a philosopher in his disposition from the beginning; in labour, as a lover, about beauty, but not putting up with the beauty in body, he flees from there to the beauties of the soul, virtues and kinds of knowledge and fine ways of life and laws, and goes up again to the cause of the beauties in the soul, and again to whatever may be before this, until he comes at the last to the first, which is beautiful of itself. Arrived there, he will cease from his labour, but not before. But how will he go up? Where is the power for it to come from, and what reasoning will guide this love? This: this beauty on bodies is brought in to the bodies; for these are shapes of bodies, on them as on matter. At any rate, the underlying thing changes and from beautiful becomes ugly. It is by sharing, then, says the argument. What, then, made the body beautiful? In one way the presence of beauty, in another the soul, which moulded it and put such a shape into it. What then? Is soul beautiful of itself? No: for one soul would not be wise and beautiful and another foolish and ugly. It is by wisdom, then, that beauty is in the soul. And who, then, gave wisdom to the soul? Intellect, necessarily, and intellect that is not sometimes intellect and sometimes without intellect, the true intellect: beautiful of itself, then. And must we stop here, as at the first, or must we go beyond intellect too? Intellect stands in front of the first source, as far as we are concerned, as if in the porch of the good, announcing all things in itself, as an imprint of that one, rather, in multitude, while that one stays wholly in one.
!flag yet will not be satisfied with the beauty which is in body | Taylor is close; "will not put up with" is stronger in the Greek, which also has the lover "flee" from the beauty of bodies.

@ch V.9.3 | Matter, form and the maker
We must make sure intellect exists, and is what we say: separate, the beings, the place of the forms. Everything here is composite: artworks of bronze or wood and the form the art puts in; natural things of matter and form, man of soul and body, body of the four elements, each of matter and what shapes it. Then ask whether soul too has something like matter and something like form, the intellect in it. Carried over to the universe, this leads up to intellect as the true maker: soul gives the elements the world's shape, intellect gives soul its forming principles.

@p
We must look into this nature of intellect, which the argument declares to be what really is and true being, having first made sure, going another way, that there must be such a thing. Perhaps it is ridiculous to inquire whether intellect is among the things that are; but perhaps some might dispute even this. Rather, whether it is such as we say, and whether it is separate, and whether it is the beings, and whether the nature of the forms is there, which is what we now propose to speak about. We see, then, that all the things said to be are composite, and not one of them simple, both what each art makes and what is put together by nature. For the things of art have bronze or wood or stone, and are not yet complete from these, until each art makes a statue, or a bed, or a house, by putting in the form that is in it. And again, of the things put together by nature, those that are much compounded and called mixtures you will analyse into matter and the form over all the things mixed: man, for instance, into soul and body, and body into the four elements.
