And finding each of these composite, of matter and what shapes it (for the matter of the elements is shapeless of itself), you will ask where the form comes to the matter from. And you will ask again about the soul, whether it is already one of the simple things, or there is something in it as matter and something as form, the intellect in it, one like the shape on the bronze, the other like the one who made the shape in the bronze. Carrying these same things over to the universe too, one will go up here as well to intellect, setting it down as the true maker and craftsman, and will say that the underlying thing, receiving shapes, became fire, water, air and earth, and that these shapes came from another; and that this is soul; and that soul in turn gave the four the shape of a world; and that intellect became the supplier of its forming principles, as with the souls of craftsmen the principles for their working come from the arts: one intellect as the form of the soul, the one in the shape, and another providing the shape, like the maker of the statue, in whom all is present that he gives. What he gives to soul is near the truth; what the body receives is already images and imitations.
!flag may be analyzed into the form which is inherent in all | Taylor's sentence, as the scan gives it, has lost a line, running straight from "the form which is inherent in all" into "may be analyzed into soul and body"; the Greek has matter and form, "man, for instance", into soul and body.
!flag The things, however, which body receives, are now nothing more than images | Taylor leaves out the first half of the Greek sentence, that what intellect gives to soul is near the truth.

@ch V.9.4 | Intellect before soul
Why go above soul? Because intellect is other than soul and better, and the better is first by nature. Soul does not, as some think, produce intellect when it is perfected: what is potential becomes actual only by a cause already actual. So the first things must be actual, needing nothing and complete; the incomplete come after and are completed by them, as children by fathers. And since soul is affected, there must be something unaffected; since soul is in the world, something outside the world; or nothing would stay the same.

@p
Why, then, must we go up beyond soul, and not set it down as the first? First, intellect is other than soul and better; and the better is first by nature. For soul, when it is perfected, does not generate intellect, as they think: for from where will what is potential come to be actual, if there is not a cause bringing it to act? For if by chance, it may not come to act. So the first things must be set down as actual, and needing nothing, and complete; and the incomplete things after them, completed by the very things that generated them, like fathers completing what they generated incomplete at the start; and these are first matter relative to their maker, and then are themselves brought to completion with a shape. And if soul is subject to affection, and there must be something unaffected (or all things will perish in time), there must be something before soul. And if soul is in the world, and there must be something outside the world, in this way too there must be something before soul. For if what is in the world is what is in body and matter, nothing stays the same: so that man and all the forming principles would not be everlasting, nor the same. That there must be intellect before soul, then, one might see from these and many other arguments.

@ch V.9.5 | Intellect is the beings it thinks
True intellect is not potential, nor one that came from folly to intellect, but always actual. Its wisdom is not brought in: it thinks from itself and is what it thinks, or its being would be without thought. It thinks the beings, not elsewhere and not in perceptible things, but in itself, as the archetypes before the world. So "thinking and being are the same", knowledge without matter is the same as its object, and recollection is true. Perceptible things are what they are by sharing, as bronze takes form from the sculptor's art; intellect holds them up and needs no place itself.

@p
We must take intellect, if we are to be true to the name, not as the potential one, nor the one that came from folly to intellect (otherwise we shall look again for another before it), but the one that is actual and always intellect. And if it does not have its wisdom brought in, if it thinks anything it thinks it from itself, and if it has anything it has it from itself. And if it thinks from itself and out of itself, it is itself what it thinks. For if its being were one thing and what it thinks other than it, its very being would be without thought, and again potential, not actual. Neither, then, must be separated from the other; but it is our habit, from the things with us, to separate those too in our conceptions. What, then, is its activity, and what does it think, so that we may set it down as being what it thinks? It is plain that, being really intellect, it thinks the beings and brings them into existence. It is, then, the beings. For it will think them either as being elsewhere, or in itself as being itself. Elsewhere is impossible: for where? Itself, then, and in itself. For surely not in perceptible things, as they think. For each first thing is not the perceptible thing: for the form in them, on matter, is an image of what is, and every form in another comes to it from another and is an image of it. And if there must be a maker of this universe, he will not think the things in what does not yet exist in order to make it. Those things must, then, be before the world, not imprints from others, but archetypes, and first, and the being of intellect. And if they say that forming principles are enough, plainly everlasting ones; and if everlasting and unaffected, they must be in intellect, and in such an intellect, prior to state and nature and soul: for these are potential.

@p
@greek ὁ νοῦς ἄρα τὰ ὄντα ὄντως
Intellect, then, is really the beings, not thinking them as they are somewhere else: for they are neither before it nor after it; but it is like a first lawgiver, or rather itself the law of being. Rightly, then, "for thinking and being are the same", and the knowledge of things without matter is the same as the thing, and "I searched myself", as one of the beings; and the recollections too: for none of the beings is outside, or in place, but they stay always in themselves, admitting neither change nor destruction; which is why they really are. Otherwise, coming to be and passing away, they will use being as something brought in, and they will no longer be, but that being will be. Perceptible things are what they are called by sharing, the underlying nature having its shape from elsewhere: as bronze from the sculptor's art and wood from carpentry, through an image of the art going into them, while the art itself stays outside matter in sameness and has the true statue and bed. So with bodies too. And this universe, sharing in images, shows the beings to be other than them, those unchanging, these changing, those settled on themselves, not needing place: for they are not magnitudes: but having an intellectual existence, enough for itself. For the nature of bodies wants to be kept by another, but intellect, holding up by its wonderful nature what falls of itself, does not look for where it is itself to be settled.
!flag For the objects of its perception are not external to itself | The Greek says that the beings are "neither before it nor after it".

@ch V.9.6 | All together and yet distinct
Intellect is the beings, having them not as in a place but as itself, one with them. They are all together there, and yet distinct, as a soul holds many kinds of knowledge without confusion, each acting when it is needed. The whole intellect contains all as a genus its species and a whole its parts. The powers in seeds give an image of this: in the whole seed all is undivided, the principles as in one centre, one for the eye, another for the hands, known as other from what they produce. Some call this power in seeds nature, which shapes matter by giving forming principles.

@p
Let intellect, then, be the beings, having them all in itself, not as in a place, but as having itself, and being one with them. All are together there, and none the less distinct. For the soul too, having many kinds of knowledge together in itself, has none of them confused, and each does its own work when it must, without dragging the others along with it, and each thought acts pure from the thoughts lying within. So, then, and much more, intellect is all things together, and again not together, because each is its own power; and the whole intellect contains all as a genus contains species and a whole its parts. And the powers of seeds too bear an image of what is said: for in the whole all are undivided, and the principles are as if in one centre; and there is one principle of an eye, another of hands, known to be other from the perceptible thing that comes to be from it. The powers in the seeds, then, are each one whole principle with the parts contained in it, of which the bodily part has matter, as much as is moist, while the principle itself is the whole form, and a principle that is the same as the generating form of soul, which is an image of another, better soul. Some call this, in the seeds, nature, which, setting out from there, from what is before it, as light from fire, turned and shaped the matter, not by pushing or using the much-talked-of levers, but by giving of its principles.
!flag Every intellect, however, comprehends all things | The Greek says "the whole intellect" (ὁ πᾶς νοῦς) contains all things, not "every intellect".

@ch V.9.7 | Forms are not made by being thought
Our knowledge of perceptible things is really opinion, coming after them, images of them. But knowledge of intelligibles comes into the rational soul from intellect and is the very things it thinks, because intellect is within, always with itself and active, not reaching for what it lacks or going through steps, which belong to soul. Intellect did not become God by thinking God, or movement by thinking movement. So to call the forms thoughts, as if a form came to be when intellect thought it, is wrong: what is thought must be before the thinking.

@p
The kinds of knowledge in the rational soul that are of perceptible things (if we should call them knowledge of these, for the name of opinion suits them), coming after the things, are images of them; but those of intelligibles, which are really knowledge, coming from intellect into the rational soul, think nothing perceptible; and in so far as they are knowledge, they are each the things they think, and have the intelligible and the thinking within, because intellect is within, which is the first things themselves, always with itself and being actual, and not reaching for things as not having them, or acquiring them, or going through what was not at hand: for these are affections of soul: but it stands in itself, being all things together, not thinking in order to bring each into existence. For it did not become God when it thought God, nor movement when it thought movement. So to say the forms are thoughts, if it is meant that when it thought this, then this came to be or is, is not right. For what is thought must be before this thinking. Or how would it come to think it? Not by chance, surely, nor did it reach for it at random.

@ch V.9.8 | Being comes first in thought
If thinking is of something within, that within is the form, the idea. Each idea is intellect, not other than it; the whole intellect is all the forms, each form an intellect, as a body of knowledge is all its theorems, each part of it with its power in the whole. Intellect is in itself, at rest, always full. Since being must be thought of before intellect, the beings are set in the thinker and the activity of thinking is on them, as fire's activity on fire. But being is activity too, so the two are one. Only our dividing puts one before the other.

@p
If, then, thinking is of what is within, that within is the form, and this is the idea. What, then, is this? Intellect and intellectual being; each idea is not other than intellect, but each is intellect. And the whole intellect is all the forms, and each form is each intellect, as the whole of a knowledge is all its theorems, and each is part of the whole, not as separated in place, but having its power in the whole. This intellect, then, is in itself, and having itself at rest it is always fullness. If, then, intellect were conceived as prior to being, we should have to say that intellect, by acting and thinking, completed and generated the beings; but since we must conceive being before intellect, we must set the beings as lying in what thinks, and the activity and the thinking as upon the beings, as the activity of fire is upon fire already, so that they may have intellect, one with them, upon themselves as their own activity. And being is activity too: there is one activity, then, of both, or rather the two are one. Being and intellect, then, are one nature; and so are the beings, and the activity of being, and such an intellect; and the thoughts of this kind are the form and the shape of being, and its activity. But when they are divided by us, the one is conceived before the other. For the intellect that divides is another; the undivided intellect, which does not divide, is being and all things.
!flag If, therefore, intelligence is truly one | Taylor read the Greek as "if thinking is one being"; the text has "if thinking is of what is within", which is what the rest of the sentence needs.

@ch V.9.9 | The intelligible world
What are the things in one intellect that we divide in thinking? This world is a living being containing all living beings, and has its being, and such being, from another, which leads back to intellect. So the whole archetype must be in intellect, and this intellect is the intelligible world, which Plato in the Timaeus calls "what living being is". As a living being comes to be where a forming principle meets matter, so where an all-powerful intellectual nature meets what can receive it, the one must be ordered and the other order it: the ordered has the form divided, the orderer all in one.

@p
What, then, are the things in one intellect which we divide in thinking them? For we must bring them out while they are at rest, as if looking at what is in a knowledge that is one. Since this world is a living being containing all living beings, and has its being and its being such from another, and that from which it is leads back to intellect, the whole archetype must be in intellect too, and this intellect must be an intelligible world, which Plato in the Timaeus calls "what living being is". For as, when there is a forming principle of some living being, and matter too that has received the seed principle, a living being must come to be, in the same way, when there is an intellectual and all-powerful nature, and nothing in the way, nothing between it and what is able to receive it, the one must be ordered and the other order it. And what is ordered has the form divided, here a man and there the sun; the other has all in one.
!flag But in the adorning cause all things are | Taylor's chapter, as the scan gives it, breaks off here; the Greek ends "has all in one".

@ch V.9.10 | What there are forms of
Whatever is form in the perceptible world comes from there; what is not, does not. So nothing against nature is there, as nothing against art is in the arts, nor lameness in the seeds: lameness comes from a forming principle that did not prevail, or from chance damage. Qualities, quantities, numbers, sizes, comings to be, relations, actions and affections according to nature, movements and rests, are there; instead of time, eternity; place there is one thing in another, intellectually. All is together, living, intellectual being. But there is no form of evil, which comes from lack, privation and matter.

@p
Whatever is in the perceptible world as forms, then, comes from there; what is not, does not. So of things against nature there is nothing there, just as nothing against art is in the arts, nor lameness in the seeds. Lameness of the feet is either already in the coming to be, the forming principle not having prevailed, or from chance, a damage of the form. And qualities in harmony, and quantities, numbers and magnitudes, comings to be and relations, actions and affections that are according to nature, movements and rests, universal and particular, are among the things there: and instead of time, eternity; and place there is, intellectually, one thing in another. There, then, since all are together, whatever you take of them is intellectual being, and each shares in life; and same and other, and movement and rest, and what is moved and what stands, and being and quality, and all is being. For each being is in act, not in potency, so that the quality of each being is not separated from it. Are the things in the perceptible world, then, alone there, or others too, more of them? But first we must look at the things of art. For about evil, none: for evil here comes from lack and privation and deficiency, and is an affection of matter that has missed its aim, and of what has been made like matter.
!flag According qualities, therefore, and quantities | "According" is a scan error for Taylor's "accordant"; he also leaves out "comings to be" (γενέσεις) from the list.

@ch V.9.11 | The arts
The imitative arts, painting, sculpture, dance and mime, take their pattern from perceptible things, and are there only through the rational form of man, though a sense of the proportion of whole living beings would be part of the power that sees proportion in the intelligible. Music, with its thought on harmony and rhythm, is like the music of intelligible rhythm. Building and carpentry have principles and wisdom from there, in so far as they use proportion. Farming, medicine and training, rhetoric, generalship, household and kingship share in the knowledge there, in so far as they see the beautiful. Geometry and wisdom belong there.

@p
Of the arts, those that are imitative, painting and sculpture, dancing and mime, which take their make-up somewhere here, use a perceptible pattern, and imitate forms and movements and transfer the proportions they see, could not reasonably be led back there, except through the rational form of man. But if some state arising from the proportion in living beings looks at that of whole living beings, it would be part of the power that there too looks at and contemplates the proportion in the intelligible about all things. And again all musical art, having its thoughts about harmony and rhythm, would be in the same way, like the musical art that is about intelligible rhythm. The arts that make perceptible things by art, like building and carpentry, in so far as they use proportions, would have their principles from there, and from the kinds of wisdom there; but having mixed these with the perceptible, they would not be there as a whole, except in man. Nor farming either, which works with a perceptible plant, or medicine, which looks at health here, or the art concerned with strength and good condition here: for there is another power and health there, by which all living beings are untroubled and enough. Rhetoric and generalship, household management and kingship, if any of them share the beautiful with their actions, if they look at that, have a portion for their knowledge from the knowledge there. Geometry, being of intelligibles, must be placed there, and wisdom too, which is highest, about being. So much about the arts and what is by art.
!flag and also transfer [to their originals] the forms, motions and symmetries | Taylor's bracket is his own; the Greek says only that these arts carry over into their works the proportions they see.

@ch V.9.12 | Is there a form of the individual?
If there is a form of man there, there is one of the rational and the skilled, and the arts, offspring of intellect. Forms are of universals, not of Socrates but of man. But we must ask whether the individual man is there too. Snub nose and hook nose should be placed as differences in the form of man, as there are differences of animal; that this man has this kind of hook nose and that man that kind comes from matter. So with colours: some differences are in the forming principle, others come from matter and differences of place.

@p
And if of man there, then of the rational and the skilled there too, and the arts too, which are offspring of intellect. We must say too that the forms are of universals, not of Socrates but of man. But we must look into whether man in the particular is there too: and the particular, because the same is not other in each, as one is snub-nosed, another hook-nosed. Hook-nose and snub-nose, then, must be set down as differences in the form of man, as there are differences of animal; and that one has this kind of hook-nose and another that kind comes from matter too: and of the differences of colours, some are in the forming principle, while others are made by matter and place being different.
!note A word in the Greek of the second sentence is damaged; "the same" follows the sense most editors give it.
!flag not because he is the same with another man, but because one man has a flat, and another an aquiline nose | Taylor is close, but makes this a reason for there being a form of the particular man, which the Greek leaves as a question.

@ch V.9.13 | Soul itself and the soul here
Is there also, as man-himself beside man, soul-itself beside soul and intellect-itself beside intellect? Not everything here is an image of an archetype: soul is not an image of soul-itself, but souls differ in worth, and soul-itself is here too, though perhaps not as here. Justice, self-control and true knowledge in our souls are not images, as in the perceptible, but the same things there in another way, for they are not bounded in a place. Wherever a soul comes up out of the body, they are there. So if we count soul and what is in it, all that is there is here.

@p
It remains to say whether only the things in the perceptible are there, or whether, as man-himself is other than man, so too soul-itself is other than soul there, and intellect-itself than intellect. We must say first that we must not think all the things here are images of archetypes, nor that soul is an image of soul-itself, but that one soul differs from another in worth, and that soul-itself is here too, though perhaps not as here. And since each soul really is, there is a certain justice too, and self-control, and in our souls true knowledge, not images or likenesses of those things, as in the perceptible, but those same things, being here in another way: for those are not marked off in some place: so that wherever a soul has come up out of a body, they are there too. For the perceptible world is in one place, the intelligible everywhere. What such a soul has here, then, is there: so that if by the things in the perceptible we take the things among visible things, not only what is in the perceptible is there, but more; but if the things in the world are said to include soul and what is in soul, all things are here that are there too.
!flag soul, so far as it is here, is not soul itself | Taylor turns the Greek around: it says that soul-itself is here too, though perhaps not as here.

@ch V.9.14 | The source, and the things without forms
This nature that holds all in the intelligible must be set down as a source; how many comes from the simple One must start from another beginning. As for things from rot, and harmful things, and dirt and mud: all that intellect has from the first is best, and these are not among them; soul, having things from intellect, takes others from matter, and these are among those. Chance compounds come from perceptible things meeting, not from forms; things from rot from a soul unable to make anything else. Arts of man's nature are in man-himself; before them, soul-itself, the life in intellect.

@p
This nature that holds all things in the intelligible, then, must be set down as a source. And how, when the source is really one and wholly simple, and there is multitude among the beings? How the multitude comes beside the one, and how all these are, and why intellect is these, and from where, must be told by those who start from another beginning. As for the things from rot, and the noxious things, whether there is a form of them there too, and of dirt and mud, we must say that all intellect brings from the first is best; these are not among them; nor is intellect made of them; but soul, from intellect, taking other things from matter, has these among them. About these more clearly when we come back to the puzzle of how multitude comes from one. That composite things, being by chance, are not from intellect, but are perceptible things come together of themselves, not in forms; and that things from rot are from a soul perhaps unable to do anything else; otherwise it would have made something natural: at any rate it makes where it can; and about the arts, that those referred to what is according to nature for man are contained in man-himself; and that before it there is another, universal one, and the soul-itself of the universal, or the life in intellect before soul came to be, so that it may also be called that soul-itself.
!flag to be considered as the first principle of things? | Taylor makes a question of the Greek's statement, that this nature must be set down as a source.
!flag and to things artificial | The Greek χαλεπῶν means troublesome or noxious things, not "artificial" ones; Taylor's footnote quoting Ficinus, which takes it so, is printed into his text later in the chapter.
!flag Ubi vero quaeritur nunquid ibi sit species | Taylor's Latin footnote from Ficinus is printed into the middle of his sentence on the arts in the scan.

@treatise VI.9 | On the good, or the One
Porphyry put this treatise last, and it is the summit of the Enneads. Everything is by being one: an army, a house, a body, health, beauty, the soul's virtue. But the One is not soul, nor intellect, nor being, all of which are many; it is the formless source of all, not any of them, known not by knowledge but by a presence better than knowledge. Plotinus tells how to approach it: let go of all shape, turn inward, become one, and wait. The soul's natural love is for the good; when it sees, seer and seen are no longer two. This is the life of gods and godlike men: "the flight of the alone to the alone".

@ch VI.9.1 | All things are by being one
Everything that is, is by being one: an army, a chorus, a flock, a house, a ship cease to be what they are when they lose their unity; bodies broken up lose their being. Health is the body ordered into one, beauty the parts held by the nature of the one, virtue the soul unified in one agreement. Is soul, then, which makes all things one, the One? No: as it gives bodies shape and form which are other than itself, so it gives the one as something other, looking to it. Soul is one, but many, with many powers; it brings unity and receives it from another.

@p
All beings are beings by the one, both those that are beings in the first sense and those that are said in any way to be among beings. For what would they be, if they were not one? Since, deprived of the one they are said to be, they are not those things. For there will not be an army if it is not one, nor a chorus nor a flock if they are not one. Nor will there be a house or a ship without the one; since the house is one and the ship is one, and if it lost this, the house would no longer be a house nor the ship a ship. Continuous magnitudes, then, would not be if the one were not present to them: at least when they are cut, in so far as they lose the one, they change their being. And the bodies of plants and animals, each being one, if they flee the one, broken up into multitude, have lost the being they had, no longer being what they were, but become other things, and these too in so far as each is one. And health is when the body is ordered into one, and beauty when the nature of the one holds the parts together; and the virtue of the soul is when it is unified into one, into one agreement. Then, since soul brings all things to one, crafting and moulding and shaping and ordering them, must we come to it and say that it supplies the one and that it is the one? Or, as in supplying other things to bodies it is not itself what it gives, such as shape and form, but these are other than it, so we must think that, if it gives the one too, it gives it as something other than itself, and makes each thing one by looking to the one, as it makes man by looking to man, taking with the man the one in him. For of the things called one, each is one in the way it has also what it is: so that the things that are less have the one less, and those that are more, more. And soul, being other than the one, has the one more, in proportion as it more really is, but is not the one itself: for soul is one, and the one is in a way an accident, and these are two, soul and one, as body and one. And what is separated, like a chorus, is furthest from the one, the continuous nearer. And soul shares in it still more. But if, because without being one soul would not be either, one brings soul and the one together into the same, first, the other things too are each with being one; but still the one is other than them: for body and one are not the same, but body shares in the one. Then, soul is many, even the single soul, even if it is not made of parts: for there are very many powers in it, reasoning, desiring, perceiving, which are held together by the one as by a bond. Soul brings the one to another, being one itself; but it too has this done to it by another.
!flag The original here is defective | Taylor's footnote on the Greek text (with Ficinus' reading) is printed into the middle of his chapter in the scan.

@ch VI.9.2 | Being and intellect are not the One
Is being, then, the same as the one, so that who found being found the one, and intellect is the one? No: the being of each thing is many, and the one cannot be many. Man is a rational living being with many parts bound by one; the whole of being, having all beings in it, is many, with life and intellect; the forms are number. The one is first; intellect, forms and being are not. Intellect thinks what is before it, turning to its source; thinking itself, it is two; it is with the good and with itself, knowing itself as all things. So the One is not all things, nor intellect, nor being.

@p
Is it, then, that for each of the things that are one in part, its being and the one are not the same, but for being as a whole, and its being, the being and the one are the same? So that one who has found being has found the one too, and being itself is the one itself: if, for instance, intellect is being, intellect is the one too, being first being and first one, and giving the others a share of being, so, and in the same measure, of the one. For what else would one say it is, beside being itself? Either it is the same as being: for "man" and "one man" are the same; or it is like a number of each thing, as if you said "two" of some things, so "one" of something alone. If, then, number belongs to the beings, plainly the one does too, and we must ask what it is. But if it is an activity of the soul going over things in counting, the one would be nothing in the things. But the argument said that, if each thing loses the one, it will not be at all. We must see, then, whether each one and being are the same, and being as a whole and the one. But if the being of each thing is a multitude, and the one cannot be a multitude, each would be other. At least man is a rational living being and many parts, and these many are bound together by one: man and one are other, then, if the one is divisible and the other undivided. And being as a whole, having all beings in itself, would still more be many and other than the one, having the one by taking part and sharing. And being has life and intellect: for it is not dead: being is many, then. And if this is intellect, in this way too it must be many. And still more if it contains the forms: for the idea is not one either, but rather a number, both each idea and the whole, and one in the way the world would be one. In general, the one is first, but intellect and the forms and being are not first. For each form is out of many and composite and later: for what each thing is made of is prior. That intellect cannot be the first will be plain from this too: intellect must be in thinking, and the best intellect, which does not look to what is outside, must think what is before it: for in turning to itself it turns to its source. And if it is itself thinker and thought, it will be double and not simple, and not the one: and if it looks to another, surely to what is better and before it. And if both to itself and to the better, in this way too it is second. And we must set down intellect as being such as to be present to the good and the first and to look at that, but to be with itself too and think itself, and think itself as being all things. It is far, then, from being the one, being varied. Nor will the one, then, be all things, for so it would no longer be one; nor intellect, for so it would be all things, since intellect is all things; nor being: for being is all things.
!flag since if it were, it would no longer be the one | Taylor's chapter ends here, leaving out the Greek's last lines: nor is the One intellect, for intellect is all things, nor being, for being is all things.

@ch VI.9.3 | The formless source
What, then, is the One? It is no wonder it is hard to say, since even being and form are hard, and our knowledge leans on forms. When the soul goes towards the formless it slips, fears it has nothing, and gladly comes down to the solid perceptible. To philosophize about the One, rise from the last things, be free of vice, go up to the source within, become intellect, and see the One with pure intellect, without shape or size, which belong to perception. The One is not a something, nor quality, quantity, intellect, soul, moving or at rest, in place or time: it is before all form. We only circle it from outside.

@p
What, then, would the one be, and what nature has it? It is no wonder that it is not easy to say, when even being is not easy to say, nor form; but we have a knowledge that leans on forms. And the more the soul goes towards the formless, utterly unable to grasp it because it is not bounded and, as it were, stamped by a varied stamp, it slips away and fears that it may have nothing. So it grows weary in such things and gladly comes down, often falling away from everything, until it comes to the perceptible, resting as it were on something solid: as sight too, wearied by small things, gladly falls on large ones. But when the soul wishes to see by itself, seeing only by being with it, and being one by being one with it, it does not think it yet has what it seeks, because it is not other than what is thought. Yet this is how one must act who means to philosophize about the one. Since, then, what we seek is one, and we are looking at the source of all things, the good and the first, we must neither go far from the things around the first, falling to the last of all things, but, pressing on to the first, lead ourselves back from the perceptible things, which are last, and be freed from all vice, as hastening to the good, and have gone up to the source in ourselves and become one out of many, if we are to be watchers of the source and the one. One must, then, become intellect, and entrust one's soul to intellect and set it under it, so that it may receive, awake, what that sees, and with this see the one, adding no perception and receiving nothing from it into that, but with pure intellect seeing the purest, and with the first of intellect. When, then, one who has set out to see such a thing imagines size or shape or bulk about this nature, intellect is not his guide to the sight, because intellect is not of a nature to see such things, but the activity is of perception and of opinion following perception. We must take from intellect the promise of what it can do. Intellect can see either what is before it, or what is its own, or what comes from it. What is in it is pure and simple, and what is before it still purer and simpler, or rather what is before it. Not intellect, then, but before intellect: for intellect is something among the beings; but that is not something, but before each thing; nor is it being: for being has, as it were, the shape of being, but that is shapeless, even of intelligible shape. For the nature of the one, being generative of all things, is none of them. It is not, then, something, nor quality, nor quantity, nor intellect, nor soul; not moving, nor again at rest, not in place, not in time, but by itself of one form, or rather formless, being before all form, before movement, before rest: for these are about being, and make it many. Why, then, if it is not moving, is it not at rest? Because about being one or both of these must be, and what is at rest is at rest by rest and is not the same as rest: so that it will be an accident to it, and it will no longer stay simple. For to call it the cause is not to predicate something accidental to it, but to us, because we have something from it while it is in itself. One who speaks exactly must not say "that" or "this" of it, but we, running round it as it were from outside, wish to explain our own experiences, sometimes near, sometimes falling away in our puzzles about it.
!flag for being has, as it were, the form of the one | The Greek says that being has, as it were, the shape "of being"; Taylor writes "of the one", which the argument rules out.
!flag the ant in the mean time | "ant" is a scan error for "it".

@ch VI.9.4 | Presence better than knowledge
The difficulty is that the One is grasped not by knowledge or thought but by a presence better than knowledge; knowledge is reasoning, and reasoning is many, so the soul falls from being one. We must run above knowledge and leave everything. So Plato calls it unspeakable and unwritable: we speak and write only to point the way; the seeing is the work of the one who wants to see. Whoever has not seen, or is still weighed down, or lacks the guiding argument, should blame himself and try to be alone. The One is absent from nothing, but present only to those ready to receive it.

@p
The difficulty arises most of all because the understanding of that one is not by knowledge, nor by thinking, as with the other intelligibles, but by a presence better than knowledge. The soul suffers a departure from being one, and is not wholly one, when it takes knowledge of anything: for knowledge is reasoning, and reasoning is many. It passes the one by, then, falling into number and multitude. We must, then, run above knowledge, and in no way go out from being one, but we must stand away from knowledge and its objects, and from everything of that, and from what comes from that, as all daylight comes from the sun. That is why he says it is neither to be spoken nor written. But we speak and write, sending towards it and waking from the arguments to the sight, as if showing the way to one who wishes to see something. For the teaching goes as far as the road and the journey, but the seeing is already the work of the one who has wished to see. But if someone has not come to the sight, and the soul has had no understanding of the splendour there, nor felt nor had in itself something like a lover's passion from seeing, the lover resting in what he loves, having taken true light and lit up all the soul by having come nearer; if he has gone up still weighed down behind by what was in the way of the sight, and has gone up not alone but having what divides him from it, or not yet gathered into one (for that one is absent from none, and from all, so that it is present and not present, except to those able and prepared to receive it, so as to fit it and as it were touch and handle it by likeness and by the power in them akin to what comes from it, when it is as it was when it came from it, and is then able to see as that one is of a nature to be seen); if, then, he is not yet there, but is outside because of these things, or for lack of the argument that guides him and gives him belief about it, let him blame himself for the first, and try to stand away from everything and be alone, and for what he does not believe in the arguments, falling short, let him think in this way.
!note The Greek of "from everything of that, and from what comes from that" seems damaged; the fuller text that Taylor translated has "from everything else, and from every beautiful sight; for everything beautiful is later than that and comes from it".
!flag the perception of the highest God | The Greek says only "the understanding of that one"; "the highest God" is Taylor's.

@ch VI.9.5 | The one that is not a point
Those who think things run by chance and bodily causes are far from God and from the thought of the One. The argument is for those who go up to soul, see that it has virtue from intellect, and then see intellect, the intelligible world, all things together and yet distinct. This is not first, for it is not simple. Before it is the wonder of the One, which is not being, the source of the best things, a power that stays in itself and is not lessened. We call it one only to signify it and to make our soul one, not as a point or a unit, which are only likenesses of it in simplicity.

@p
Whoever thinks that the beings are managed by chance and the spontaneous, and held together by bodily causes, has been driven far from God and from the thought of the one; and the argument is not for them, but for those who set down another nature beside bodies and go up to soul. These must have understood the nature of soul, both in other ways and that it is from intellect and, sharing in reason from it, has virtue; and after this they must take intellect, other than what reasons and is called the reasoning part, and the reasonings as already in separation and movement, and the kinds of knowledge in the soul, such forming principles, as already come into the open because intellect, the cause of the knowledge, has come to be in the soul; and having seen intellect as if it were perceptible, because it can be grasped, standing above the soul and being its father, an intelligible world, we must call it a quiet intellect and an untroubled movement, having all things in itself and being all things, a multitude undivided and again divided: for it is not divided as the forming principles already thought one by one, nor are the things in it confused: for each goes forth separately: as in the kinds of knowledge too, all being in what has no parts, each of them is still separate. This multitude together, then, the intelligible world, is next to the first, and the argument says it must exist, if indeed soul does; and it is more sovereign than soul; but not first, because it is not one or simple; and the one, the source of all things, is simple.

@p
@greek τὸ δὴ πρὸ τοῦ ἐν τοῖς οὖσι τιμιωτάτου
What is before the most honoured among the beings, if there must be something before intellect, which wishes to be one but is not one, but one-like, because intellect is not scattered from itself but is really with itself, not dividing itself from what is near it after the one, having dared somehow to stand away from the one: what is before this, the wonder of the one, which is not being, so that here too "one" may not be said of another; to which in truth no name is fitting, but if we must name it, it would fittingly be called, in common speech, one, not as being something else and then one; hard to know because of this, but known rather by what is born from it, being: for it leads intellect to being, and its nature is such as to be the spring of the best things and a power generating the beings, staying in itself and not lessened, nor in the things that come to be from it, because it is before them too; which we name one of necessity, to signify it to each other by the name, leading to an undivided conception and wishing to make the soul one, not saying "one" and "without parts" in the way we say it of a point or a unit: for what is one so is a source of quantity, which would not exist unless being came before, and what is before being: we must not throw our thought there, then, but must liken these to those by analogy, in simplicity and in fleeing multitude and division.
!flag It is necessary therefore to project the dianoetic power to these | The Greek says the opposite: we must "not" throw our thought onto the point and the unit, but only liken them to the One.
!flag their dying from multitude | "dying" is a scan error for "flying".

@ch VI.9.6 | Without need, beyond thinking
How is it one? Not as a point, smallest, but greatest in power, unbounded not in size or number but in power beyond grasping. Think of it as intellect or God and it is more; it is one as most self-sufficient, for what is many lacks what it is made of. It needs nothing: not to be, nor to be well, nor a place. So nothing is good for it, it wills nothing; it is beyond good, good for others. It has no thinking, for that brings otherness; this is not ignorance, since ignorance needs another to be ignorant of. It is not the good it gives, but good in another way.

@p
How, then, do we say one, and how must we fit it to our thinking? Setting it down as one in more ways than a unit and a point are made one: for here the soul, taking away magnitude and the multitude of number, ends at the smallest and leans on something without parts, but which was in what has parts and is in another; but that one is neither in another nor in what has parts, nor without parts as the smallest is: for it is the greatest of all things, not in magnitude but in power, so that it is without magnitude in power too: for the things after it too are undivided and without parts in their powers, not in bulk. And we must take it as unbounded, not because its magnitude or number cannot be gone through, but because its power cannot be grasped. For when you think of it as intellect or God, it is more; and again when you make it one in your thought, here too it is more than you imagine, even if you imagine it as more unified than your thinking: for it is by itself, nothing being accidental to it. One might think of its oneness by its self-sufficiency too. For it must be the most sufficient of all and most self-sufficient and most without need: and everything many and not one is needy, made of many. Its being needs to be one, then: but it does not need itself: for it is itself. And being many, a thing needs as many things as it is, and each of the things in it, being with the others and not by itself, lacking the others, shows itself, both singly and as a whole, needy. If, then, there must be something most self-sufficient, it must be the one, being alone such as to be needy neither towards itself nor towards another. For it seeks nothing in order to be, nor to be well, nor to be settled there: for, being cause to the others, it does not have what it is from others; and what would its well-being be outside it? So the well is not an accident to it: for it is it. And there is no place for it: for it needs no settling, as if unable to carry itself: and what is to be settled is soulless, and a mass falling, if it is not yet settled. The others are settled because of this, through which they came into existence at the same time and got the place in which they were set. And what seeks a place is needy; but a source is not needy of what comes after it: and the source of all is in need of none of them: for whatever is needy is needy as longing for its source. And if the one were needy, it would plainly seek not to be one: so that it would be in need of what would destroy it. And everything said to be in need of the well is in need of what keeps it: so that nothing is good for the one. Nor, then, is there will of anything; but it is beyond good, and itself not good for itself, but good for the others, if any can share in it: nor thinking, so that there may be no otherness: nor movement: for it is before movement and before thinking: for what will it think? Itself. Before thinking, then, it will be ignorant, and will need thinking in order to know itself, the self-sufficient for itself. It is not, then, because it neither knows nor thinks itself that there will be ignorance about it: for ignorance arises when there is another, when one is ignorant of the other: but what is alone neither knows nor has what it is ignorant of, but, being one and with itself, does not need the thinking of itself. Since we must not even attach being with itself, if you are to keep the one, but take away both thinking and being with itself, and the thinking of itself and of the others: for we must not rank it with the thinker, but rather with thinking. And thinking does not think, but is the cause of thinking for another: and the cause is not the same as what is caused. The cause of all things, then, is none of them. Nor, then, must it be called good with the good it gives, but in another way the good beyond the other goods.
!flag But not deriving that which it is from others, nor its happiness | Taylor's text, as the scan gives it, has lost the middle of the argument here, running from "the essence of it requires to be one" into a later sentence, without the steps on needing nothing in order to be.

@ch VI.9.7 | Withdraw within
If this leaves you without bounds, stand on what has been said and look from it, but not by throwing your thought outside: the One is not somewhere, leaving the rest empty, but present to whoever can touch it. As matter must be without quality to take all imprints, the soul must become formless, turned wholly inward, not knowing all things, nor even itself, and be with it, then come back and tell, if it can, of that meeting, as Minos did, making laws as images of it; or stay above, if it wishes. God is outside no one, but people flee outside themselves.

@p
But if, because it is none of these, you are left without bounds in your judgment, stand yourself in these things and look from them: and look, without throwing your thought outside. For it does not lie somewhere, leaving the others empty of it, but it is there present to one who can touch it, and to one who cannot it is not present. And as with the others one cannot think something while thinking something else and being on something else, but must attach nothing to what is thought, so that it may be the very thing thought, so here too we must understand that one cannot think that one having the imprint of another in the soul, while the imprint is active; nor again can the soul, taken up and held by other things, be stamped with the imprint of what is opposite; but as it is said of matter that it must be without qualities of all things if it is to receive the imprints of all, so too, and much more, the soul must become formless, if there is to be nothing in the way settled in it, against its filling and lighting up by the first nature. If so, one must let go of all outside and turn wholly to what is within, not inclined to anything outside, but not knowing all things, first in disposition, then in forms too, and not knowing even oneself, come to be in the sight of that one, and having been with that one, and having had, as it were, enough converse, come and announce, if one can, to another too that meeting there: such perhaps as Minos too had when he was famed as the familiar companion of Zeus, remembering which he set down laws as images of it, filled for the laying down of laws by the touch of the divine; or else, thinking civic matters unworthy of himself, stay above, if he wishes: which might happen to one who has seen much. God, he says, is outside no one, but is with all without their knowing. For they themselves flee outside him, or rather outside themselves. They cannot, then, take hold of what they have fled from, nor, having lost themselves, seek another; nor will a child that has gone out of itself in madness know its father: but one who has learned himself will know from where he comes too.
!flag fix yourself in God | The Greek says only "look from these", with no word for God.
!flag will consider political concerns as unworthy to be the subject of conference with deity | The Greek says the man may think civic matters unworthy of himself and stay above; "conference with deity" is Taylor's.
!flag If, therefore, a certain soul has known itself at another time | Taylor's heading for chapter VIII is garbled in the scan ("VXIL"), so his chapter 8 runs on here, as part of chapter 7.

@ch VI.9.8 | The dance round the centre
A soul that knows itself knows its natural movement is not straight but round a centre, its source, and it hangs from that; the gods' souls always move towards it and are gods by this, while one who stands far off is the many-sided man or a beast. Part of us is held by the body, like a man with his feet in water, but with the rest we join our centre to the centre of all. Bodiless things are separated not by place but by otherness; the One has no otherness and is always present, we when we have none. Like a chorus round its leader, we always circle it, but sing well only when we turn to it.

@p
If, then, a soul knows itself the rest of the time, and knows that its movement is not straight, except when it takes a break, but its movement according to nature is like that in a circle round something, not outside, but round a centre, and the centre is that from which the circle is, it will move round this from which it is, and will hang from this, bringing itself to the same thing to which all souls should, but only the souls of gods always move: and moving to it they are gods: for what is joined to that is a god, and what stands far away is the man of the many, and a beast. Is this, then, like the centre of the soul, what we are seeking? Or must we think it something else, in which all the centres, as it were, meet, and that it is by analogy with the centre of this circle? For the soul is not a circle as the figure is, but because the ancient nature is in it and round it, and because it is from such a thing, and still more because souls are wholly separate. But now, since part of us is held down by the body (as if someone had his feet in water and the rest of his body above it), raising ourselves by the part not plunged in the body, by this we join, by our own centre, to what is like the centre of all things, as the centres of the greatest circles join to the centre of the sphere that contains them, and we rest. If, then, they were bodily circles, not soul circles, they would join the centre in place, and would be round it lying somewhere; but since the souls are intelligible, and that is above intellect, we must think the joining comes about by other powers than those by which what thinks naturally joins what is thought, and more than that, as what thinks is present by likeness and sameness and joins its kin with nothing to divide them. For bodies are prevented from sharing with other bodies, but bodiless things are not divided by bodies: they are not apart from each other in place, then, but by otherness and difference: when, then, otherness is not present, things not other are present to each other. That one, then, having no otherness, is always present, and we when we have none: and that one does not long for us, so as to be round us, but we for it, so as to be round it. And we are always round it, but we do not always look to it; but as a chorus singing, though it is round its leader, may turn to look outside, but when it turns back sings well and is really round him: so too we are always round it, and if we were not, there would be complete dissolution for us and we should no longer be; but we do not always look to it, but when we look to it, then there is the end for us, and rest, and no singing out of tune, as we really dance round it a god-inspired dance.
!flag even when we are perfectly loosened from it | The Greek says that, if we were not round it, we should be wholly dissolved and no longer exist; Taylor makes it a condition in which we still circle it.

@ch VI.9.9 | The soul's love for its father
In this dance the soul sees the spring of life and of intellect, the source of being, the cause of good, the root of soul, which give without being lessened. We are not cut off from it; we breathe and are kept by it, and are more when we turn to it. There the soul rests, free of evils, thinks, is unaffected, truly lives, and gives birth to beauty, justice and virtue. Its inborn love shows the good is there: the soul is Aphrodite, heavenly there and common here, like a girl who loves her noble father, deceived by mortal suitors, then purified and home again. Whoever has seen knows: we must cut away the rest and become light.

@p
In this dance it sees the spring of life, the spring of intellect, the source of being, the cause of good, the root of soul: not that these are poured out from it and then lessen it: for it is not a mass: or what is generated would be perishable. But as it is they are everlasting, because their source stays the same, not divided into them but staying whole. So they stay too: as when the sun stays the light stays too. For we are not cut off, nor apart, even if the nature of body has come in and drawn us to itself, but we breathe and are kept, not by its giving and then going away, but by its always supplying, as long as it is what it is. We are more, though, when we have turned to it, and our well-being is there, while being far is only being less. There the soul rests too, outside evils, running up to the place clean of evils; and it thinks there, and is unaffected there; and true living is there: for living now, and without God, is a trace of life imitating that one, while living there is the activity of intellect; and the activity generates gods too, in quiet touch with that one, generates beauty, generates justice, generates virtue: for the soul filled with God conceives these, and this is its beginning and end: beginning, because it is from there, end, because the good is there, and coming to be there it becomes itself what it was: for the life here, among these things, is a falling away and an exile and a shedding of wings. That the good is there the soul's inborn love shows too, by which Eros is paired with souls in paintings and in stories. For since the soul is other than God but from him, it loves him of necessity: and being there it has the heavenly love, but here it becomes common. For there is a heavenly Aphrodite there, and here she becomes the common one, like a harlot: and every soul is Aphrodite. And the birth of Aphrodite, and Eros born with her, hint at this. The soul, then, in its natural state, loves God, wishing to be united with him, as a girl has a noble love for a noble father; but when, coming into birth, she is deceived, as it were, by wooings, exchanging him for another, mortal love, she is outraged by the absence of her father: and hating again the outrages here, and purifying herself of the things here, setting out for her father once more, she is happy. And those to whom this experience is unknown, let them think of it from the loves here, what it is to get what one most loves, and that these loved things are mortal and harmful, loves of images, and they change, because this was not the truly loved, nor our good, nor what we seek. But there is the true beloved, with which one can be, sharing in it and really having it, not embracing it from outside in flesh. Whoever has seen knows what I mean: how the soul has another life then, coming to it and having already come and shared in it, so that, so disposed, it knows that the giver of true life is present, and needs nothing more: on the contrary, it must put away the rest, and stand in this alone, and become this alone, cutting away all else we wear: so that we hasten to go out from here, and are vexed at being bound to the other things, so that we may embrace it with the whole of ourselves and have no part with which we do not touch God. There one can see both that one and oneself, as it is right to see: oneself made splendid, full of intelligible light, or rather pure light itself, weightless, light, having become a god, or rather being one, set alight then, but, if weighed down again, as it were withering.
!flag He however who knows this | The Greek says "whoever has seen" knows what I mean; Taylor has "knows this".
!flag governs all the co-ordinations in the celestial world | Taylor's long footnote (from Proclus, on the two Aphrodites) is printed into the middle of his sentence in the scan, which goes on, after it, "terrene wantonness and injustice".

@ch VI.9.10 | Seer and seen are one
Why does the soul not stay there? Because it has not yet wholly come out of the body. The part that saw is not the part disturbed; when it rests from the sight, knowledge by proof and reasoning goes on. Seeing there is not reasoning but greater than it. Perhaps we should not even say "see": the seer then does not distinguish two, but has become another, not himself, one with that, as centre joined to centre. That is why the sight is hard to tell: how can one report as other what one did not see as other, but as one with oneself?

@p
How, then, does it not stay there? Because it has not yet wholly come out. But there will be a time when the sight will be continuous too, for one no longer troubled by any trouble of the body. What has seen is not what is troubled, but the other, when what has seen is idle from the sight, though not idle from the knowledge in proofs and beliefs and in the soul's reasoning: but the seeing and what has seen is no longer reason, but greater than reason and before reason and over reason, as is what is seen. Seeing himself then, when he sees, he will see himself as such, or rather will be with himself as such, and perceive himself as such, having become simple. But perhaps we should not even say "will see". And what is seen (if we must speak of these as two, the seer and the seen, and not both as one: a bold word), then the seer neither sees nor distinguishes nor imagines two, but, having become as it were another, and not himself, nor belonging to himself there, he has become that one's and is one, as if joining centre to centre: for here too, when they meet, they are one, and two when they are apart. So we too now speak of it as other. That is why the sight is hard to put into words. For how could one report it as other, when one did not see it, at the time one beheld it, as other, but as one with oneself?
!flag as Proclus well observes | Taylor's footnotes (on Proclus and on what Plotinus means by calling a man a god) are printed into the middle of his sentence in the scan.
!flag but that he is the thing seen | The Greek says only that perhaps we should not even say "he will see"; "he is the thing seen" is Taylor's.

@ch VI.9.11 | The flight of the alone to the alone
This is what the rule of the mysteries means, not to tell the uninitiated. The seer was one with what he saw, not seeing but united, with no anger or desire, no reason or thought, not even himself, but rapt in stillness, past beauty and the chorus of the virtues, like one who enters the inner shrine and leaves the statues behind. It was not a sight but another way of seeing: standing out, simplifying, giving oneself, longing for touch, rest. Falling from it, he rises again through virtue to intellect, and through wisdom to that. This is the life of gods and of godlike, happy men: the flight of the alone to the alone.

@p
This is what the command of these mysteries wishes to show, not to divulge to the uninitiated: since that is not to be divulged, it forbids showing the divine to another, to whom it has not been granted to see it himself. Since, then, there were not two, but the seer himself was one with what was seen, as not seen but united, if he could remember what he became when he was mingled with that one, he would have an image of it with himself. He was one himself, having no difference in himself, either towards himself or in other ways: for nothing moved in him, no anger, no desire for anything else was present in him when he had gone up, nor any reason or thinking, nor himself at all, if we must say this too: but as if snatched away or possessed, he has come quietly into a solitary state, unmoving in his own being, turning aside nowhere, not even turning round himself, standing wholly still and become as it were stillness; not among the beautiful things, but running even beyond beauty, gone beyond even the chorus of the virtues, like one who goes into the inner shrine and leaves behind the statues in the temple, which become first again for one who comes out of the shrine, after the sight within and the meeting there, not with a statue or an image, but with itself: and these become second sights. But that was perhaps not a sight, but another way of seeing, a standing outside and a simplifying and a giving of oneself, and a longing for touch, and rest, and a thinking round towards fitting together, if one is to behold what is in the shrine. If one looks in another way, nothing is present to him. These, then, are imitations, and they hint to the wise among the prophets how that god is seen: and a wise priest, understanding the riddle, might make a true sight of the shrine, having come there. And even without having come there, thinking this shrine something invisible, and a spring and source, he will know that he sees source by source, and is with it, like with like, leaving out none of the divine things the soul can have. And before the sight he asks for what remains of the sight: and what remains, for one who has gone beyond all things, is what is before all things. For the nature of the soul will not come to what wholly is not, but going down it will come to evil, and so to non-being, but not to utter non-being: running the opposite way, it will come not to another but to itself, and so, not being in another, it is not in nothing, but in itself: and to be in itself alone and not in being is to be in that one: for one becomes oneself not being, but beyond being, in this way, by which one is with it. If, then, someone sees himself become this, he has himself as a likeness of that one, and if he passes on from himself as image to archetype, he would have the end of his journey. And falling from the sight, waking again the virtue in himself, and seeing himself wholly ordered, he will be lightened again, through virtue going to intellect, and through wisdom to that one. And this is the life of gods and of godlike and happy men, a release from the other things here, a life that takes no pleasure in the things here, a flight of the alone to the alone.
!flag according to a perfection which derives its completion from all sensible natures | Taylor's long footnote (from Proclus, on the solitary life of the world) is printed into his text in the scan, straight after the treatise's last words.
